Katalog Berita (Halaman 57/58)

#202. Anthropic rival says companies can (mostly) skip the pricey models

On the same day that Anthropic went public in the largest IPO on record, with investor enthusiasm for the AI lab through the roof, far away at the Slush startup conference in Helsinki, the topic du jour was open source everything.

That’s the enormous opportunity being chased by companies like Fireworks AI, as enterprises seek ways to avoid paying frontier prices for their AI needs.

Fireworks runs AI models for other businesses. It specializes in open-weight models, which anyone can download, and helps customers tune them on their own data so they run faster and cheaper than the models of Anthropic, OpenAI, and other heavyweights, and are harder for rivals to copy. Customers have included Uber, Shopify, and Notion. Co-founder Chenyu Zhao and I spoke onstage toward the end of the day.

Zhao and many others believe companies are gravitating toward platforms like his because frontier models are pricey and, in most cases, he says, unnecessary unless you need the very latest and greatest generalized intelligence. There are also growing concerns that frontier labs absorb too much of their customers’ know-how, even if only through the prompts used to get answers, a worry Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella raised in July.

Fireworks has also gotten its first black eye, having disclosed two security incidents this fall. In one, hackers reached API keys and tokens stored as secrets for a limited number of accounts; in the other, attackers breached its cloud provider, made their way into a development system, and nabbed customer data, including billing records and some datasets.

Notion says AI chat transcripts from fewer than 1% of its workspaces were exposed, but it has disconnected from Fireworks. Uber says hackers stole the names, addresses, birth dates and driver’s license numbers of about 150,000 drivers through a vendor that TechCrunch identified as Fireworks. Fireworks will have to win back the confidence of customers like them.

Fireworks, assigned a $17.5 billion valuation by its investors in summer, is reportedly in “early talks” for yet another round, though Zhao declined to comment.

There’s money chasing everyone in the category, seems like. Rival companies Together AI and Baseten have announced massive rounds in recent months. On Wednesday, Palo Alto Networks said it will buy Applied Compute, which, like Fireworks, helps companies train and run custom AI models on their own data. The deal for the startup, founded just a year ago by former OpenAI researchers, is $4.5 billion, all of it in stock.

Here’s some of what Zhao had to say. This conversation has been edited for length and clarity.

You spent a decade at Google, including managing a 50-person engineering team on Google Cloud Vertex AI. Why leave when you did?

When you meet great co-founders, it’s obvious within five minutes that they’re really smart. I was sold after talking to Lin [Qiao] and Dima [Dzhulgakov]. It’s also important to find people who are genuinely nice, people you’ll enjoy working with.

How do you describe Fireworks to a CEO who has never heard of it?

I describe Fireworks as a specialized intelligence platform. OpenAI and Anthropic are building general intelligence. We use open models, which are often smaller, cheaper, and faster, and let companies add their own data to specialize them. That way you own your AI rather than being beholden to giant AI conglomerates. If you’re just using Opus or Sonnet, that’s not a differentiator.

How far behind are open models right now?

A few years ago I’d have said a year, a year and a half. Then came DeepSeek and a waterfall of fantastic open models. These days it’s at most six months behind, probably closer to three.

If the models are so close behind, why are businesses slow to switch?

Frontier models are a household brand, and before you find product-market fit, using the best model is low risk. But once you pass 10,000 or 100,000 users and you’re spending $10,000 to $100,000 a month on tokens, it’s time to think about open source.

In July, Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella warned that companies using proprietary AI models pay twice, once in money and again in know-how. Do you think that warning had a material effect?

We think we’ll live in a two-model world, with frontier and open models in parallel. You want the best intelligence for high-level planning, but you don’t need a frontier model for basic arithmetic. That’s why there’s so much interest in routing, and why people now think about cost per task, not just cost per token.

Anthropic went public today. What do you think of its valuation? There’s one scenario in which it just continues to cement its place in this sort of oligopoly; there’s another where it starts to lose traction.

On the record: I’ll probably call my broker tonight and buy a few shares. This is a long-term bet. In 10 years AI usage will be at a completely different scale, and that takes an incredible amount of capital, which is why these companies are going public.

Fireworks has had a couple of security breaches recently. What do you tell customers?

Every company is probably seeing an unprecedented frequency of attacks. For our incident, a lot of the motivation was LLM jacking: hunting through providers for API keys and secrets to sell tokens on illicit marketplaces. Our promise is zero data retention for inference traffic, and there’s no indication that has been compromised.

Notion has disconnected from Fireworks. Did you lose other customers?

I don’t think I can publicly name the specific customers that were impacted. Some, such as Notion and Cursor, have gone public already. It was a small subset of our customers that had this data impact.

How did that customer data get exposed, given your zero-retention promise?

Notion was using batch prediction, where you upload a dataset that’s stored at rest, so it’s not a zero-retention product by nature. We’re moving those services to an online flow so we keep data only as long as we need it.

Jensen Huang left Nvidia last week, and Nvidia has backed Fireworks since 2024. Does that affect you?

It’s been a blow to the industry, and we feel for Jensen and his family. But Nvidia has always been a strong supporter of open source, and I don’t see that changing. We expect it to keep investing in Nemotron, and we’re excited to keep partnering with them.

#201. Price tag for US war in Iran barrels past $48 billion

A Pentagon budget officer has said the price of the Iran war surpassed $48.3 billion last week. That does not include costs sustained in Iranian attacks on US bases — lawmakers want details the Pentagon is loath to give.

The cost of the US war in Iran, which Donald Trump launched without Congressional approval on February 28, blew past $48.3 billion (€44 billion) on November 13, according to Pentagon budgetary official Arthur Miller.

Miller laid out the figures during Thursday testimony before the US Senate Armed Services Committee as his nomination to become Navy comptroller is considered.

Miller said the costs cover US military operations, equipment and munitions but not damage sustained at US military installations across the Middle East.

According to news agency AP, damage to hundreds of aircraft hangars, barracks, radar systems, warehouses and other US military infrastructure has run up into the billions of dollars, however, Miller failed to provide an estimate of the total cost when pressed during the hearing.

Committee Chairman Roger Wicker, a Republican from Mississippi, called on Miller to provide the figures as soon as possible.

In October, the Wall Street Journal cited Pentagon sources who said damage to US facilities in the region could range from $5 billion to as much as $15 billion.

The Washington-based think tank Center for Strategic and International Studies put the cost for repairing buildings alone — excluding equipment and systems inside — at more than $4 billion.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The Trump administration has variously argued that the war is not a war, that it has been won, and that it will be over soon. There is currently no end in sight and on top of the US military's costs, the global economy is getting pummeled every day as a result of the ongoing closure of the Strait of Hormuz — which is the direct result of US attacks on the Islamic Republic.

The Trump administration is calling on Congress to pass the Pentagon's $1.5 trillion 2027 defense budget — a 40% increase over 2026 — in order to fund America's military. Unlikely prior to the November 3 midterm elections, the prospects of that figure being approved now that Democrats have taken control of both houses of Congress from Republicans would seem nigh impossible.

#200. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

Two women arrested for not wearing hijab following ‘yogurt attack’

Two women were arrested in Iran for failing to wear the hijab in public, after a man threw a tub of yogurt at them at a store in the city of Shandiz on Thursday, according to Mizan News Agency, the state-run outlet for Iran’s judiciary.

Oil prices surge after OPEC+ producers announce surprise cuts

Oil prices spiked Monday after OPEC+ producers unexpectedly announced that they would cut output. Brent crude, the global benchmark, jumped 5.31% to $84.13 a barrel, while WTI, the US benchmark, rose 5.48% to $79.83. Both were the sharpest price rises in almost a year. The collective output cut by the nine members of OPEC+ totals 1.66 million barrels per day.

Iran blames Israel for the killing of second IRGC officer, vows to respond

A second Iranian Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) officer died following an attack in Syria on Friday, according to Iranian state media on Sunday. Iranian state media said the Iranian military adviser died after an Israeli attack near the Syrian capital Damascus left him wounded. The attack also killed another IRGC officer. In a tweet on Sunday, Iranian government spokesman Ali Bahadori Jahromi said the alleged Israeli attack wouldn’t go unanswered. Iranian Foreign Ministry spokesman Nasser Kanaani said on Sunday that Iran has the right to respond to “state terrorism.”

What’s the difference between Persians and Arabs?Comedian @MazJobrani shares with me his thoughts on touring the Middle East, his support for Iran’s protests, and his latest comedy special: pic.twitter.com/UvYwVgJIlw

Iranian-American comedian Maz Jobrani, who has been touring the Middle East, spoke to CNN’s Becky Anderson about his support for the protests in his homeland, saying that he used his standup comedy platform to highlight the “brutality against the Iranian people.”

“It was an opportunity for me to say, ‘let’s keep fighting,’” he said.

China and Brazil have reached an agreement to ditch USD and use their own currencies—China Daily pic.twitter.com/q33U4kVO5Q

An Iranian state news outlet is gloating at what it sees as the demise of the US dollar.

IRNA recreated a popular meme to mark China and Brazil’s decision to reportedly ditch the US dollar as an intermediary in trade, citing the Chinese state news outlet, China Daily. It shows two men representing China and Brazil posing in front of a grave labelled “USD.”

The meme was pinned to the top of IRNA’s Twitter page, and was met with laughter and ridicule. “Dream on,” said another user, pointing to the dollar’s use as the main reserve currency around the world.

China Daily said that the agreement was part of “the rising global use of the Chinese renminbi.” It would reportedly enable China and Brazil to conduct trade and financial transactions using local currencies instead of the dollar.

#199. 350 years ago, Ole Rømer showed that light has a finite speed

Where Readers Share Great Links. Join Free

Ole Rømer used Io’s eclipses to show that light does not travel instantaneously. In 1676, he predicted a delayed appearance of Jupiter’s moon Io and was right, providing the first solid evidence that light takes time to travel.

#198. Anthropic’s 7 founders cashed out nearly $2B in record-breaking IPO

Anthropic became a public company on Thursday, raising a total of $81.3 billion, including $1.95 billion for its seven founders. As we previously reported, each of them ponied up about 3.4 million shares apiece to sell in the IPO.

At the upgraded IPO price of $83 per share, that means they each pocketed about $280 million before underwriting fees and taxes. It looked like they had calculated the number of shares they planned to sell to give them each around $250 million if shares hit $74. That was the midpoint price from Anthropic’s original price range. So that’s a cool $30-ish million more for each of them than perhaps they were gunning for.

Anthropic implied in its IPO filings that the founders aren’t entirely in this for the money though. The company said that each of the founders has pledged to dedicate 80% of their personal equity in the company to philanthropy.

However, if any of them struggle to live on their quarter of a billion, they can scale back their charitable commitments. The company points out that these commitments are “voluntary, revocable expressions of each co-founder’s philanthropic intent and do not constitute legally binding obligations.”

With the IPO in the record books, there were a few other interesting numbers worth highlighting.

$104.8 billion. That’s really the amount Anthropic raised for its coffers (with a possibility of $117 billion, if the bankers buy all of their allotment of shares). In addition to selling its Class A common stock, it also sold a previously announced $26 billion worth of a special non-voting Class N stock to some of the biggest tech names in the industry.

Nvidia bought $10 billion of these Class N shares, Google bought $10 billion, and AMD, Broadcom, Micron, Microsoft, and TSMC divvied up the rest.

Just for comparison: When Facebook had its then record-breaking IPO in 2012, it raised $16 billion on IPO day.

28% stake to Amazon and Google combined. All told, at the higher IPO share price, Google wound up owning 13.4% of the company. Amazon also now has a big stake in Anthropic because when it previously invested in the AI lab as a startup, it was granted promissory notes that could be converted to equity. It converted a small percentage of them as part of the IPO, leaving it with a 14.6% stake. Had it converted all of them, Amazon could have owned nearly 24%.

The two large cloud providers now own 28% of Anthropic, the AI lab says.

More than $1 trillion. As mind-blowing as it is to raise nearly $105 billion, giving Anthropic nearly $230 billion of working capital available (along with capital it already had on hand), the company’s need for future capital is still far greater.

Anthropic needs about $70.7 billion just to cover its compute and infrastructure spending commitments in the next 12 months as of the quarter ended September 30, 2026. It needs $451.6 billion to cover these commitments before the end of 2030. And it has racked up hundreds of billions of other commitments that are either conditional (and may not happen) or haven’t been priced yet.

All told, the company has disclosed a ballpark of $1.05 trillion to $1.08 trillion in infrastructure commitments in its foreseeable future. This was a huge IPO. But it is a pricey business to run.

#195. Apple may be developing its own AI agent that runs your Mac

Apple appears to be working on its own AI agent to conduct computer work on behalf of users — including using apps, accessing websites, and coding, among other things.

The news was originally reported by Bloomberg, which spotted a job listing the iPhone maker recently posted for a senior engineering manager to oversee the creation of an “AI agent that can use a computer.”

“Imagine an AI agent that can use a computer the way a capable person does,” the posting says. “It browses the web, works across apps, runs commands, writes and debugs code, and calls services, and it chooses the right tool for each step. It does all this privately and with the polish people expect from Apple.”

The posting additionally notes that the agents should be able to move “fluidly between tools” and “carry out multi-step tasks across apps, browsers, command lines, code, and APIs.”

TechCrunch reached out to Apple for more information but did not receive a response.

Agents capable of computer work have become one of the hottest commodities in the tech industry over the past year. Major labs like Anthropic and OpenAI have launched such services, while other major tech brands — like Meta, which recently launched its Muse agent line — have also moved into the space. It makes sense that Apple — which is trying to expand its AI offerings in the new John Ternus era — would want to follow suit.

Bloomberg additionally notes that, at the same time that Apple is working on its own digital assistant, the company is making it significantly harder for third-party agents to gain total control over its computers.

The outlet notes that the version of its upcoming macOS 27.3 will deploy warnings discouraging users from granting full access to third-party agents. In October, Apple issued a notification to developers stating that the company planned to “introduce additional controls to ensure that users who genuinely wish to grant an app this extraordinary level of access can only do so with very explicit user action.”

Bloomberg writes that while the iPhone maker “has eyed introducing the service as soon as the end of next year,” progress on its in-house agent “remains in the early stages.”

#194. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#190. US army postpones plan to detonate bomb-making chemical amid outcry

The controlled detonation can lead to a plume containing lead particles, worrying residents and state officials

The US army has delayed its plan to detonate a stash of bomb-making chemicals in south-eastern Iowa as questions swirl about the public health risks.

The army was set to blow up about 5 tons of lead azide in Middletown, Iowa, at noon on Friday, a move it described as the safest way to dispose of the highly sensitive compound.

During the controlled explosion, barrels of the material would be stored inside concrete structures and surrounded by sand-filled Hesco barriers reaching up two stories high.

Army officials advised certain residents of Middletown to evacuate between 8am and 2pm local time, due to the potential for window damage. The vibrations from the two blasts would not last long but could break old windows in town, and the resulting dust was expected to linger for no more than one hour, depending on wind speed, officials said.

The detonation is also expected to produce a plume containing lead particles.

Officials have released maps showing the potential for lead dust to drift north. Updated maps showed the plume’s shift, and the army will continue to update the map based on weather. Some schools in the path of the plume have already canceled classes.

Nearly 400 people live in Middletown, but there are thousands more in the path of the plume.

While the army recommends cleaning cars, windowsills and outdoor surfaces after the detonation, residents have taken to social media to voice concern and question whether that will be enough.

Iowa’s top leaders appeared to learn about the plan along with the public. The office of the Iowa governor, Kim Reynolds, was first briefed on Monday about the public advisory, her office said.

“An unacceptable lack of communication and clarity has made Iowans understandably concerned and angry about this situation,” governor-elect Rob Sand wrote on X on Thursday. “The public deserves to know, if this is the safest option, why other options were worse.”

Adam Telle, the acting secretary of the US army, alluded to the criticism in his Thursday announcement about the delayed detonation.

“Public communication is essential to public trust,” he wrote on X, adding that he was engaging state officials.

The plan is now expected to proceed on Monday. According to Telle, it’s the “latest possible moment”, before temperatures drop, which could risk destabilizing the material.

Middletown residents learned in March that the army needed to dispose of several dozen barrels containing lead azide, after the ammunition plant paused operations as part of a review and the degraded material was discovered.

“The safety of our people and our neighbors is non‑negotiable,” Lt Col Daniel Nosse, the commander of the plant, said at the time, acknowledging the volatility of the chemical compound used in munitions production.

The army shared more details during a public meeting in Middletown in October, advising that a detonation was planned for an unspecified date in November. The Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) on 13 November approved an emergency permit, paving the way for Friday’s detonation.

That permit required the army to track specific rates of potential air and dust dispersion and notify the public five days in advance, prompting Sunday’s original advisory to communities as far as 100 miles (160km) away.

Just a few miles north-west, the Danville community school district will close on Friday, the superintendent said in an announcement on Tuesday. Pat Wallace said his initial intention was to keep schools open, but there were enough uncertainties that made canceling “the most responsible decision”.

“I am not suggesting that the detonation will result in unsafe conditions,” he wrote. “The reality is that we simply do not know exactly what conditions will look like Friday afternoon.”

In Mediapolis, less than 20 miles (32km) north-east of the ammunition plant, city officials were approached about allowing soil, water and air testing late last month. But they only learned of their potential exposure on Sunday, when the initial map put them dead-center in the dust exposure area. Schools and the public library will close on Friday and the local fire department planned to hose down playgrounds that could be covered in lead particles.

The city of nearly 2,000 residents was no longer in the plume in an updated map.

“This is probably a first for me. I believe it’s a first for a lot of our citizens,” the deputy city clerk, Amy Stucker, said on Wednesday. “The biggest issue we’re having is the lack of information.”

Patrick O’Shaughnessy, a professor at the University of Iowa’s department of occupational and environmental health, said it was encouraging to see that the army and the EPA were doing all they could to minimize the dust, but it was still unknown how much would end up in the atmosphere and on surfaces.

Lead is a neurological toxicant that can decrease brain activity and development, especially in young children, O’Shaughnessy said. He is particularly concerned for toddlers who are at the age of putting everything in their mouth.

According to EPA documents, alternatives such as neutralizing and decomposing the contents of the drums using other chemicals were considered. But officials said any plan that involved handling the drums would increase the probability of accidental, fatal detonation.

#185. Trump DoJ moves to drop legal challenge to Mary Peltola’s Alaska Senate win

President had sought to block results if Democrat won race that featured two candidates named Dan Sullivan

In a legal filing on Thursday, the Trump administration asked a federal appeals court to dismiss the lawsuit it filed before the midterm elections seeking to block the certification of the US Senate race in Alaska should Democrat Mary Peltola win.

The legal challenge centered around the presence on the ballot of a second candidate with the same name as the incumbent, Republican senator Dan Sullivan. The Alaska supreme court already ruled that the second Sullivan’s candidacy was legal and that he could be on the ballot.

Peltola did win the election, with 50.13% of the vote, giving her more than the combined vote totals of all other candidates, including the incumbent Republican senator, Dan Sullivan, and a second Daniel Sullivan, who was reportedly recruited to enter the race by Democrats to siphon votes away from the sitting senator.

The suit was filed at the direction of Donald Trump saying that a Peltola win should not be certified until an investigation can determine if voters were confused. The administration never explained how such an investigation could take place given the secrecy of the ballot.

The administration moved on Thursday to have an appeals court declare its case moot, two days after the race was decided.

Peltola faced significant controversy on the campaign trail, after the New York Times reported that her staffers were involved in a scheme to recruit the “decoy” Dan Sullivan.

Peltola denied any involvement in encouraging the second Sullivan’s run.

Peltola, who is Yup’ik, will join the Minnesota senator-elect Peggy Flanagan, a member of the White Earth band of Ojibwe, as the first two Native American women to serve in the US Senate.

The legal filing came a day after Alaska concluded its vote-count and used ranked-choice tabulation to decide races where no candidate got more then 50% of the votes cast, including the election for the state’s sole representative in Congress.

One of those candidates, Eric Hafner, was apparently also included on the ballot in an attempt to confuse voters. Hafner, who was allowed to contest the race as a Democrat, over the objections of the state’s Democratic party, and despite having no connection to the state and currently being incarcerated in federal prison in New York, got 22,715 votes.

Had all of his ballots been cast for Hill, who was backed by the state’s Democratic party, Hill would have defeated Begich.

#183. Texas botches execution of Reinaldo Dennes as staffers struggle to find vein

Dennes, 70, was convicted of killing a Houston jeweler, and groaned for an hour after pentobarbital was administered

Texas executed Reinaldo Dennes on Thursday in a procedure that took over an hour as prison staffers struggled to find a suitable vein for the two intravenous needles carrying a lethal dose of pentobarbital, a sedative.

Dennes, who was found guilty of capital murder in the death of Janos Szucs, a diamond dealer found shot multiple times in his Houston office in January 1996, was the second person put to death in the US on Thursday, after Florida carried out a record-breaking 20th execution of the year, of Thomas Mitchell Overton.

Dennes gave a brief statement before the procedure began, then he told the warden: “Go, warden!”

Almost immediately he exclaimed over the pain, swearing and begging “the father” to “let me go”. He complained of more pain in his leg,

then said, “I don’t think this is normal. Take me, father. Don’t let me suffer like this. C’mon.”

The procedure was completed at 7.07pm, 63 minutes after he was strapped to the gurney. A minute later, after being examined by a physician, Dennes was pronounced dead at 7.08pm at the penitentiary in Huntsville.

Prison officials said all procedures were followed appropriately.

This took place at the same time Florida carried out a record-breaking 20th execution for the year, of a man convicted of killing a pregnant woman and her husband during a home invasion.

Thomas Mitchell Overton, 71, was pronounced dead at 6.19pm after receiving a three-drug injection at Florida state prison in Raiford. In a final statement, he denied ever breaking into the couple’s home and attacking them, saying: “I never killed anyone.”

The executions of the two septuagenarians in one day reflect the ageing of the US death row population.

Overton was convicted of killing Michael and Susan MacIvor and their unborn child during an August 1991 break-in at their Florida Keys home. Dennes was convicted of capital murder in the death of Janos Szucs, a diamond dealer found shot multiple times in his office in January 1996.

On Thursday, Overton woke up at 5am and remained compliant, a Florida department of corrections spokesperson, Jordan Kirkland, said during a news conference. He did not meet with any visitors or a spiritual adviser.

Florida has now carried out more than half the country’s 39 executions this year under Ron DeSantis, the state’s outgoing Republican governor who has made aggressive use of the death penalty like few other governors. Thursday’s executions come amid renewed scrutiny of capital punishment in the US after Tennessee’s failed execution of Christa Pike in October.

Texas set a record for executions there when 40 people were put to death in 2000. Dennes became the ninth person to be executed by the state this year.

The execution followed renewed scrutiny of capital punishment in the US in the aftermath of Tennessee’s failed execution in September of Christa Pike, who survived two doses of pentobarbital that her lawyers say probably did not enter her bloodstream correctly and instead pooled in her arms, leaving them burned and blistered.

On Thursday, the US supreme court denied a final appeal in the Florida case while an appeal remained pending in the Texas case.

Overton was convicted in 1999 of two counts of first-degree murder and received a death sentence for each. Defense attorneys had argued that investigators had planted the DNA evidence.

The MacIvors were found dead in their Tavernier, Florida, home in August 1991, according to court records. Autopsies determined both were strangled and that Susan MacIvor, who was eight months pregnant, had been raped. Overton, who had worked at a gas station near the MacIvors’ home, was arrested on an unrelated burglary charge in 1996. Authorities said a sample taken from him matched the sample collected at the MacIvors’ home, and he was charged with their killings.

In the Texas case, Dennes, a jewelry designer, had an office in the same building as Szucs, according to court documents.

A security guard, who was shot twice but recovered, identified Dennes as his attacker.

Police found the jeweler’s body upon entering his office, according to court filings. The documents said officers found the jeweler’s safe empty with no sign of the diamonds or the $200,000 in cash he was supposed to have on hand. The diamonds have never been recovered.

A year after Dennes was convicted, his brother, Jose Albert Dennes, pleaded guilty to murder in the case and was sentenced to 45 years in prison. He was released on parole in 2019.

#181. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.

#179. Trump administration asks federal court to dismiss its lawsuit over US Senate election in Alaska – as it happened

Trump DoJ moves to drop legal challenge to Mary Peltola’s Alaska Senate win

In a legal filing on Thursday, the Trump administration asked a federal appeals court to dismiss the lawsuit it filed before the midterm elections seeking to block the certification of the US Senate race in Alaska should Democrat Mary Peltola win.

Peltola did win the election, with 50.13% of the vote, giving her more than the combined vote totals of all other candidates, including the incumbent Republican senator, Dan Sullivan, and a second Daniel Sullivan, who was reportedly recruited to enter the race by Democrats to siphon votes away from the sitting senator.

The suit was filed at the direction of Donald Trump even though Alaska’s state supreme court had already ruled that the second Sullivan’s candidacy was legal.

The administration moved on Thursday to have an appeals court declare its case moot, two days after the race was decided.

Peltola faced significant controversy on the campaign trail, after the New York Times reported that her staffers were involved in a scheme to recruit the “decoy” Dan Sullivan.

Peltola denied any involvement in encouraging the second Sullivan’s run.

Peltola, who is Yup’ik, will join the Minnesota senator-elect Peggy Flanagan, a member of the White Earth band of Ojibwe, as the first two Native American women to serve in the US Senate.

The legal filing came one day after Alaska concluded its vote-count and used ranked-choice tabulation to decide races where no candidate got more then 50% of the votes cast, including the election for the state’s sole representative in Congress.

In that race, Alaska’s incumbent Republican congressman, Nick Begich, narrowly defeated independent challenger Bill Hill based on the second choices of voters who cast their ballots for other candidates.

One of those candidates, Eric Hafner, was apparently also included on the ballot in an attempt to confuse voters. Hafner, who was allowed to contest the race as a Democrat, over the objections of the state’s Democratic party, and despite having no connection to the state and currently being incarcerated in federal prison in New York, got 22,715 votes. Had all of his ballots been cast for Hill, who was backed by the state’s Democratic party, Hill would have defeated Begich.

Donald Trump, who bragged in a 2016 debate that not paying any federal income tax certain years “makes me smart”, and is now suing the New York Times for defamation over its reporting on his taxes, announced on Thursday that he has pardoned the late baseball star Pete Rose, who served five months in federal prison in 1990 after being convicted of filing false tax returns.

Rose pleaded guilty that year to failing to pay $162,703.15 in taxes on $354,967.60 in undeclared income from personal appearances, baseball card shows, the sale of personal memorabilia and gambling winnings.

The two felony convictions came a year after Rose was banned from baseball for life after admitting that he had gambled on baseball games.

Trump issued his posthumous clemency to Rose two years after the fallen star’s death, and two weeks after the president told 60 Minutes that even if, after his own death, it was a condition to enter heaven that he forgive enemies like James Comey and Nancy Pelosi, “I probably cannot do that, no, because I do hate some of my enemies and I always will.”

Religious messages that US agriculture secretary, Brooke Rollins, emailed to the agency’s 100,000 employees were unconstitutional, and she must be careful to avoid proselytizing when communicating with workers, a federal judge in San Francisco ruled on Thursday.

US district court judge Trina Thompson, who was nominated by Joe Biden, said the emails violated the First Amendment’s establishment clause, pointing to her position of authority, her use of an official email account and the messages suggesting that Christianity is the shared faith of USDA workers.

The Court ordered “that the Secretary must recognize certain boundaries when disseminating messages which convey religious content”.

Rollins sent the messages, which included Christian messages and images, from her official email account on holidays such as Independence Day, Thanksgiving, Christmas and Easter. She did not send messages commemorating holidays from other religions.

“Happy Easter – He is Risen indeed!” Rollins wrote in an April 5 email marking Easter.

“And so, like the very first disciples to encounter our risen Lord in the Upper Room almost two thousand years ago, this Easter let us too be alive with hope, full of Paschal joy, and confident in the mission each of us has been called for,” she added.

On 23 December 23, 2025, the judge noted, “Rollins emailed USDA employees a Christmas message containing a video in which she stated that ‘The spirit of generosity flows from the very first Christmas when God gave us the greatest gift possible, the gift of his Son and our Savior Jesus Christ, who came to free us from our sins and open the door to eternal life. This is the reason for the season’”.

Senator Elissa Slotkin, a Michigan Democrat, has endorsed her colleague Chris Van Hollen in his bid to replace Chuck Schumer as the party’s leader in the Senate when Democrats take the majority in January.

Slotkin, a former CIA analyst, said in a social media video announcing her support for Van Hollen that that she has been “vocal for many, many years about the importance in the Democratic Party of getting a new generation of leadership”.

“The American public want a new generation, and they want big and bold ideas,” Slotkin said. “I’ve been clear as well that I don’t think that senator Schumer is best positioned to be our next leader.”

In a less-than-ringing endorsement, Slotkin also said that because she wants Schumer out, she is backing Van Hollen.

“I’m encouraged that people like Chris Van Hollen got in the race, and because I’ve been clear that I wasn’t supporting Schumer, I will be supporting Chris Van Hollen,” she said. “But I think no matter the outcome, it’s going to be imperative for our leaders to demonstrate how they’re going to incorporate that next generation and what big and bold ideas specifically they’re going to put forward in the first 100 days.”

Van Hollen responded to Slotkin’s statement by saying that they agree: “the American people spoke, and we need new leadership in the United States Senate. Now is the time to act boldly, and we’re going to work together to deliver results.”

A day earlier, Van Hollen’s Maryland colleague, senator Angela Alsobrooks, said that she would be supporting Schumer, but framed it as a comittment she had made before he entered the race. Calling Van Hollen “a friend and respected colleague” with “an absolute right” to challenge Schumer, she said that she would continue to support Schumer. “I offered him my commitment, and I am a person of my word.”

“I respect her position that she made a commitment prior to when I joined the Majority Leader race,” Van Hollen said in a statement after Alsobrooks came in for some criticism for her decision. “These discussions among our party are critical to move our democracy and our caucus forward. And above all, they must be based in mutual respect and civility – there is no space in this discussion for vitriol or hostility.”

Constantino, a Trump-endorsed sticker magnet from northern New York, narrowly won the race to represent New York’s 21st congressional district, defeating Democrat Blake Gendebien just 1.5 points in a district former Republican congresswoman Elise Stefanik won by over 24 points in 2024.

Constantino was spotted in Washington DC last week, when he told a TMZ reporter that his call for Trump to free Combs from federal prison after his conviction on prostitution-related crimes, “caught attention.”

“I’m the most talked about person entering Congress right now,” he added.

The newly elected congressman is already facing calls from local Republican officials to focus on his work, instead of promoting his sideline as a right-wing rapper best known for a music video attacking Zohran Mamdani, the mayor of New York City, which is over 200 miles south of his district along Canadian border.

“The City of Plattsburgh Republican Committee is calling on Congressman-elect Anthony Constantino, to immediately change course, and put the urgent needs of NY-21 first, or face a formal call for his expulsion from Congress,” a local party official said in a statement on Thursday.

“We do not care about your rap career. We do not care about anything that does not directly serve the people of NY-21,” David Souliere, the chairman of the Plattsburgh Republican Committee said. “The residents of this district are counting on real results … if this is not your singular priority, then it is time for you to seriously question whether you belong in this role at all.”

#175. Fiona the hippo dies after ingesting knitted hat at Cincinnati zoo

Zoo staff tried a colonoscopy, vibration plate and other procedures to aid the hippo in passing the hat to no avail

Fiona the hippo, one of America’s most famous zoo animals, has died after efforts to remove a knitted hat that she swallowed after it fell into her enclosure proved unsuccessful.

“It is with extreme sadness that we share with you the devastating news that Fiona did not make it after multiple attempts from our amazing vets and keeper team to help her through what ended up being a medical emergency,” the zoo wrote in a post on social media.

With surgery too risky for full-grown hippos such as nine-year-old Fiona, who weighed in the region of 2,500lb (1,113kg), veterinarians tried an alternative method of getting the hat to pass through her system.

The hat fell near Fiona when a visitor to the Cincinnati zoo tossed it in the air, after which it bounced off a ceiling fan and fell into the enclosure, according to the Associated Press. Fiona ate the hat and was initially asymptomatic.

Days later, however, she began to lose appetite and staff at the zoo were forced to feed Fiona more in the hope that she would eventually get the hat through her digestive system.

“Despite multiple attempts to move the hat out of her system, it was ultimately too much to overcome. The team performed necessary supportive care today in an attempt to save her, but it was not successful and she did not recover,” the zoo statement said.

The zoo announced on Wednesday that the animal care team was using a vibration plate from a horse farm to help move things along, according to the Associated Press.

Fiona was sedated a second time on Tuesday for evaluation. After an ultrasound, veterinarians could see her intestines still trying to push the hat through. They also used a 9ft scope colonoscopy to view her intestines.

“The hope is that the vibrations will help encourage movement through her digestive tract and help the object pass,” the zoo had said on social media. Fiona remained out of her enclosure while she was receiving treatment.

Footage from the procedure released by the zoo on Wednesday shows her snorting and moving around her enclosure, which vets said was a sign of progress. Her hydration levels were being monitored.

Within an hour of the announcement, visitors began leaving flowers and cards by a hippo statue outside the zoo. “Forever our Fiona,” one note read.

Fiona became a global sensation after her premature birth in 2017, when she couldn’t stand on her own.

Fiona was born six weeks premature at just 29lb (13.2kgs), falling below the lowest recorded birth weight for her species at the time. Because she was initially too weak to nurse, Cincinnati Zoo staff hand-milked her mother while documenting Fiona’s recovery through photos and videos that captivated the internet.

Fiona admirers flocked to the zoo when her death was announced. Amy Haacke, a special educator from Dayton, said outside the Cincinnati Zoo that she has been a “huge fan” of Fiona since she was born because she likes underdogs. Haacke said she uses the Fiona book series in her classroom and owns three Fiona shirts.

“We were coming here anyway, but it kind of means a lot to be here,” Haacke said on Thursday. “We were here when she was first born and, I guess, now we’re here when she’s gone.”

Aftab Pureval, the Cincinnati mayor, said on social media that Fiona was “a symbol of perseverance and joy” in the city. Mike DeWine, the governor of Ohio, called her “a true icon”. The Cincinnati Bengals also expressed condolences, dubbing Fiona “the queen of the Queen City”.

The Cincinnati Reds baseball team also paid tribute: “Forever an icon. Rest in peace, Fiona.”

Chelsea Clinton, daughter of Hilary and Bill Clinton, shared her memories of Fiona and a tribute. “I will always treasure the art Fiona made for me and the smiles she gave me,” Clinton wrote.

The San Antonio zoo – whose hippo, Timothy, had a long-distance romance with Fiona – offered heartfelt condolences to the Cincinnati zoo. “Fiona held a special place in our San Antonio Zoo family and community … beyond the playful romance, Timothy and Fiona’s connection created a unique opportunity to educate audiences,” the zoo wrote. “Fiona’s impact reached far beyond Cincinnati, and the joy she brought to so many will never be forgotten.”

In a joint post with JD Vance, the US vice-president, on Instagram, second lady Usha Vance shared a photo of one of their children face-to-face with Fiona through a glass wall of her enclosure. The family has a home in Cincinnati.

“We are so sad to hear the news about Fiona,” she said in the caption. “Our family loved visiting her at the Cincinnati Zoo.”

Her survival in 2017 had offered a much-needed positive chapter for the zoo following months of intense public backlash over the fatal shooting of Harambe, the silverback gorilla killed after a child breached his enclosure.

Wildlife facilities have long warned of the dangers posed by foreign objects dropped or blown into animal habitats. Past incidents have demonstrated the fatal risks involved, including a rare antelope that choked to death on a plastic cap of a snack pouch at a Tennessee zoo two years ago, and a polar bear at a German zoo that died in 2014 after swallowing a visitor’s dropped jacket.

→ Baca artikel asli

#174. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

Hotly anticipated Bonnie and Clyde-inspired adventure GTA VI has gamers glued to their screens around the world

Grand Theft Auto fans around the world pulled all-nighters, skipped work and school, and attended overnight launch parties to vicariously embark on police chases and coordinate robberies as the latest instalment of the eagerly anticipated series arrived.

GTA VI, a Bonnie and Clyde-inspired crime adventure game set in the fictional US state of Leonida, was launched at midnight on Wednesday after a 13-year wait.

The hype around the release led to schools encouraging pupils not to miss lessons in anticipation of “GTA flu”, a term coined for the swathe of absences when GTA V was released in 2013, which is expected to recur over the coming weeks. The human resources platform Remote said there had been a 32% increase in leave requests worldwide on Thursday.

The launch also surpassed broadband records in the UK. Virgin Media O2 said its evening traffic between 8pm and 10pm on Wednesday was 9.4% higher than the previous record, set last week by “pre-loading” for GTA VI.

Judge in Florida says an expert has determined the 82-year-old estranged father of the Duchess of Sussex is mentally competent to face charges

An expert has determined that Thomas Markle, the 82-year-old estranged father of the Duchess of Sussex, is mentally competent to face charges of keeping child sexual abuse material in storage in California, a judge said on Thursday.

Some images appear to depict him with children, the FBI said.

Markle was initially released on bond but was arrested again when authorities accused him of cutting off his electronic ankle monitor on 10 November.

The Sun, a British tabloid newspaper, reported that Meghan’s half-brother found child sexual abuse images while clearing out their father’s home in the Philippines. The discovery led to a search of the two US storage units in California, which Markle had rented since 2008.
