#167. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#161. Patient ‘horrified’ to find her sensitive discussions were shared with more than 100,000 NHS staff

Exclusive: Investigation launched after details of grief and trauma uploaded, raising questions over extent of issue

The Information Commissioner’s Office (ICO) has launched an investigation after a patient was “horrified” to discover that notes of sensitive discussions with her psychotherapist had been shared with more than 100,000 NHS workers.

Rosanna (not her real name) found out that her detailed therapist’s notes, including about her grief and trauma, had been uploaded to her patient records when she saw them on a computer screen during a visit to her GP.

Inquiries made by Rosanna under freedom of information laws revealed that her therapy notes had been accessed by staff who did not work at the hospital trust where she was being treated.

Her discovery led to her stopping the treatment she was receiving for bipolar disorder with South London and Maudsley NHS trust (SLaM). It meant she had no mental health support during a close family bereavement.

Rosanna’s case has prompted questions in parliament, and suggests many other patients’ psychotherapy notes, including those of children, could have been shared without their knowledge or consent. This week the ICO confirmed it would investigate the case after a request to expedite it by the former Labour minister Philip Hunt.

Her case also raises questions about whether the new single patient record, proposed under the health bill, will include intimate details from any psychotherapy sessions provided by the NHS.

SLaM said it was reviewing how it documented psychotherapy on electronic patient records and how it communicated how data was shared.

Rosanna’s psychotherapy notes were uploaded to the London Care Record (LCR), an electronic patient record accessible to up 140,000 NHS staff, at a time of increasing concern about health workers snooping on patients.

She said: “I felt horrified looking over my GP’s shoulder as they checked what I had told them about my therapy records being available on the LCR. It’s like discovering that your personal diary has been photocopied and placed in a library.”

SLaM said “no intimate details” from therapy sessions had been shared, but in a response to Rosanna it admitted that “key session content” from therapeutic discussions was automatically uploaded. It also told her: “Very private material can be shared in the context of treatment.”.

In September Rosanna attempted to clarify exactly what had been shared on her medical notes by submitting a subject access request to SLaM. So far SLaM has failed to respond to that request.

She is concerned that sharing therapy notes, including those of children, could present a danger to patients.

She said: “Where trauma is involved, it can create a safety risk. You don’t want the most intimate parts of your life getting into the hands of the wrong person. SLaM’s own list of what it shares includes all events from its child and adolescent mental health services.”

She added: “When I asked what was being shared, a senior therapist told me he didn’t know all the answers. SLaM now accepts staff and patients may not have been given enough information.”

Rosanna said there should have been a choice to opt out of such information being shared, but SLaM had failed to inform her that her therapy notes would be available to tens of thousands of staff.

Her MP, Bell Ribeiro-Addy, asked the government what guidance had been issued to mental health trusts on sharing therapy notes. James Frith, a junior health minister, replied that all records “relating to direct care are shared on a basis of implied consent”.

Rosanna said: “It’s implicit that therapy is confidential unless you’re told otherwise, within the normal safeguarding limits. Patients should be told where their data is going. You can’t object to something if you don’t know it’s happening and you haven’t been told.

“I paused my psychotherapy because I couldn’t be confident where what I said in treatment would end up. I’ve been without mental health secondary care support since, including while a parent was seriously ill and after they died.

“I am concerned other people receiving therapy from SLaM will see this and, like me, pause treatment. I would urge you not to, and to learn about your data rights instead.”

Rosanna supports the new planned single patient record, but thinks the focus should be on sharing medical details, not the contents of therapy sessions. “Transparency over what will be on the shared record and the option to opt out is essential,” she said.

Prof Derek Tracy, SLaM’s chief medical officer executive, said: “We apologise again to the patient for the delays in responding to her concerns.

“We put the utmost importance on patient confidentiality and the trust placed in us by our patients and service users. We have clear guidance on limiting the information recorded. This includes any significant changes or themes, risks and agreed actions.

Andy Bell, the chief executive of the Centre for Mental Health, said: “Normally when you start therapy there’s a discussion about confidentiality. The therapist will say ‘If you tell me something that creates a safeguarding risk to yourself or others I will have to tell x y and z’. Not everything can remain confidential.

“A situation like this does raise issues about the boundaries of what is shareable and what isn’t, and what is appropriate to go on someone’s notes and what absolutely shouldn’t.

“If a patient is finding out information about themselves on the system that they didn’t expect, that suggests that the system isn’t working properly.”

#160. Academy trust pays out after Prevent threat over children’s Palestine badges

Lion Academy Trust apologises after threatening four pupils in east London with referral to counter-extremism scheme

An academy trust will pay out tens of thousands in compensation to four children who were told they could be referred to Prevent for wearing Palestine flag badges to school.

The school, part of Lion Academy Trust, has issued a formal apology after it sent letters to the children’s parents saying they might be referred to the counter-extremism programme or to local hate crime teams.

The issue dates back to November 2023 when the children’s school, Barclay primary school in Leyton, east London, held a non-uniform fundraising day for Children in Need.

The four children, all British Muslims aged between six and 10 at the time, attended wearing items displaying Palestinian colours or the Palestinian flag, in memory of children who had been killed in Gaza and those who continued to suffer during the conflict, which was ongoing at the time.

One child wore a Children in Need Pudsey sticker featuring a bow tie in Palestinian colours. Another wore a homemade badge bearing the Palestinian flag and colours.

Parents of the four children said they had repeatedly asked the school to apologise for their treatment of the children and had launched a legal challenge when none was forthcoming.

At a hearing at Central London county court on Thursday, the court heard that the academy trust had now made a formal apology to the children and their families and would pay them substantial compensation.

Lawyers for the children alleged direct discrimination, indirect discrimination and harassment on grounds including race, ethnicity, religion and belief, together with breaches of their rights to freedom of expression and freedom of thought, conscience and belief.

Shahid Achhala, the father of one of the children, said: “What happened to our son was deeply disturbing and should never have happened to any child. He was just eight years old, expressing compassion and solidarity with children suffering in Gaza, including members of his own extended family. Instead of being supported and protected by the school entrusted with his care, he was deliberately subjected to mistreatment that caused him considerable emotional distress.

“For almost three years, we have had to fight legally for accountability and an acknowledgment of the wrongs we believe were committed. While we welcome Lion Academy Trust’s apology, we cannot overlook the distress caused to our son and our family, or the fact that this matter could have been resolved long time ago.”

The families were represented by law firm Leigh Day and supported in their legal claim by Prevent Watch, which provides casework support to individuals and families affected by the Prevent duty.

Layla Aitlhadj, a director at Prevent Watch, said: “In this case, parents who were protecting their children’s rights were told that their concerns were ‘false and malicious’. They held firm through three years of pressure. Today the families, and the wider community who stood with them, have been vindicated.”

The academy trust has been approached for comment.

#156. ‘I saw a picture of a two-year-old girl buried in mud. I knew we had to do something’ – This is climate breakdown

When the floods hit Pakistan he went out to help. Then the water reached his own family. This is Imran Lodhi’s story

Please enable Javascript to fully experience this interactive article.

Disaster: Pakistan intense monsoon rainfall

Imran Lodhi is a university lecturer with a doctorate in climate change and sustainable development. The 41-year-old was living in Dera Ghazi Khan, a city in south-western Punjab, when floods devastated provinces across Pakistan. Climate change made the monsoon rainfall that caused the floods more intense.

In 2022, I was living in an area that had less risk of flooding. I had a small child and my PhD thesis was just a few weeks away. I tried to avoid flood news on social media until I saw a picture of a two-year-old girl who was buried in mud. It was probably her last breath. She died after that. That was the turning point.

I immediately called my friends, six to seven people, in a mosque. I talked to them: “We don’t know what to do, but at least we can start from somewhere.” Initially, it was food. We hired some commercial cooks.

We planned to work only in villages close to our city. A lot of people were taking refuge in nearby high places, high enough that the water did not reach them. On the first day we went with some food to the nearest village.

When the rain is over, the sun comes out. Usually, it’s a good sign. But, in those areas, it didn’t feel good. It felt like it opened a scar. The pain that was hiding somehow had become open. It was like being naked. I could see trolleys, tractors, vehicles and homes all drowned in the flood. There was nothing from the government, nothing from any development sector. There was no camp. People were just living under open skies.

Men felt that their women being in an open place was a kind of emotional damage for them. It was not normal for most of the people here. Many women never came out. They came out for their work, the agriculture work, but not sitting that openly among the public. Their hair was full of dust and dry mud. Their clothes had a heavy layer of dry mud; no shoes on their feet. Mothers were not breastfeeding. Babies crying for milk, and the milk was not there. Mothers trying to soothe them with fake sounds.

The cows did not have feed and were not giving milk. Many of the animals were lost, their bodies wide open, decomposed. Then the water, which stayed there for a week, started to smell very bad. There was no good smell.

We prepared food in the morning. Then we prepared food at lunchtime. We generally finished distributing food around 8pm. One night it was 11pm and we thought some people might not have food. So we just cooked some food late at night and went to every camp.

One old woman started to laugh. I was surprised. I asked her: “It’s OK, we have food, but why are you laughing?” And she said: “I was just complaining, ‘Lord, that you left me with an empty stomach today.’ And suddenly you appeared.”

It was a very strange experience for her. For us also. People, when they are in tough conditions, are attached to their beliefs. And, when things like that come up, they establish a spiritual connection with the people who are helping out.

We posted on social media. We tried to mobilise. A lot of people contacted us who were from other parts of Pakistan. Money poured in and we kept expanding the team. From two or three villages, we started to cover the entire district, and then almost the entire region.

The government protocols were to not go into certain areas that looked dangerous or were not safe for a boat. Families were trapped, many, many kilometres into the water. There was no land in one village. Small wooden cupboards were hung on trees so stuff could be saved from the water. The immediate shelter was a big tree near them. The whole family was in the tree. The elders were lying on the branches. They somehow made a swing for the kids. They spent days in those trees.

A few villages were completely destroyed. Not a single home was standing. Everything was in shock, even the trees. One old woman who was living with her married kids was not ready to leave that rubble. She was so attached to that home that she felt like there was no life after it. She had a small cupboard in which she kept some small things to sell, for her independence. She was so concerned about that small cupboard. When I reached there, that cupboard was all broken.

One day I had a fever and my wife stopped me: “Don’t go out – you are not well enough to help other people.” But I still went out to a village to help people. When we reached there, I got news that the city we were living in – which I thought was safe – suddenly was a source of threat. There was water coming toward the city. Within hours, the water would be in the city. My family was there. And I was almost 100km away.

So I rushed back. But the road toward the city was cut off by the flood. There was no way I could go back to my family. I called my wife and I asked them to stay vigilant and look out for water in the city. I gave them some suggestions: how they could save themselves and evacuate. That was a very, very tough call.

Eventually, the city was saved. Next day, we took a boat to get past the road that was cut off. Sitting in that boat, I was thinking about my family. Once I reached the other side, I called some people to take me home.

When I reached home, I had a very strong fever and I spent that day at home. I tried to behave normally, but everybody knew I was not feeling well. I took one day off from community work.

The next day I wanted to stop. But the problem was, I was in the middle of that network. If I stop, this whole process will stop. This whole network of relief work will stop.

The next day, even with a high fever, I left for the field because I didn’t want that to stop. When I grew up, the community fabric was very strong. It was common practice that whenever people got sick, got in an accident, hospitalised, people would visit the person and share some money.

This was the kind of culture I observed happening around me.

I went back to those areas where the flood came. There was still some water standing so there were remains of that devastation. But there’s a very common sweet in that area we call jalebi. I can smell that sweet in those villages. I see shops open. I see farmers, labourers. I felt good watching people working again. It looked like life was coming back.

There was an annual poetry event happening three months after the flood. Saraiki culture is very rich, one of the oldest. I was consulted by one of my friends who is the organiser of that event. Should we suspend this year’s events or not?

I said you should never suspend the event. Let people believe the normalisation, let people believe in the spirit of survival. They organised the poetry event.

This is climate breakdown was put together in collaboration with the Climate Disaster Project at the University of Victoria, Canada. Read more.

#154. India has been named a ‘destination of the year’ for 2027 – here are insiders’ tips on travelling beyond the tropes

As tourism booms, three experienced female travellers give their take on how to make the most of travelling here

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

The 13th-century Persian scholar Zakariyya al-Qazwini never travelled to India. But he catalogued it in his famous work The Wonders of Creation and Rarities of Existence, which drew on accounts of a distant land filled with “rarities par excellence”. For al-Qazwini, as Helen Pfeifer writes in the London Review of Books, wonder was not an emotion but an intellectual method of making sense of the world and acquiring knowledge.

India has long occupied such a space in the global imagination, as a land of extraordinary riches; spirituality, customs, chaos and contradictions. This has shaped how the country is perceived from afar, but it can often flatten its complexities.

So when, last week, Travel + Leisure named India its destination of the year for 2027, I found myself considering what it means that the country holds such multitudes. The magazine’s recognition will undoubtedly add to its growing appeal as a travel destination. But expectations built around India can differ from the experience of being there, particularly for women.

My own experiences of travelling in India largely surround my work as a journalist, often visiting places that would never make it into a travel guide. I remember the towering chinar tree outside the home of a woman in Kashmir whose son had been killed in an encounter with the security forces; the rolling hills of Odisha, where we travelled to meet a guerrilla fighter; the tiny hut in Chhattisgarh where, after a 13-hour journey, our team found a meal of rice and lentils. These scenes have stayed in my mind, for their landscapes as much as for the stories and circumstances.

Ultimately, such experiences were far more illuminating than those from a conventional travel itinerary, and taught me to navigate uncertainty and embrace discomfort. For this week’s newsletter, I spoke to three women about the journeys in India that stayed with them and what travelling here has meant to them.

One of the most memorable journeys for travel writer Shivya Nath was a trek through the Tirthan Valley in Himachal Pradesh, with a crew of local women.

Trekking has always been a male-dominated industry and Nath, author of the book Rootless and Restless, was struck by the personal journeys of the women leading the expedition. (She travelled with an organisation which trains women to become guides, overcoming social and family restrictions.) “For some of the women, spending even two nights away from home required negotiating with their families,” she told me.

The route wound through dense forests in the Great Himalayan national park, across ancient bridges, over gushing, undammed rivers, accompanied by the soundtrack of birdsong. “The only sounds you hear are of nature,” she recalled. Along the way, they stopped to forage lingri, a seasonal wild fern used in regional cooking, which they later prepared at a riverside camp they set up for the night.

The following morning, the group hiked to a waterfall, standing together on a large rock in the middle of the cascading water. Being with “just a group of women who love being in the outdoors, was a really powerful feeling for me”, she said.

For Afsha Sethi, a corporate leadership professional from Bengaluru who has travelled extensively in India, one of her most cherished travel experiences is an unlikely one. As part of an extracurricular project when she was in high school, students were paired with a local community through an NGO. She spent a week shadowing a farming family growing rice and areca nut in Karkolli, in northern Karnataka.

Walks through lush forests with waterfalls were a daily routine. “We would come back with leeches on our legs, and then put salt to get rid of them,” she recalled.

The trip also brought her into contact with something she had not encountered before. When her roommate told the host family about getting her period, their sleeping arrangements were shifted to the courtyard. “It turned out to be a great experience, because the weather was very nice and [we were] looking at the stars and just chatting late into the night,” she said. It reminded her that travelling through India can mean encountering different social conventions.

‘The moment somebody puts up something on Instagram, everyone throngs to it’

The travel boom in India is led by domestic travellers. The country recorded almost 2.95bn domestic tourist visits in 2024, a jump of 17.5% from the previous year, according to recent government data. Overall, India ranks 31st in the World Economic Forum’s Travel and Tourism Development Index 2026, up eight spots since 2024.

Women accounted for 43% of foreign arrivals, an interesting counterpoint to India’s reputation as a challenging destination for female travellers.

But the boom raises questions about what happens to the places being discovered. “Social media has definitely accelerated the over-tourism problem,” says Nath. “I think one of the most basic things travellers can do is to avoid geotagging locations.” She suggests choosing community-owned tourism initiatives that allow local people to benefit directly from visitors.

Nayanika Gaffino, a mother and recreational athlete who has also clocked up the miles around the country, has seen this happen first-hand in Goa, where she lives. “The moment somebody puts up something on Instagram, everyone throngs to it … even though it doesn’t have the capacity to handle so many people,” she says, adding there are beaches her family now avoids because of this.

Travelling with girls, Gaffino also has to consider practicalities such as hygiene and access to clean public toilets and safety. As such, she gravitates towards boutique properties that are accustomed to hosting families with children.

Nath acknowledges that solo travel can require extra vigilance. On solo trips, she prefers family-run homestays or guesthouses where someone is aware that she is on her own and can watch out for her. At the same time, she adds, India can be an intensely hospitable place where people generously invite you for tea or a meal.

Discovering a place, she says, should also include finding ways to preserve the very qualities that led you to it in the first place. The journeys the women described involved seeing a side of India that differed from their own expectations. This is what makes al-Qazwini’s understanding of wonder relevant for me. To approach something with wonder is not to be impressed by just beauty or uniqueness but to have a curiosity about what it might show us – its possibilities and its limitations.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Politics | More than one-third of the international delegates – about 115 people – expected to attend the Congress party’s Bharat summit over the weekend were denied entry to India or had their visas cancelled, according to the Wire. Congress described this as an attempt to stifle voices of criticism, while the foreign ministry said that visa-related decisions are “sovereign functions” but did not share further details.

Lawsuit | The US state department has served summons in a civil lawsuit brought by Khalistani separatist leader Gurpatwant Singh Pannu against national security adviser Ajit Doval and former research and analysis wing, Samant Goel, according to the Hindu. The foreign ministry described the lawsuit as “frivolous”.

Culture | A 50-year-old Navaratri festival at Wesleyan University, in Connecticut, has made it an unlikely home for Carnatic music and dance traditions. Although such festivals take place at cities around the world, few institutions have become as significant a platform for Indian music and dance.

A recommendation from someone with great taste

Shaunak Sen, director of the Academy Award-nominated All That Breathes, recommends: “If you find yourself in the oft-trodden window of wanting to watch something for an hour after dinner, but feel drowned in the mediocre flotsam of the big streamers, I have a suggestion watch the strangely inventive short films of the artist-bureaucrat SNS Sastry from the 1960s and 70s [on YouTube]. Unlike the regular pedantic state films of the time, Sastry somehow created avant garde, surreal, even funny films, funded by the Films Division of India. I Am 20, which interviewed young adults born in the year of India’s independence, is an inquiry into a certain national zeitgeist. Between the candour of the participants and the sharpness of their complaints, one can scarcely believe this was made under the aegis of the government.”

Firecrackers were responsible for most of Delhi’s fine particulate matter of PM 2.5 – about 71% – in the hours after Diwali celebrations, according to a first-of-its-kind assessment by the Indian Institute of Tropical Meteorology. The national capital reported its most-polluted Diwali since 2021, data from the Central Pollution Control Board showed.

AI anxiety is everywhere. Pranav Bhasin’s comedy We Were Here has brought that to screens in a short film that is making an impression internationally. Bhasin’s mockumentary is about three retired men in a small Indian town who, persuaded that artificial intelligence is taking over human jobs, fight back by impersonating household appliances. One becomes a dish antenna, another a vehicle’s reverse camera and a third a microwave. The situation becomes more complicated when one of their children takes a job at an AI company. “Do you want artificial intelligence?” “No, I have real one,” goes one caustic exchange.

The New Yorker released the film on YouTube and it’s eligible for Oscars consideration after winning at the Palm Springs International ShortFest.

#151. Violent protests and killings in Bangladesh ahead of exiled Sheikh Hasina’s planned return

Awami League party called for ⁠nationwide protest on Thursday after the government rejected Hasina’s request for passport

Parts of Bangladesh were shut down Thursday as protests by supporters of former prime minister Sheikh Hasina sparked clashes, bomb blasts and arson attacks, with the ancestral home ⁠of former leader Muhammad Yunus set ablaze in the violence.

Hasina’s Awami League party had called for a ​week-long programme of protests and rallies to mobilise support ‌for Hasina’s planned return in December, culminating in a ‌nationwide lockdown on Thursday.

Hasina, who has lived in exile in neighbouring India since she fled Dhaka following violent ⁠protests demanding her resignation in August 2024, has requested a new passport and identity card to facilitate her return. Her supporters are protesting against the government’s rejection of that request.

To deter ⁠violence, 97 platoons of the Bangladesh Border Guard fanned out across seven districts, including Gopalganj and Faridpur, considered to have many Hasina supporters, a spokesperson ⁠said.

Police said former interim leader Yunus’ ancestral home in Chattogram, about 300km (186 miles) south-east of Dhaka, ​was set on fire late on ‌Wednesday.

It is likely that flammable ‌substances were thrown into an unused ground-floor room, police told Reuters, adding that Yunus’ paternal family members, who live on the upper floors, are safe.

Reuters could not immediately reach the Yunus Centre thinktank that speaks on his behalf to seek comment. Yunus and his government, which led Bangladesh after Hasina’s ouster, have faced hostility from her supporters.

During the last 10 days of protests, police have reported dozens of crude bomb blasts and incidents of ‌vehicles being set afire in at least 28 districts, injuring several police officials and members of the public, media have said.

Late on Tuesday, an activist of the ruling Bangladesh Nationalist party (BNP) was allegedly killed by unidentified people during a protest near Hasina’s home district of Gopalganj, police said.

On the same night, a crude bomb exploded outside the Dhaka residence of cultural affairs minister Nitai Roy Chowdhury, and the house of a BNP lawmaker and shops ‌belonging to BNP supporters in Gopalganj were set on fire, police added.

On Wednesday morning, a bus company helper was burnt to death in the southern district of Pirojpur after two buses ​were set on fire, authorities said.

Tuesday marks a year since Hasina was ​sentenced to death by a local court that found her guilty of ordering a deadly crackdown on a student-led uprising against her in 2024. She has lived in exile in neighbouring India since fleeing Dhaka.

On Thursday, authorities detained former education minister Nurul Islam Nahid, who faces charges ⁠over several killings during the 2024 protests, from his house in Dhaka, police told Reuters.

At ​least 37 branches and offices of ​Grameen Bank, set up by Yunus, have been targeted in arson attacks or ​attempted fires since 10 November, ‌with 14 more ​attacked since midnight ​on Tuesday, the bank said.

On Tuesday, the home minister Salahuddin Ahmed accused Awami League leaders abroad of inciting supporters to destabilise Bangladesh and torch property, adding that those involved in violence would be identified and prosecuted.

Awami League leaders, most of them in hiding, could not be reached for comment.

#150. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#149. Myanmar committed genocide against Rohingya minority, world’s top court rules

Judgment finds ‘intent to destroy a substantial part of the Rohingya population’ after case seen as critical test for dealing with most serious of state crimes

Myanmar committed genocide against its Rohingya minority during a 2017 military campaign, the world’s highest court has ruled, in a historic and unprecedented decision.

The judgment from the international court of justice (ICJ) – which has never previously found a state responsible for genocide – ends a seven-year legal battle that was seen as a critical global test for dealing with the most serious of all state crimes.

The ICJ president, Yuji Iwasawa, said a majority of nine judges on the 15-seat court found that “the Republic of the Union of Myanmar has committed genocide against the Rohingya”.

The 2016-17 campaign by Myanmar’s military forced more than 700,000 mostly Muslim Rohingya to flee across the border into Bangladesh, arriving with reports of mass killings of adults and children, gang-rapes and burned-down villages.

Nearly a decade later, in a refugee camp in Bangladesh, crowds of Rohingya people watching on outdoor TV screens leaped up in celebration and applause as the final verdict was read out.

One Rohingya survivor, Yousuf Ali, had travelled to the court to hear the verdict. “Today, we have finally been heard, and it is a profound relief,” he said. “This judgment gives hope to our children and the next generation of Rohingya. But our fight is not over.”

Governments had been anxiously awaiting the decision handed down in the Netherlands, where the UN-run court is based, in part because the ruling will set a precedent for genocide claims, notably a high-profile case against Israel.

In what could become a crucial factor for the Gaza case, where Israel claims its mass killings are part of a counter-terrorism operation, judges dismissed Myanmar’s claims of carrying out legitimate military operations against insurgents.

Genocide, defined after the Nazi Holocaust by a 1948 UN convention, is harder to prove than war crimes because it requires evidence of specific “intent” to destroy a national, ethnic, racial or religious group. Iwasawa said during the reading of the decision that the court was “fully convinced that Myanmar committed acts falling under … the genocide convention with an intent to destroy a substantial part of the Rohingya population in Myanmar”.

After the ruling, Britain’s foreign minister, Ed Miliband, said the government was expelling Myanmar’s defence attaché and called “urgently” for a session at the UN security council, the body that has the power to enforce ICJ rulings.

The case was raised by the Gambia, a Muslim-majority state, on behalf of Myanmar survivors. It rested on claims of genocidal statements and “patterns of conduct” that showed attempts to destroy the group during assaults on Rakhine state, including gunning down people as they fled and blocking their return.

In January, the ICJ held closed-door sessions to hear from survivors who had travelled from camps in Bangladesh. More than a million Rohingya still live there, in the world’s largest refugee settlement, and fewer than 600,000 are estimated to remain in Buddhist-majority Myanmar.

Refugees who had fled to Bangladesh told the Guardian in 2017 of a massacre in the village of Tula Toli, where survivors described systematic shooting of civilians, mass graves and the drowning of children in a river. That village, which satellite footage later showed had been burned down, was one of several analysed by rights investigators and built a case for genocide.

During hearings, the Gambian justice minister, Dawda Jallow, told the court the case was not “about esoteric issues of international law”. “It is about real people, real stories and a real group of human beings,” said Jallow. “The Rohingya of Myanmar. They have been targeted for destruction.”

Legal experts see Thursday’s judgment as a key test for sexual violence in genocide assessments, as the convention says the crime can include acts with the intent to “prevent births”. Investigators have said gang-rapes and soldiers’ mutilation of women’s genitals were carried out intentionally and systematically to stop reproduction.

The court appeared to back that assessment, accusing the military of using sexual violence to “physically and mentally impair the willingness and ability of the [Rohingya] population to procreate”, referring to cultural stigma for rape survivors. Myanmar had also imposed other measures to restrict births, the court ruled, such as limits of two children per Rohingya family.

Myanmar has denied carrying out a genocide, claiming its actions targeted a Rohingya militant group that conducted guerrilla-style attacks. Its military responded with massive “clearance operations”.

However, judges rejected Myanmar’s claim that its actions were primarily intended as counter-terrorism operations, or even that they were an attempt to punish or forcibly displace the population but without committing genocide.

The country’s leader, Min Aung Hlaing, who headed the military during the 2016-17 attacks, has a pending arrest warrant from the international criminal court (ICC), which, unlike the ICJ, prosecutes individuals and not states, on allegations of crimes against humanity.

The former general overthrew the elected government of Aung San Suu Kyi in a 2021 coup, triggering a civil war that has left tens of thousands dead. He was installed as civilian president in April after elections that were widely discredited.

While the ICJ does not have the power to enforce rulings, its decisions are considered binding, carry diplomatic weight and can shape international action, including sanctions.

During a reading of the decision that lasted more than two hours in a packed Great Hall of Justice in The Hague, Iwasawa called for Myanmar to provide reparations and restitution. This included allowing the safe return of Rohingya to their homes, returning or replacing their property, and providing physical and mental rehabilitation.

The ruling was also significant as it formally classified the Rohingya as a distinct ethnic group under international law, something they have been denied by Myanmar, which refers to them as Bengalis or foreign migrants.

Reached by phone shortly after he left the courtroom, the ​lead counsel for the case against Myanmar told the Guardian the mood had been emotional in the room as the judgment was read out in front of his legal team, which included Rohingya.

“This is the ICJ recognising their history of discrimination, recognising them as a group, recognising the horrific violence they endured and suffered,” said Arsalan Suleman. “It was a really emotional moment for them and the whole team. We felt the weight of the court’s judgment.”

Israel and its chief ally, the US, have been closely following the Myanmar case, as the decision provides the strongest hint of how judges could assess the Gaza case, brought by South Africa. UN independent investigations, as well as genocide scholars, have already said Israel has committed acts of genocide in Gaza.

A judgment finding Myanmar had breached the convention has been predicted to lead to the US imposing sanctions on ICJ judges, including this year’s Nobel peace prize winner, Navi Pillay.

Washington had considered the Rohingya crisis to be a genocide, based on a decision by the Biden administration. However, the administration of Donald Trump has been on the offensive against international rights bodies, including imposing sanctions on the ICC.

The US state department said in a statement that it would study the ICJ’s decision “to fully assess its implications” and that “the proper interpretation of the genocide convention is of paramount importance, as is preventing politicisation of the ICJ”.

It reiterated US support for the people of Myanmar and said it shared “grave concerns over the continued abuses of human rights against Rohingya Muslims and members of other minority groups”.

The Trump administration of has been implacably opposed to international legal organisations. On Thursday, the president used a trilateral meeting with Russia and China on Thursday to condemn “rogue globalist organisations”. But Trump’s ire has been directed most towards the ICC, a separate body which the administration has vowed to dismantle “brick by brick”, rather than the ICJ.

Two other ICJ cases dealing with atrocities in the 1990s Balkan wars fell short of finding states directly responsible for genocide, although Serbia was found legally responsible for failing to prevent or punish genocide committed by Bosnian Serb troops at the Srebrenica massacre.

Other UN tribunals have established genocide in only a handful of cases since the Holocaust, including Cambodia’s Khmer Rouge rule in the 1970s and the 1994 mass killings in Rwanda.

#147. ‘Everyone struggled for this justice’: tears in court as Rohingya vindicated in Myanmar genocide ruling

Relief in the immediate aftermath of a landmark ruling, but what happens next is in the hands of governments around the world

There were tears in the halls of The Hague in the Netherlands and an eruption of applause in the refugee camps of Bangladesh as the scattered communities of Rohingya watched the UN’s top court rule the Myanmar military had committed genocide against them.

For almost a decade, the Rohingya have awaited this moment; recognition from the international court of justice (ICJ) that Myanmar in 2017 intentionally tried to destroy the mostly Muslim minority group through a campaign of mass killings and rape. More than 10,000 Rohingya were killed and 700,000 fled to neighbouring Bangladesh.

By the time they spilled into the court’s halls, the relief was evident. A Rohingya community leader threw a big thumbs up as he left the courtroom and others immediately took out their phones to call family in the refugee camps.

Salma, 30, a Rohingya survivor who lost family members in the 2017 genocide, said: “Everyone worked hard and struggled for this justice, for this cause. When they hear it today, everyone will be happy, thank God. I don’t know how to express it or even what to do with myself.”

Another Rohingya present, who cannot be named because of safety concerns, said they cried as the judge went through each of the rulings.

“During the last few minutes I couldn’t even sit down any more, I had to move to the back because I didn’t know how I’d react,” they said. “I saw some of the other Rohingya around me crying. They were so emotional.

“Now the responsibility is with the international governments. It has been declared genocide now and they have to act.”

In Bangladesh, where most of the Rohingya population have lived – in the world’s largest refugee camp – since being forced to flee in 2017, the verdict was translated into the Rohingya language for the crowds who had gathered to watch on screens specially set up for the purpose.

They immediately leapt up, cheering, applauding and shouting gratitude for the Gambia – the faraway African nation that brought the case to the ICJ on behalf of the stateless minority. Many held posters that read “Thank You Gambia” and “Justice = Safe Return”.

“Since we arrived here, we have been waiting and looking forward to this justice,” said Farida Khatun, 50, who has been a refugee in Bangladesh since 2017, when her village, Tula Toli, was targeted by soldiers. The village in north-western Myanmar, near the Bangladesh border, was the scene of a massacre in August of that year, with reports of mass killings and gang rapes.

She said, however, that she does not expect to be able to return home. Tula Toli was completely destroyed by Myanmar’s forces and no longer exists. “Even though today’s verdict makes us happy, my heart still cannot believe that they will take us back in true peace.”

The case has taken seven years to reach this point, with Rohingya researchers working to collect testimony from survivors. The initial hope for justice had faded during the long wait for a ruling. Many were also left deflated during early hearings when the one-time pro-democracy hero Aung San Suu Kyi defended the Myanmar military at The Hague.

Before today’s ruling, some were optimistic, others were prepared to be disappointed, but all said they knew themselves that they were victims of genocide.

The tension was finally released when the court declared Myanmar was responsible for genocide against the Rohingya, highlighting the use of sexual violence and attacks on children alongside the killing of Rohingya who tried to flee violence, which it said meant the violence amounted to more than ethnic cleansing.

The court ordered Myanmar to provide reparations for the Rohingya and end continuing breaches of the genocide convention.

There is now also an international criminal court investigation into the violence of 2017, looking specifically at the crime of mass deportation, and for which prosecutors in 2024 sought an arrest warrant for Myanmar’s leader, Min Aung Hlaing.

With continuing violence against the Rohingya, the possibility of a return to Myanmar, as happened after previous exoduses to Bangladesh in 1978 and the early 1990s, remains unlikely.

But many hope the ICJ ruling will be the start of a process of ensuring justice and lead to real international pressure to ensure their safety in Myanmar.

“We will be able to return to our homeland – with our rights, with our citizenship, and having received our full security. We will be able to return,” says Salma.

#146. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#141. 'It's a dream come true' – South African DJ celebrates featuring in GTA 6

DJ Lag is one of South Africa's best-known electronic music producers

South African DJ Lag says it is a "dream come true" to have his music featured on Grand Theft Auto 6 (GTA 6), the hugely popular video game released on Thursday.

DJ lag, one of South Africa's best-known electronic music producers, is among several African stars to feature, alongside Nigerian singers Burna Boy and Asake.

"I can't explain the feeling. I started playing the game last night and it's amazing," DJ Lag told the BBC's Newsday programme, adding that he had been playing GTA since he was about 10 years old and could hardly believe he was now part of the latest edition.

Many players have already had a chance to play the game following its worldwide launch.

The game, which lets players explore a fictional American city, features a dedicated African music radio station playing Afrobeats, amapiano and other sounds from across the continent.

The release of GTA 6 ends a 13-year wait for a new edition of the game.

GTA 6 is a satirical video series associated with crime and violence

Rockstar Games, the developer behind the franchise, has revealed details of the game's soundtrack and some in-game radio stations, but has yet to release a complete list of songs featured in the game.

Rockstar says AfroBank FM features everything from 20th Century African classics to contemporary amapiano and 3-step, giving the continent's music a prominent place in the latest edition.

Burna Boy co-hosts the station alongside British DJ and producer Palms Trax, while Asake's hit song Joha is among the tracks players can listen to as they drive around the game's virtual world.

DJ Lag, whose real name is Lwazi Asanda Gwala, is regarded as one of the pioneers of gqom, a genre of electronic dance music that emerged in the port city of Durban in the early 2010s.

Ghanaian highlife legends Pat Thomas and Ebo Taylor also feature, alongside South African artists Robot Boii and Kamo Mphela, bringing a mix of classic and contemporary African music to one of gaming's biggest franchises.

Rockstar has also announced a 34-track official soundtrack album, released in partnership with Atlantic Records, featuring an original Burna Boy song titled Bigger.

For African fans, the inclusion of familiar music adds another dimension to the experience, allowing players to hear some of the continent's biggest sounds while exploring the American setting.

The series allows players to roam through sprawling fictional cities, drive vehicles and complete missions involving crime, police pursuits and other adventures.

The latest instalment takes players to Vice City, a fictional version of Miami, and the surrounding US state of Leonida, inspired by Florida.

Its story follows two criminals, Jason and Lucia, who become caught up in a wider conspiracy after a robbery goes wrong.

The launch has generated enormous excitement among gamers around the world.

Some fans downloaded it in advance so they could begin playing as soon as it became available, while others have booked leave from work.

The previous edition, GTA 5, was one of the best-selling video games of all time, selling more than 200 million copies worldwide.

The new release is expected to be another major commercial success, although it remains to be seen whether it will match its predecessor's extraordinary sales.

#140. Israel delays building contracts for West Bank settlement after UK sanctions

Attorney general says E1 settlement decision should not be taken by caretaker government led by Netanyahu

Israel has postponed issuing contracts for the construction of a large settlement on the West Bank seen as likely to render a two-state solution unviable, days after the UK imposed sanctions against building firms operating in the area.

Israeli diplomats insisted the move was not in response to the measures from Downing Street. Gali Baharav-Miara, the attorney general, said any decision on settlement expansion should not be taken by the caretaker government led by Benjamin Netanyahu but by whatever new administration was formed.

She ordered the Israel land authority and ministry of housing not to open the tender process. Israeli diplomats suggested the UK foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, had acted prematurely on Monday in sanctioning the eight construction firms. But UK diplomats believe the growing political pressure on Israel over settlements, including from US Democrats, is having an impact.

The deferral puts the E1 settlement expansion in the mix for any coalition negotiations. The leader of the Change bloc, Gadi Eisenkot, seen as Israel’s most likely next prime minister, has been clear in opposing violent settler activity, as has the newly elected Knesset speaker, Matan Kahana, an ally of Eisenkot.

Eisenkot has also opposed the E1 settlement expansion, describing it as a mistake likely to alienate international opinion. He said of the plan: “I recall that Netanyahu himself opposed this for a long time, and due to coalition pressure, he gave in and approved it.”

The EI settlement expansion, due to have been announced on Thursday, has long been seen as qualitatively different since the land – between Jerusalem and Ma’aleh Adumim, a West Bank settlement city of nearly 40,000 residents – bisects the West Bank and cuts it off from East Jerusalem.

Miliband has said it would mark the death knell of a future Palestinian state. The UK, along with a group of European countries, has recently recognised Palestine, and the preservation of a two-state solution has been described as the north star of Labour policy towards the region.

The construction tender was published for more than 3,401 housing units in the area. At the beginning of November, the Israel Defense Forces began demolishing homes in a Bedouin community within the E1 area.

Israel has already enforced the downgrading of the UK consulate in East Jerusalem as a reprisal for Miliband’s decision in September to try to end trade links with the illegal settlements.

Bennett said: “The British Labour government’s hostility, bias and one-sided approach are particularly glaring given its failure to take any action against the Palestinian Authority, which continues to encourage terrorism through financial payments, promote incitement in its education system and engage in illegal construction in violation of signed agreements.”

Although the Israeli election was largely fought on domestic security-related issues, Eisenkot seems to be more sensitive to the implications of Israel’s crumbling global reputation, and the need to make efforts to be seen to be listening to the criticism.

If he becomes prime minister, Eisenkot will, indirectly at least, also have to take account of the views of the Arab parties even if these parties are not going to become members of the government.

The group Peace Now welcomed the attorney general’s decision: “A government that lost the elections is not permitted to exploit its final days in power to advance destructive moves that will endanger Israel’s future and its international standing.

Construction in E1 is liable to eliminate the possibility of a two-state solution and drag Israel into a severe diplomatic crisis and international sanctions. The AG’s decision is an important step, but the struggle is not over yet.”

#138. This is a remarkable diplomatic moment for China's Xi - but it comes with pitfalls

Donald Trump, Vladimir Putin and Xi Jinping are meeting for a historic trilateral summit

Xi Jinping will believe he has achieved something no other world leader could in securing a historic summit with Vladimir Putin and Donald Trump in Shenzhen.

For decades, Washington and its western allies shaped the world order.

Yet it is China that has orchestrated the remarkable diplomatic spectacle of three of the world's most powerful protagonists sitting around the table.

And surrounding it all is the futuristic display that is Shenzhen, with driverless cars cruising past as drones fly overhead and robots - literally - dance in the streets.

Ostensibly, this is an economic summit for Asia Pacific leaders. But for Xi, it is also a demonstration of China's grip on the global economy and world politics.

His country is no longer just reacting to decisions made in Washington, London or Brussels - instead Beijing is at the heart of conversations which are reshaping the international order.

But this meeting is not without its pitfalls for Xi as there are many things he does not appear keen to discuss with Putin and Trump.

Follow live updates: Trump, Xi and Putin hold historic meeting on Apec sidelines

First, China is not keen for the war in Ukraine, now well into its fifth year, to be on the agenda.

This is a delicate balancing act for Xi who is aware his relationship with Putin may damage China's connections in Europe. The EU is a key market, especially now when the Chinese economy is heavily relying on exports.

However Xi also doesn't want the war to end in a way that will destabilise Russia, a key ally and neighbour.

Beijing is still trying to maintain that it is neutral in this war and has repeatedly called for negotiations and a political settlement while avoiding condemnation of Russia's full-scale invasion.

Russia's invasion of Ukraine, now into its fifth year, has claimed hundreds of thousands of lives

The US, especially under the previous Biden administration, and Europe have accused China of sending Russia components that it could use in its war in Ukraine, while also propping up the country's economy by buying oil and gas.

Xi wants to keep Putin close, but not too close because he also doesn't want Russia's problems to become China's problems.

Donald Trump has already made it clear he wants the three leaders to discuss limiting the world's supply of nuclear weapons.

The US and Russia possess the world's largest stockpile of nuclear warheads. But China is rapidly expanding its own arsenal, which is raising alarm bells in Washington.

The last major US-Russian arms control treaty, known as New START, expired in February this year, triggering fears of an arms race. The agreement had set a cap to control the number of nuclear weapons in US and Russia's arsenal.

Trump would like China to be included in any new agreement, but Beijing has pushed back.

Xi's argument will be that his arsenal is considerably smaller than what Washington and Moscow own, and, therefore, it would be unfair for Beijing to be bound by their limits. China also believes it should have the freedom to develop its own nuclear capabilities.

China unveiled intercontinental nuclear missiles at a military parade in 2025

This subject could complicate Xi's bid for China to be recognised as a leader on the world stage - because the US could argue that the status includes being a responsible nuclear power.

Xi will want to control the optics of this meeting, from start to finish.

Chinese officials like to nail down agendas with talking points and produce carefully choreographed speeches.

But in this unprecedented summit, Xi will have to live with two very unpredictable leaders.

Both Trump and Putin have their own agendas and the US President has already threatened to go off script.

There is a risk that they both dominate the meeting and Xi ends up looking like a host, rather than the architect of a major diplomatic moment.

Of course, Xi may be ready for this. It may even play into his hands.

The Chinese leader has been trying to portray himself as a stable global leader - at this summit he will be flanked by two men embroiled in wars they cannot seem to end.

So any unscripted moment could serve to highlight his preferred role as senior statesman.

For all the attention, this meeting of three men with vastly different agendas is unlikely to yield any significant outcomes.

But the power it projects is important for Xi as he heads towards next year's Communist Party Congress, where he is expected to secure a historic fourth term as leader of the party and country.

A job fair in Jiangsu province: China has been battling youth unemployment in a sluggish economy

He may hope to use this as propaganda at home, given the country is grappling with huge domestic challenges.

A country-wide property crisis has dented consumer confidence. Local governments are heavily in debt. Manufacturing has slowed and more of China's workforce has become dependent on flexible jobs with no pension or social security. One in five young people are thought to be struggling to find work. Frustration and anxiety about the future bubble up routinely.

Xi does not have to deal with elections, and the country has not seen major protests in decades. But he will be concerned about his popularity within the party elite as these problems - and the palpable frustrations they produce - grow.

At a time like this, a photo-op with Putin and Trump may offer him proof that his country's "century of humiliation" is over. China is no longer a rising power, he can argue - rather, it is increasingly shaping the rules of a new era.

#137. Eight injured after driver in Israel repeatedly rams car into Palestinians

Arab MPs accuse politicians of fuelling hostility as footage shows driver accelerating towards Palestinians at four locations in Kafr Qasim

Eight people were injured on Wednesday evening when a driver struck Palestinian residents at four separate locations in Kafr Qasim, a city in central Israel.

Footage shared on social media appears to show the car accelerating towards people, all Palestinians, at several locations in the city, striking them before driving away. Three of those injured were in moderate condition, and several of the victims were women leaving a nearby wedding, the Times of Israel reported.

Haaretz has reported that investigators are examining whether the driver struck people deliberately. Avi Levy, a police district commander, said the incident appeared to be a hit-and-run and that the driver had tested positive for drugs. He said the Shin Bet security service was taking part in the investigation and said: “As of this moment, nothing is coming up. The suspect has no criminal record.”

The incident prompted Arab lawmakers and activists to accuse Israeli politicians of fuelling hostility towards Arab citizens. Benjamin Netanyahu has called Arab parties “terror supporters” and said partnering with them would put Israel’s security at risk. The parties won a record 19 seats in the Knesset in the country’s recent election.

Yousef Jabareen, the chair of the Joint List, said the incident “raises difficult questions about the claim that the driver lost control”. He said: “I’ve been warning for weeks that the incitement against Arab citizens will turn into assaults and even assassinations.”

Said Issa, a community activist in Kafr Qasim, described the incident as an “act of terror”. “Someone who loses control of his vehicle might hit one or two vehicles, but not multiple people in several different places,” Issa said. “The attacker struck at four locations, one after the other.”

In a separate incident in the occupied West Bank, the Palestinian foreign ministry called on Monday for an Israeli settler to be held accountable after eight Palestinians were run over and injured in Tuqu, south-east of Bethlehem. Local sources told the Anadolu news agency that the driver had first struck three young men, then returned and ran over five others who had gathered to help them.

#136. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.
