#134. Why Canada is embracing AI data centres while the US pushes back

Shark Tank investor Kevin O'Leary is behind a major AI data centre project in Canada that has so far been embraced by legislators.

As Americans have grown increasingly sour on artificial intelligence (AI) data centre development, Canada's industry leaders are sensing an opportunity.

"Data centres in Canada looking pretty appealing right now," Lucy Hargreaves, CEO of non-profit policy group Build Canada, wrote on X the day after American voters passed 40 of 41 local ballot initiatives aimed at limiting or preventing data centre development in this month's midterm elections.

While the number of proposed AI data centres in the US dwarfs that of its northern neighbour, in recent months Canada is being increasingly pitched as a hub for these so-called "hyperscale" projects.

Those in favour argue that Canada's abundant land, natural gas and cold climate make it uniquely positioned to address some of the problems facing US AI infrastructure, from data centres' proximity to populated areas to noise pollution and strain on electricity grids.

"Some of the capital that would have been deployed to build data centres in the US is now more available for Canada," Hargreaves told the BBC in an interview.

"The question really is whether or not we want to go for that."

Unlike in the US, pushback to these projects in Canada has been muted - at least so far. Many are going ahead without significant disruptions, and legislators have openly embraced them without major political consequences.

At the heart of Canada's AI data centre boom is Alberta, where 90% of all currently proposed sites are set to be built.

The western Canadian province, rich with natural gas deposits, has pitched itself as "North America's destination of choice" for AI data centres, including by offering a "concierge" service for investors to help build these projects at "unparalleled speed".

One of those investors is Kevin O'Leary, the Canadian businessman, TV personality and an ally of Donald Trump, who announced plans this year to build two large AI data centres - one in Alberta and one in the US state of Utah.

His planned sites, both named Wonder Valley, are being received in very different ways on either side of the border.

Residents in Utah have organised a fierce opposition campaign., external The pushback led outgoing state senator Stuart Adams to demand in June that O'Leary scale down the project by 75%, external, from 40,000 to 13,000 acres.

Financing for the Utah Wonder Valley data centre is still underway, and it is unclear when - and if - shovels will hit the ground on the project.

The proposed 7,000-acre, 7.5-gigawatt Alberta Wonder Valley project, meanwhile, has received strong backing from both the provincial government and the local municipality.

The project is billed as "off-grid", external, relying on its own power system fuelled by natural gas sourced from the region. In April, the Alberta government exempted the project from an environmental impact assessment, external, arguing it was unnecessary, though it will still need to obtain permits prior to construction.

The project was met with only limited opposition at a local town hall earlier this year. One resident, Dan Harms, told Bloomberg in June , externalthat he saw the economic benefits of the Wonder Valley data centre and that it was being built "out in the middle of nowhere".

In an interview with BBC News, Reeve Ryan Ratzlaf, of Greenview Municipality in north-western Alberta, said the project was the "perfect fit" for his rural community, which had been looking for an industrial tenant to fill a resource-rich parcel of land for 13 years.

Some community members have expressed concern about the amount of water needed to cool the proposed site annually, which is projected to be six million cubic metres, or about the size of 2,400 Olympic-size swimming pools.

Ratzlaf argued the amount was a drop compared to the annual flow of the nearby Smoky River, from which the centre would pull water. More broadly, he said he believed that economic benefits to the region outweighed potential risks.

"It's a giant warehouse with computer equipment inside," he said, arguing that it was less risky than petrochemical plants that could have been built in its place.

Speaking to BBC News, the CEO overseeing both O'Leary projects, Paul Palandjian, said the two were proceeding at an "equal" pace.

He acknowledged it had become more of a "hot topic" in the US than in Alberta. But there are signs public anger towards AI data centres in Alberta has grown in recent months.

Recent public information sessions on provincial AI data centre plans were attended by some furious residents.

"If no one wants this, why are you pushing forward with this?" one resident asked in an August town hall reported on by the Canadian Press, external, held shortly after Alberta announced that Meta would build a C$13bn ($9bn; Â£6.8bn) AI data centre in the province.

Sturgeon Lake Cree Nation has also recently sued the government over its decision to override the environmental assessment for O'Leary's Wonder Valley data centre, labelling it "irrational" in court filings.

This week, the Alberta government proposed legislation that would put restrictions on where data centres could be built in a bid to ease some concerns.

There are also signs that public cooperation may be fading elsewhere in the country.

On Saturday, hundreds of protesters gathered in Canada's Atlantic province of Nova Scotia against a proposed AI data centre, external that will be used by technology giant Anthropic.

But experts say the US backlash offers a cautionary tale for Canada as it tries to capitalise on the AI boom.

"AI is generally not very popular. This is both true in Canada and the United States," noted Sean Mullin, a senior fellow at the University of Toronto and former adviser to Canada's prime minister on the national sovereign AI compute strategy.

"I think we could experience some of the same backlashes if we're not thoughtful about it," he said.

BBC went to Arizona before the midterms to understand the data centre boom in the US

So far, lawmakers in Canada have stood firm.

The Carney government, which has made reducing Canada's reliance on the US a priority, has framed the development of AI data centres as a matter of national security and sovereignty. It has projected that Canada needs to expand its computing capacity 16-fold over the next four years to meet domestic demand.

Alberta is defending its data centre plans by saying they will create jobs and generate revenue, and that sites will be developed "responsibly".

In Ontario, Premier Doug Ford has said that his province needs to move "full steam ahead" with building data centres.

Mullin said one reason the backlash had been less severe in Canada was simply due to scope and timing.

"There has been less of these projects happening, and as a result, they've attracted less attention," he said.

Canada has released some high-level guidance on data centre development, which has been co-signed by Anthropic, Meta and other big AI players. It calls on data centres to pay for electricity costs, minimise water use and create long-term benefits for the regions they are in.

If politicians don't tread carefully, Bek MacIntosh, an Alberta resident who has fought a proposed hyperscale data centre in her community, said she believed public discontent could translate into angry votes, like those seen in the US.

MacIntosh successfully halted a data centre in Olds, Alberta,, external over concerns with its scale, scope and proximity to her home. Even after her eight-month battle, she said she was not opposed to all AI infrastructure and believed Canada had the resources and landscape to build them responsibly.

But she fears Alberta has opened the door to "the most reckless of reckless developers", and warned that voters might have a "knee-jerk reaction" if provinces don't take care to develop AI data centres responsibly.

"And I don't think any political party in Canada is prepared for it."

'We all need data, but do we need the largest data centre in Europe here?'

Did a data centre revolt affect the US midterm election results?

#132. Israeli tanks cross ‘yellow line’ boundary into Hamas-controlled central Gaza

First incursion across boundary since ceasefire deal comes as Eisenkot seeks to form government without Netanyahu

The Israeli military crossed into Hamas-controlled central Gaza on Wednesday in its first ground operation across the “yellow line” boundary since a fragile ceasefire was agreed last October, forcing dozens of displaced Palestinians to flee their tents.

Israeli tanks rolled along the Salah al-Din road, Gaza’s main north-south route, a day after the US-backed Board of Peace, a body overseeing the ceasefire, accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities.

An Israeli military spokesperson said troops were carrying out an operation targeting “Hamas infrastructure” west of the yellow line in central Gaza.

The operation lasted about 24 hours and on Thursday the military said it had been “completed”.

The IDF ​has not commented extensively on the operation, which came as the Israeli opposition politician, Gadi Eisenkot, has been seeking to form a government without the caretaker prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, who has built his political reputation on promises to keep Israel safe.

A Gaza security source told AFP there was “intense Israeli artillery shelling” north of Nuseirat refugee camp and reported Israeli military vehicles in central Gaza “amid ongoing gunfire and airstrikes since midday”.

Hamas condemned the Israeli raid and the forced displacement of Palestinians as “a flagrant violation of the ceasefire agreement” and a dangerous escalation. The Israeli military did not immediately respond to Hamas’s statement.

Residents reported hearing tank fire in the area. Palestinian medics said one person was killed in an Israeli airstrike near the site of the raid.

After the statement from the IDF that it had concluded the tank raid to dismantle a suspected Hamas tunnel, the defence ministry’s body in charge of civilian affairs in Palestinian territories, Cogat, said Palestinians would again be free to move in the targeted areas near al-Bureij and Nuseirat refugee camps.

In separate incidents on Wednesday, Palestinian medics said two people were killed by Israeli fire, one in Deir al-Balah and the other in Gaza City. The Israeli military said it was unaware of any incident in Deir al-Balah and had struck a militant in Gaza City.

Israel has stepped up its attacks on Hamas, which it says has been regrouping and rearming. Hamas has accused Israel of trying to derail the US-brokered ceasefire agreed in October 2025.

Israeli strikes have continued since the ceasefire, and Hamas remains armed.

Last week, an Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in southern Gaza killed six people, including a surgeon and a disabled man, and wounded 28 others, according to the hospital director.

The body overseeing Donald Trump’s plan to end the war in Gaza has accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities, leaving Palestinians in dire conditions and the ceasefire at risk of collapse.

In its second report to the UN security council since the truce began 13 months ago, the Trump-led Board of Peace said the gap between what was agreed and what was happening on the ground remained the biggest threat to the deal. The report, seen by Reuters, warned that “a ceasefire that delivers nothing visible cannot be sustained indefinitely”.

Gaza’s health authorities say at least 1,600 Palestinians have been killed since the ceasefire came into effect. The Israeli military says five of its soldiers have been killed over the same period.

Palestinian authorities say more than 74,000 people have been killed in Gaza since Israel launched its military campaign in response to the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, in which about 1,200 people were killed.

In June, an ⁠independent UN inquiry determined that Israel was continuing to commit genocide by deliberately targeting Palestinian children. A previous report by the commission in September 2025 found that Israel had committed genocide in Gaza, and Israeli officials, including the prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, incited those acts. Netanyahu is separately wanted by the international criminal court (ICC) for war crimes.

Reuters and AFP contributed to this report

#131. More climate money for Africa? Europe says use what's already pledged

Africa is pushing for adaptation finance to be tripled by 2035, from an annual $40bn (Â£30bn)

In Uganda's northern Karamoja region, Elijah Lobur and his family now eat just one meal a day.

A severe drought that began in April has destroyed crops, while food brought in from elsewhere has become increasingly unaffordable.

"Seven months without rain has brought us to this point where we now live in hunger," the 44-year-old farmer, who lives with his grandmother, wife and five children, told the BBC.

"Even some of the wild plants that we used to eat have withered and dried up."

Local officials say more than 90% of crops have failed in the region, where agriculture is largely rain-fed.

Further south, in Malawi, Mathews Nkhambala has experienced the devastating effects of flooding.

In April 2024, barely a year after Tropical Cyclone Freddy devastated the country's southern region, floods displaced people in Chikwawa district.

Nkhambala fled to higher ground with his wife and four children as floodwaters swept through his village.

"As we reached a safe height, we saw our house swept away right in front of our eyes. Everything I had was gone," he told the BBC.

He lost a harvest of 15 bags of sorghum and part of his farmland. His children were so traumatised that they stopped going to school.

"They refused to go to school for many months. They were so disturbed," he recalled.

After relocating and trying to rebuild his life, Nkhambala is now facing another climate challenge - this time a lack of rain.

"This time we fear there may be no harvest. I have no clue what I will do."

A government assessment after Cyclone Freddy found that Malawi had experienced more than 50 disasters linked to storms, floods, landslides and droughts since 1980, affecting millions of people.

While Uganda and Malawi are suffering the effects of climate change and their governments are asking for assistance adapting to the new global weather patterns, they are among 23 African countries yet to submit National Adaptation Plans (NAPs) to the UN climate convention secretariat.

These are the plans that detail how climate change has affected them and the risks they face, and so help make a case for what assistance they need. Without them, experts say, it becomes difficult to secure funding.

"Our region has experienced cyclical droughts for decades, but there has been no action plan to tackle these climate impacts," said Milton Lorpia, who works for Warrior Squad Foundation in Karamoja region of Uganda.

"If only such a plan were in place and implemented, it would certainly have helped us."

Mathews Nkhambala and his wife fear their harvest may fail this year

The African Group of Negotiators at the ongoing UN climate summit (COP31) in Turkey is pushing for adaptation finance to be tripled by 2035, from an annual $40bn (Â£30bn) baseline that it says developed countries committed to providing by 2025. The interpretation of that commitment remains contested.

The proposal was not accepted for formal negotiations at the summit, although African negotiators have continued to push for it.

The funding is intended to help vulnerable countries prepare for and withstand climate change-induced extreme weather, such as droughts, floods and wildfires.

But the European Union, one of the largest providers of climate finance, says the priority should be ensuring existing commitments reach vulnerable countries rather than setting new funding targets.

"We haven't come to the table with additional monies or additional capital. It's about what we have committed already and ensuring it's deployed," Darragh O'Brien, who leads the EU delegation at COP31 on behalf of the Irish presidency of the Council of the EU, told a press conference.

He acknowledged that developing countries faced difficulties accessing available funding, saying the process needed to be improved.

Wopke Hoekstra, the European Commission's climate commissioner, also argued that the summit should focus on delivering existing pledges rather than making new ones.

"If you are a vulnerable nation in the Pacific or one of the LDCs (Least Developed Countries) and the money is in the bank but does not land on the ground then it is effectively meaningless," he said.

'It can kill us': Kenyans fear impact of El NiÃ±o with floods predicted

Hoekstra said the EU had provided $30bn of the $100bn in climate finance committed by developed countries in recent years, adding that other wealthy nations should contribute more.

The scale of the need is substantial. According to the UN's Adaptation Gap Report published last year, sub-Saharan Africa requires about $51bn annually in adaptation finance, while the Middle East and North Africa needs $112bn.

Globally, developing countries will need about $300bn annually by 2035, according to the UN's latest report. Yet they received only about 10% of that amount from developed countries in 2024.

Climate-related loss and damage in Africa is projected to cost $440bn between 2020 and 2030, according to Nairobi-based think tank Power Shift Africa.

Under the 2015 Paris climate agreement, developed countries are required to provide financial support to developing nations, including for climate adaptation.

But why have so many African countries failed to prepare plans that could help them identify their needs and build resilience?

"If you look closely, the countries that are yet to submit their NAPs are already dealing with multiple crises, including debt distress, economic pressures and other issues," said David Abudho, climate justice lead at the aid agency, Oxfam.

"Their governments' ability to invest in climate adaptation is limited."

Sheila Apiny, regional climate justice adviser at ActionAid, said the lack of plans reflected a wider shortage of financial and technical support.

"The absence of NAPs in so many countries is, in itself, evidence of an adaptation finance and capacity gap," she argued.

She said this created a vicious cycle - countries lack support to prepare their plans, while not having the plans makes it harder to secure funding for climate resilience.

"When countries lack adequate adaptation planning and, more importantly, the resources to implement those plans, governments are forced into a cycle of responding to disasters instead of preventing or reducing their impacts," said Amos Wemanya, senior climate adviser at Power Shift Africa.

"Instead of investing in early warning systems, climate-resilient agriculture, water security and flood protection, scarce public resources are repeatedly diverted towards emergency relief and reconstruction. This costs more over time and leaves communities increasingly vulnerable."

Under the 2015 Paris climate agreement, developed countries are required to provide financial support to developing nations, including for climate adaptation

African negotiators acknowledge that adaptation plans help countries prioritise investments and attract funding, but argue that their absence should not be used to deny them financial support.

"Of course, having a comprehensive NAP helps countries prioritise investments and prepare proposals for climate finance," said Mahlet Eyassu Melkie, a senior strategic adviser to Ethiopia's COP32 presidency.

"But Africa's adaptation financing needs are based on scientific assessments, climate vulnerability, national priorities and regional estimates â not simply on whether every country has submitted a particular planning document."

The UN Development Programme, which has helped more than 45 countries prepare their plans, said NAPs can help governments mobilise international support and coordinate climate action.

Fred Njehu, a Pan-African political strategist at Greenpeace Africa, said their absence could nevertheless create significant obstacles.

"It is fair to acknowledge that missing adaptation plans act as a major functional barrier that severely bottlenecks the speed, volume and quality of international funding a country can access," he said.

But having a plan does not guarantee the money needed to implement it.

"Even countries that have completed their NAPs have not received money anywhere near what they require for implementation of their adaptation plans," Apiny said.

Kenya offers an example of how adaptation measures can help communities cope with climate pressures.

The walls of the sand dams store water underground when the rivers dry up

In the country's arid and semi-arid south-east, concrete sand dams slow the flow of seasonal rivers, allowing sand to accumulate and store water underground. Communities can access it during dry periods using hand pumps or by scooping it out.

UK charity Sand Dams Worldwide and its local partner, the Africa Sand Dam Foundation, said they had built about 1,300 such dams, improving access to water as droughts become more frequent and intense.

But many vulnerable communities elsewhere on the continent are still waiting for similar interventions.

Back in drought-hit Karamoja, Lobur wonders how different things might have been if farmers had access to small-scale irrigation schemes.

"We do have some underground water sources in some places. If there were proper plans and programmes, we could have tapped them to grow food, but we are having to live in hunger instead," Lobur said.

"My grandmother keeps reminding us how she lost eight children during a famine-like situation in 1982, and we all fear we might perish like that too."

#130. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#128. Myanmar ruling gives new relevance to genocide convention and is ‘full of messages’ for Israel

Hailed as ‘a rare moment of justice’, judges’ decision sets out a roadmap for assessing the question of intent

The international court of justice (ICJ) verdict making Myanmar the first country in history to be found guilty of committing genocide does not just deliver justice for Rohingya victims. It also gives the 1948 genocide convention new relevance in governing the actions of nation states.

In their nine votes to six majority ruling, the panel of judges in The Hague set out a detailed roadmap for how the convention should be applied to real-world situations in which a government is accused of committing genocide against a whole population group, especially when it comes to assessing the question of intent.

The lengthy judgment will have been read particularly carefully by Israel, the only other country currently before the ICJ accused of genocide.

It is also relevant to Germany, which has been brought before the court by Nicaragua on the accusation it has facilitated and failed to prevent genocide in Gaza.

“I think it’s a judgment that brings the genocide convention alive,” said Philippe Sands, a leading human rights lawyer who was part of the Gambian legal team. “It gives the convention practical, real meaning. And if the alternative view had been taken, the convention would essentially become a dead letter.”

Amnesty International’s secretary general, Agnès Callamard, called the ruling “a rare moment of justice for survivors, victims and their families”. But it was also an assertion of humanitarian law in an increasingly lawless world.

“This is a good day for international law. We needed it,” said Dr Iva Vukušić, an assistant professor in international History at Utrecht University and a war crimes expert. “It is really important, clarifying a lot of law, and that’s going to be very helpful going forward.”

There will be a spillover from this ruling to its sister tribunal in The Hague, the international criminal court (ICC), providing a benchmark for when genocide charges could be made against individual perpetrators.

The Trump administration, instinctively hostile to international legal institutions, is also watching critically. The US ambassador to the UN told the ICJ before this judgment not to “lower the legal threshold for proving genocide” lest it be “weaponised” against US and allied soldiers.

The court did not directly lower the standard for demonstrating genocide, and the Gambia – which brought the case to The Hague – did not ask it to.

The judges kept the existing benchmark for judging genocide intent as the “only reasonable inference” of Myanmar’s actions and referred extensively to prior case law. But it went a long way towards clarifying how that standard should be applied in assessing atrocity crimes committed by states.

Before this case, only Serbia had faced the charge of genocide at the ICJ, in cases brought by Croatia and Bosnia and Herzegovina. In the Bosnian ruling in 2007, Belgrade was found to have failed to prevent the 1995 genocide in Srebrenica, but in both cases it was cleared of committing the genocide directly.

The new ICJ judgment finds the Myanmar military directly responsible for genocide against the Rohingya, a Muslim minority targeted in a ferocious counterinsurgency campaign that reached its peak in 2017.

In assessing the Myanmar campaign, the ICJ looked not just at the death toll of about 10,000 in assessing the scale of the atrocities, but at the total Rohingya population targeted in northern Rakhine state, 815,000 people. Significantly, it cast its net wider than just the death toll, considering mental damage done to the survivors, particularly victims of sexual violence, and the impact on reproduction in the Rohingya community.

Most importantly, the ICJ judgment provided a guide to interpreting the standard set by Serbian cases, in which a finding of genocide could be made only if there were not other plausible motives for mass killing.

In this judgment, the court ruled that Myanmar could have had other motives, such as the desire to displace or punish the Rohingya population, but that those motives could exist alongside genocidal intent. The judges said genocidal intent was the “only reasonable inference” from the “totality” of evidence.

In justifying that conclusion the judges pointed to several factors that international law experts say are relevant to the Gaza case, which is now hearing written pleadings and is expected to take at least another three years.

The common factors include the targeting of children, which the court described as striking at the “foundation” of the Rohingya as a group – the ICJ said up to 2,109 Rohingya children under the age of five had been killed, while more than 5,000 Palestinian children under five years old have been killed in the Gaza war.

Another incriminating factor cited by the court was the “bulldozing and large-scale terrain clearance” after the destruction of villages, followed by “rapid construction” including security installations, which has been the hallmark of Israeli army occupation of over 60% of the Gaza Strip.

“It’s full of messages for Israel,” said William Schabas, an international law professor and expert on genocide at Middlesex University. “There are obvious differences, of course, the evidence will be different, but if you exchanged the word Myanmar with Israel, you wouldn’t have to change much in the judgment.”

The fact that there were six dissenting voices on the bench suggested that substantial parts of the majority’s reasoning were not beyond dispute, and lawyers involved in the case cautioned that it did not necessarily mean that the same bench would find Israel guilty of genocide in Gaza. But the same lawyers added that the opposite was almost certainly true. If this case had failed, the case brought by South Africa against Israel would have been all but over.

#127. Cumin-laced fizzy sodas are giving cola giants a tough fight in India

Lahori Zeera will close this year with $115-120m in revenue

It began as an impromptu kitchen experiment.

Saurabh Munjal, Nikhil Doda and Saurabh Bhutna - three cousins in India's north-western Punjab state - were messing about at home post dinner one evening, when Doda casually brewed up a tangy cumin-flavoured fizzy soda using regular pantry ingredients.

They enjoyed its earthy, piquant taste so much that they decided to explore options to sell it commercially.

Cumin-laced sodas had always been sold at street-side stalls in India, but it was a broken market of local companies. Not many had built a carbonated beverage brand around the flavour.

The trio persuaded their families to invest some capital and launched Lahori Zeera in 2017. Zeera is the Hindi name for cumin.

Almost a decade on, the drink has become a bit of a sensation in India, challenging the entrenched monopoly of global cola giants, especially in the all-important mass-market ten-rupee ($0.11; Â£0.078) beverage category - it will close this year with revenues of $115-120m.

Its popularity reflects a growing consumer preference for distinctly Indian flavours and drinks perceived to be more healthy, say analysts.

Lahori Zeera is one of several cumin-flavoured soda brands that have mushroomed across the country over the past decade.

There's Jeeru from Mumbai-based Xotik Frujus, Campa Jeera from billionaire Mukesh Ambani-owned Campa Cola and at least half-a-dozen other variants from regional players including Fizz Jeera Masala from House of Bindu.

Bindu launched their cumin soda nearly 25 years ago, but sales were flat until some years ago, according to Megha Shankar, a director with the company.

"Now it is the fastest growing category among carbonated beverages," Shankar told the BBC. "Its contribution to our revenues has doubled from 20% to 40% in the last five years."

Soaring demand for the product has pushed House of Bindu to widen its footprint across the country, after being largely based in southern India for over two decades.

Parle Agro, external, one of India's oldest consumer brands, also rolled out its own cumin soda brand Dhishoom nationally last year after exclusively selling it in rural markets since 2012.

Online orders for these fizzy drinks, meanwhile, were up 900% this summer according to a report from one of India's online delivery companies.

Cumin is a warm, earthy spice widely used in Indian cuisine

The cumin frenzy is reshaping India's non-alcoholic beverages market, which was valued at $32 billion in 2024 and projected to double by 2033.

According to a report from Kotak Mutual Fund earlier this year, Lahori Zeera, Campa and other smaller challengers - now dubbed as India's "ethnic beverages market" - collectively doubled their share of India's carbonated soft-drinks market to around 15% by September last year, while the combined market share of Coca-Cola and PepsiCo fell from roughly 93% to 85%.

"This is a "Jio moment" for beverages. Just as telecom incumbents bled market share when data prices crashed, beverage incumbents are bleeding volume," the Kotak report said, drawing an analogy to the disruption caused by the entry of Reliance-owned Jio Telecom in India's telecoms market in 2016.

"In global markets,â¯establishedâ¯duopolies rarely lose more than 1-2% share annually; anâ¯8% swingâ¯in less than a year is a structural disruption."

Saurabh Munjal, CEO of Archian Foods which manufactures Lahori Zeera, told the BBC that the company has "always been in a situation where we are sold out each year. We've doubled our capacity to 1.2 million bottles per day last year and are still unable to meet demand".

For decades, the colas have had a dominant presence in India, appealing to the aspirational part of the population as lifestyle products that were cool to consume.

With millions of dollars in marketing budgets splashed out on billboards, Bollywood stars and cricket championships, they sold a very western idea of hydration to India, says Munjal.

But home-grown brands are now mounting a serious challenge with a combination of smart distribution, attractive pricing and a turn towards nostalgic Indian flavours such as cumin, raw mango, kokum fruit and tamarind.

Munjal says that for long, there has been a tendency among consumer brands in India to either westernise Indian products or "Indianise" western products. But with Lahori Zeera, which uses the tagline "apna desi thanda", meaning India's own cold drink, his desire is to change that.

Shankar says her brand has also recently launched a zero-sugar version of their cumin soda to capture the growing market for healthier non-cola drinks.

Global giants like Coca-Cola and Pepsi have long held a monopoly on India's carbonated beverages market

This success notwithstanding, sustaining this winning trajectory will not be easy for these ethnic beverage brands, say analysts. The very factors that propelled their rise could also limit growth.

"The million-dollar question is how big can the cumin-flavoured drink market become. And I suspect it won't become as big as the colas," Rajiv Agarwal, a professor at Mumbai-based SP Jain Institute of Management, who advises family businesses on strategies to succeed against multi-national corporations, told the BBC.

The 10-rupee price point, which accounts for the bulk of sales volumes in India's cumin-flavoured carbonated drinks market, is another double-edged sword.

While it has driven up sales, the soaring cost of plastic, packaging, sugar and other raw material amid the global energy shock could start to hit margins, which are already very thin.

Raising prices, meanwhile, could affect volumes, forcing brands to further reduce bottle sizes, something the cola companies had resorted to a few years ago.

Then there is always the threat of bigger, deep-pocketed players coming in to disrupt the category.

"Competition is extremely aggressive," says Agarwal. And the established global players are better organised in terms of systems, processes, distribution channels and bottling arrangements.

Coca Cola, which had acquired the cumin-flavoured Rimzim brand in the 1990s, is strengthening its play in this category and reportedly doubled production last year.

But the challenges haven't deterred the ambitions of the homegrown players.

"Indian flavours need to find a global spotlight," says Munjal, who is looking to tap the global market in the coming years.

"For generations, we've grown up drinking colas of the world, but I hope the next generations will drink the zeeras of India."

#124. Iraq university’s removal of male professors teaching women sparks weeks of protests

Al-Qadisiyah University’s move a sign of the country’s slide into a Taliban-like gender segregation, say activists

The removal of male professors teaching female students at a university in southern Iraq has sparked protests and been criticised by activists as a Taliban-like gender segregation policy.

As many as 400 women are estimated to have joined protests over the past two weeks calling for the return of their professors at Al-Qadisiyah University in a move that appears to have surprised its administrators.

The university said the move was necessary to “prevent the mixing” of men and women and had been requested by the families of female students. But students said their families had known since before they enrolled that there were men on the teaching staff and supported their daughters’ protest.

Students said university bosses had mocked their protests and refused to discuss their concerns about the sudden loss of their academic supervisors. On social media, commentators also accused the women of seeking to keep male professors to “establish immoral relationships with them”.

“My classmates and I went to demonstrate, and the university president, Rahi al-Yasiri, blocked our path with his bodyguards, who then began making fun of us. He ordered them to take photographs of our faces, in an attempt to intimidate us and punish us later,” said Huda, 22, one of the female students.

Huda said the students tried to explain how men and women still worked together in society in Iraq. “Aren’t there doctors who treat both men and women? Why then do you prevent male professors from teaching us, too?” In response, Huda said Al-Yasiri replied: “Even if you hit your heads against the wall, I will not bring back the professors.”

The students said a large number of them were tied to graduation projects and research supervised by the professors. They said they had grown accustomed to their professors’ teaching methods, who also knew their students’ strengths and weaknesses, which made replacing them at this stage more complicated.

Female students said they had been told by the university that if they were not happy with the change, they should drop out.

An Iraqi-British feminist researcher, Balsam Mustafa, from the University of Cardiff, said the female students were “treated as if they are minors without the right to object to decisions affecting their academic lives”.

Mustafa said the removal of the male professors was a part of a worrying trend in women’s rights in the country, with “growing discussion about women’s clothing and chastity reflected in the celebration of female students who wear the abaya”.

An Iraqi lawyer and human rights activist, Israa Salman, said the restrictions on women was a “societal catastrophe”.

“Attempts are being made to suppress women’s talents and turn Iraqi society – a society that has produced remarkable minds and expertise, with women playing pioneering roles in many fields – into something resembling Kabul under Taliban rule.

“This contradicts Iraq’s constitution and the international treaties and conventions the country has signed and ratified,” said Salman.

#121. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#119. What is at stake at the EU-UK summit in Brussels?

Leaders will wrestle with easing Brexit red tape, restoring emissions trading and opening up youth mobility

The EU and UK will hold a long-awaited summit in Brussels on Friday – the second since Brexit – to hammer out the UK’s longer-term relationship with the bloc.

It will be attended by the prime minister, Andy Burnham, the head of the European Commission, Ursula von der Leyen, and the president of the European Council, António Costa.

The gathering, which follows up last year’s inaugural EU-UK summit in London, was scheduled for July but postponed when Keir Starmer resigned.

The two sides hope to announce three deals, which were initiated under Starmer’s government.

First, a food and drink agreement that will remove a lot of Brexit red tape on animal and plant products, making it easier to trade meats, cheese, wine and flowers. To secure the deal, which is not expected to take effect until 2028, the UK will align with EU standards, meaning the government, for example, must abandon a planned ban on foie gras imports.

A second agreement will link the British emissions trading system with its bigger EU cousin, restoring a link severed by Brexit. It is a significant step for industry because EU and UK businesses will be exempt from each other’s charges on high-carbon imports such as steel, cement and electricity. This agreement also requires alignment with relevant EU rules.

The third deal has proved the most contentious: a youth mobility scheme that will give those aged 18-31 the right to live, work, travel and study on both sides of the Channel for up to three years. The scheme may not be up and running until 2030 – which is bad news for anyone in their late 20s.

With less than 24 hours before the summit, the two sides were closing in on a deal. EU negotiators gave up a demand that EU students pay UK rates of university tuition, although they will get a discount on steep international rates. The UK also made a concession, agreeing to allow in 100,000 young EU citizens a year, having previously wanted fewer to keep the numbers down.

To maximise its leverage on tuition fees, the EU is linking the three agreements, warning they must come into force together.

Not on Friday. The UK has been pushing for inclusion in the EU’s Made in Europe plan, a protectionist scheme to favour EU companies when public money is being spent on goods such as electric cars, renewable energy and nuclear technology. Inclusion in Made in Europe is a big priority for the Burnham government, which has dangled major concessions on tuition fees to make sure British firms are not shut out.

The problem for London is that the EU lawmakers have not finalised the Made in Europe law, known as the Industrial Accelerator Act. Many EU member states want to include the UK, but France and the European parliament take a stricter position.

The EU law is not expected to be agreed until spring 2027. To help the British government, which has linked a generous offer on youth mobility with Made in Europe, the European Commission proposed delaying agreement on tuition fees by six months. But member states want precise guarantees now about equal treatment for EU students.

Another problem is that British inclusion in Made in Europe requires the UK to sign up to EU trade-defence policies. The EU, for instance, has imposed high tariffs on “unfairly subsidised” Chinese electric cars and fears the UK, which has not followed suit, could become an alternative route into the EU if Chinese companies set up in Britain and sell into the bloc. The EU is calling on the UK to align with its trade-defence policies, but the UK insists it can guard against circumvention without copying the EU.

In a sign of possible tensions, an earlier summit text proposing a trusted partnership on economic security has been downgraded to a partnership.

Beyond the headline deals, the EU-UK draft summit statement opens the door to British integration in European policies on energy, crime-fighting, educational exchange, tech and foreign policy cooperation.

The next major agreement, targeted for next year, is bringing the UK back into the EU’s internal electricity market. This is a priority for five EU member states: Germany, Belgium, the Netherlands, Denmark and Luxembourg. They argue that Britain’s inclusion in the EU electricity market is essential to take advantage of the nascent green power boom in the North Sea. In return for market access, the UK will be required to contribute to the EU budget to aid the development of poorer EU regions.

Beyond that, the two sides will “explore opportunities” for the UK to join the Erasmus student exchange programme after 2027; the current arrangement applies only for the 2027-8 academic year.

In a win for the UK, the EU appears ready to expand talks on the mutual recognition of professional qualifications, beyond existing negotiations on architects. The sides will sign a digital partnership, which includes joint work on AI safety, and examine how to share data on law enforcement and border management.

Far from it. A lot of diplomatic plates are spinning, but the UK remains outside the EU’s biggest economic clubs: the single market and the customs union. It also has no decision-making powers inside the EU.

Burnham says he will set out the long-term options for the future relationship after this week’s summit. “The hard Brexit we were given is not good enough for Britain,” he said on Monday in a major speech. But amid concerns among some cabinet ministers about reopening Brexit divides, the prime minister appears to be taking a more cautious approach. He said the process would take time and insisted that “at every stage the British people will be the boss”.

The EU has welcomed Britain’s interest in rejoining but is wary of a protracted negotiation that could be reversed by an anti-EU British government.

In an echo of the Brexit years, EU insiders say, once again, that it is up to Britain to define what it wants.

#118. Rohingya genocide: What was said in court and what happens now?

Myanmar has been found guilty of committing genocide against its Rohingya minority by the International Court of Justice (ICJ).

The verdict marks the first time a state has ever been found to have committed genocide by an international court.

The court found on Thursday that "genocidal intent is the only reasonable inference that can be drawn from the evidence" it had seen.

The verdict was reached by a majority of nine votes to six among a panel of judges from around the world.

The BBC's Anna Holligan is in the Hague and reports on what the ICJ's ruling means.

#116. From premature baby to social media star - Famed hippo Fiona dies

The Cincinnati Zoo in Ohio says its beloved hippopotamus Fiona has died just days after swallowing a knitted hat that slipped into her enclosure.

Fiona was born premature at the Cincinnati Zoo in 2017 and at first struggled to survive. At birth, she weighed 13 kg (29 lbs), which the zoo said was about half the previous lowest recorded birth weight for her species. She reached 1,000 lbs in 2018.

#115. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#111. New Ukraine talks including Europe to be held 'soon' — Merz

Germany's Chancellor Friedrich Merz says European leaders could take part in fresh Ukraine talks on "neutral ground." The UK and EU countries have urged a seat at the table in meetings between the US, Russia and Ukraine.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz said on Thursday he believes new talks regarding Russia's invasion of Ukraine with European leaders taking part will occur "soon."

"Of course we are prepared to hold talks with Russia, we have always said this. These talks are now set to take place soon," Merz said after a meeting of Ukraine's allies in Berlin. He did not give a concrete date for the next talks, but said Europe's interests would be represented by Germany, France, the UK, Poland and Italy.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy was present at the Coalition of the Willing summit in the German capital, along with British Prime Minister Andy Burnham, French President Emmanuel Macron, US President Donald Trump's son-in-law Jared Kushner and US Special Envoy Steve Witkoff.

Merz said the talks including Europe would take place on "neutral ground," such as the United Arab Emirates.

Merz along with other European leaders have been critical of meetings between US, Ukrainian and Russian representatives regarding the conflict, arguing that Europe needs a direct seat at the negotiating table.

Russia's invasion of Ukraine began in February 2022, with the Trump administration prioritizing an end to the war.

Trump took part in a bilateral meeting with Russian President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China on Thursday, with Trump saying the two leaders would discuss the war in closed-door talks. Putin acknowledged that Europeans want a seat at the table, and said Russia invited European leaders to Moscow.

"We understand that discussing issues linked to security in Europe is pointless without Europeans," Putin said, while rejecting any military cooperation between Europe and Ukraine.

Trump also took part in a historic trilateral meeting with Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping.

The Trump-Putin-Xi meeting raised fears that the US, Russia and China are looking to carve up the world into spheres of influence. It drew comparisons with the February 1945 Yalta Conference, where US President Franklin D. Roosevelt, UK Prime Minister Winston Churchill and Soviet Union leader Joseph Stalin discussed Europe's future as Nazi Germany's defeat was imminent in World War II.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Following the Trump-Xi-Putin summit, Merz said a "Yalta format cannot bring peace" to Ukraine. He downplayed the Shenzhen summit between the three leaders as a "photo opportunity."

Merz handed out pieces of the Berlin Wall to leaders at the Coalition of the Willing summit, saying the Yalta structure had brought "oppression, insecurity and a lack of peace."

"This is exactly what Yalta stood for," Merz said.

The Yalta format sought to divide post-war Germany into four zones: US, UK, France and the USSR. This later led to the construction of the Berlin Wall dividing the city into West and East Berlin, with city residents attempting to cross the barrier shot dead by border guards.

#109. Ukraine allies warn ‘Yalta format’ will not bring about peace – as it happened

Berlin meeting of the ‘coalition of the willing’ comes after Trump declared Putin a ‘friend’ during trilateral meeting with Xi in China

He says the leaders want “a peace for Ukraine that is both just and lasting,” with “more, not less, security for Europe and the world.”

“We will continue to support Kyiv for as long as is necessary. Should peace negotiations take place, we will be involved. Negotiations can only succeed if Ukraine and Europe are part of the process together. A “Yalta-style” format will not bring about peace.”

He says that as a gift he gave each of the guests a piece of the Berlin Wall, as an example of “what the Yalta arrangement represented for Berlin.”

“You may recall that in 1987, President Reagan stood here in Berlin and said: ‘Mr. Gorbachev, tear down this wall!’ Today, we say to President Putin: ‘end the war in Ukraine, and let us work together to find diplomatic solutions.’ We never again want to live in a divided Europe. We want to live in freedom and in peace – together.”

And, on that note, we will be closing the blog for today.

European leaders showed a united and defiant front as they repeated their backing for Ukraine “for as long as it takes” at the latest summit of the Coalition of the Willing in Berlin, declaring no decisions on Ukraine and Europe can be made without their participation (17:58, 18:06, 18:11).

Their comments appeared to play down the importance of Donald Trump’s summit with Russia’s Vladimir Putin and China’s Xi Jinping in Shenzhen (16:34), with German chancellor Friedrich Merz saying “a Yalta format will not bring about peace,” in a reference to secretive discussions between superpowers above the heads of those most affected (17:45, 17:50).

European leaders and US peace envoys insisted that some progress has been made in discussions on ending the Russian war of aggression in Ukraine, although they struck a cautious note about Putin’s intentions in the coming months (15:46, 18:38, 18:44).

US envoy Jared Kushner claimed that Russia’s leadership appears to be “comfortable” with the concept of Article 5-like security guarantees for Ukraine in any postwar settlement (18:29).

Meanwhile, Russia’s Putin struck an uncompromising tone at his press briefing in Shenzhen, vowing “painful” response to Ukraine’s attacks on Kerch bridge and defending his maximalist demands (13:09, 13:34).

I am also on Bluesky at @jakubkrupa.bsky.social and on X at @jakubkrupa.

Andy Burnham is meeting western leaders in Berlin and Brussels this week, to discuss Ukraine and closer ties to the EU. Meanwhile, Donald Trump, Vladimir Putin and Xi Jinping have met at the Apec summit in China.

Is this the start of a global shift of power away from Europe, or was it just a hyped-up photo opportunity?

Here is our latest “Politics Weekly” podcast on this if you want to catch up on today’s events:

Separately, Poland’s Donald Tusk also told reporters on his way back to Poland that Trump’s comments after his meeting with Putin and Xi sounded “quite optimistic.”

But Tusk quickly added that Trump’s “faith in Putin’s good will is probably unshakeable,” and he was a bit more sceptical about it.

“It would be good if indeed sooner or later it turned out that Russia is seriously ready to undertake peace talks,” he said.

“I don’t ‌think it’s likely ‌that Russia will sit down at the negotiating table in any serious ‌way before the winter is over.”

Zelenskyy has the last word, as he says that today’s meeting shows that “Russia did not get anywhere” with her attempt to “divide Europe.”

“A strong Europe is not only important for Ukraine, but for Europe itself,” he says.

The European leaders also get asked about Welt/Politico’s report earlier and the tone of Trump’s comments.

Burnham says that Trump’s comments focused on “horrific” loss of life during the war, saying “he spoke quite passionately about that subject.”

He stresses “nobody is winning this war,” and stresses that in his view there has been “huge progress” in now consensus that “there is no decision about Ukraine without Ukraine in the room and no decision about European security without Europe represented in the room.”

He says “there is a greater degree of alignment.”

“I would say for the first time in my period as prime minister of the United Kingdom, I see alignment; I see people saying the same thing; I see solidarity across the board in Europe; a strong, united European position.

And that gives some grounds for optimism that there can now be progress. But obviously, there is one person who can determine that more than anybody else, and that is president Putin.”

Witkoff jumps in there to say that Trump has “adamantly supported a peaceful end to this war,” with clear instructions to engage with the coalition of the willing and work in all formats that can be helpful to end the war.

“What I heard from him on the call today was that he mentioned these very same principles to the other two leaders who he was meeting with [Xi and Putin], so he made Ukraine, and a peaceful end to this war, a principal part of that trilateral today, and I think he’s to be commended for that.”

Macron gets asked about the Shenzhen talks and confirms that leaders were briefed on the discussions.

But he focuses on what’s ahead and stresses the need to get the sequence of agreements right to get a deal.

He also repeats his warnings that the war in Europe “threatens the security of Europe, and we have to be aware of that.”

Kushner says that the US managed to get Moscow comfortable with the concept that any security agreement for Ukraine would be based on Article 5-type arrangement, like in Nato.

(Not sure the Russians would say the same.)

He says “I know that there’s a lot of, scepticism and disbelief on that concept in Russia, but that’s why it’s important to restart the dialogue between Europe and Russia.”

“I will say from America’s perspective, giving an Article Five like security guarantee is a really, really big deal. And I think that that’s something that President Trump is open to. but obviously it has to be in America’s interest to do that and … we have to make sure it is part of a broader security package.”

Merz gets a question on potential multilateral talks with Ukraine, Russia, Europe and the US, and confirms that’s the plan and adds that finding the right place for it shouldn’t be too difficult.

He also goes back to his Yalta reference earlier, saying Europe was “parcelled up” by powers, and “we will not allow anything like that to happen again.”

Macron says the focus is on safeguarding Europe’s security.

He said that a peace declaration agreed by Donald Trump, Vladimir Putin and Xi Jinping in China “does not lead to a reorganisation of the world”.

“Today, if there is one critical factor in Europe’s reorganisation, its security, it is precisely the meeting that took place in Berlin,” he said.

He says the meeting was “very substantive and successful,” and “essential in helping us shape our point of views,” as he confirms that Trump dialled in for parts of the talks.

He pointedly praises Italy’s Meloni, Poland’s Tusk, European Commission’s von der Leyen, Nato’s Rutte, Finnish president and Norwegian PMs, Stubb and Gahr Støre.

He says Europe is “very united, and very, very strong in their support of Ukraine,” which he and Witkoff “relayed to president Trump.”

He says, without revealing details, that Trump came away from the trilateral China meeting thinking “peace is achievable”.

“For us, success in an outcome would be to have a sovereign, secure, prosperous and free Ukraine at the end of the war,” he says.

He says the talks have been making “a lot of progress” on postwar prosperity plan, security guarantees, and a plan to exchange prisoners of war.

On postwar security, he says that “Nato will be a central pillar” of proposals, with the right structures to avoid accidental escalations.

Kushner also says that Russians appear to be “willing to trust” the US suggestion that Europeans need to be included in the process to make sure that whatever agreement is struck will be respected.

The US envoys will be meeting with national security advisers next to agree on the detail of their proposals.

“President Trump wants to make a deal with Europe by his side,” he insists..

“Our goal is really to end this war and to really help build a durable peace. We are very committed. President Trump, will do whatever it takes to do that. He’s instructed us to commit as much time as possible, and we will continue to do that in full coordination, and unity with our European partners.”

He says today’s meeting “has put on a huge show of support” for Kyiv, and “the mood was stronger, more united, more determined.”

“Europe has a clear and consistent and united negotiating position. It’s not for the aggressor in this conflict to set out wide ranging and unrealistic security demands. That much we are all absolutely clear about.”

He says the coalition is ready for discussions on European security, but only “if there is a peace agreement between Ukraine and Russia.”

He says the UK’s support for Ukraine will continue for “as long as it takes.”

“Europe has a clear and consistent and united negotiating position. It’s not for the aggressor in this conflict to set out wide ranging and unrealistic security demands. That much we are all absolutely clear about.

And I hope that message is heard very clearly, in Moscow: for as long as it takes.”

“If we want to be credible in peace negotiations, then it is important that Ukraine defends itself and goes into these negotiations in a good position, and Ukraine can only do so if we continue supporting Ukraine.”

“Everyone who is here today have to do everything in their power so that Ukraine has everything it needs in order to resist and to defend herself …”

He says that faced with Russia’s maximalist demands, it’s time it is “the right point in time to discuss [Ukraine’s] Nato membership.”

Commenting on the current state of talks, he says Russia “did not submit any concrete and tangible proposals for peace.”

He acknowledges that “the coming weeks will be critical” for Ukraine, and so the coalition gathered at the right moment.

He says there was every attempt made to “make this meeting as concrete as possible.”

He thanks all partners for helping Ukraine and highlights “active efforts to ensure that we stand firm.”

He thanks the EU for its “very substantial” proposed loan of £45bn.

He also highlights some of the priority areas for Ukraine, including the move to stop daily drone attacks (which he confirms hit the Austrian and the Dutch embassies), and deploy more air defence systems.

He also says the next round of talks should include European leaders, and highlights the importance of security guarantees and Ukraine’s future membership in the EU.
