#108. Kuwait says it repelled first Iranian attack in two months after US strikes island

Kuwait condemned the attack, which Iran said targeted an airbase hosting US forces (file photo)

Kuwait and Bahrain have said they repelled Iranian air attacks overnight as hostilities between Iran and the United States rise.

The drone attack on Kuwait was the first in more than two months. The Iranian army said it had targeted the Ali Al Salem Airbase, which hosts US forces, but Kuwaiti authorities did not report any damage.

It came after US warplanes struck an Iranian missile launch site on Qeshm Island in the Strait of Hormuz, according to US military officials.

Meanwhile, the United Arab Emirates' state oil firm said a crew member was missing after one of its tankers was attacked in the Gulf waterway on Tuesday.

The recent exchanges of fire, as well as deadlock in negotiations on a peace deal, have raised fears of a major escalation in the nine-month-old war.

The conflict began when the US and Israel attacked Iran in late February.

Since then, Iran has retaliated for US and Israeli strikes by launching missiles and drones at Israel and Gulf Arab states hosting US military assets and personnel.

It has also attacked dozens of commercial vessels in and around the Strait of Hormuz, through which a fifth of global oil and gas shipments usually pass.

Pakistani man sentenced to 27 years for Iran-backed plot to kill Trump

Trump says US taking 'very seriously' any Iranian plot against Washington

Iran targets US military base in Bahrain with drone attack

On Tuesday night, Kuwait's military said its air defences engaged "drone threats" but did not report any casualties or damage.

The Kuwaiti foreign ministry condemned the attack, saying it "flagrantly violated Kuwait's sovereignty, directly threatened its security and stability, and constituted a grave breach of international law".

"The explosions seemed to be coming from far away, but hearing them again after a period of calm was both surprising and worrying," Hani Mohammed, an Egyptian resident of Kuwait, told AFP news agency.

Bahrain's military also said on Wednesday that its air defence systems had "intercepted and destroyed several Iranian aerial attacks".

The island kingdom, which hosts the US Navy's 5th Fleet, has reported four attacks over the past six days, following a two-month lull.

Qatar, which has mediated in the stalled indirect talks between Iran and the US, said the Iranian attacks on its neighbours were a "dangerous escalation that threatens to complicate efforts aimed at containing tensions".

The Iranian army said its drones had targeted US Patriot air defence systems and drone and missile launch platforms at Ali Al Salem Air Base in Kuwait.

Later, the army's commander-in-chief, Maj Gen Amir Hatami, warned the US and its allies that it would respond more forcefully to any new attacks on Iran.

"If the enemy repeats its mistake... we have the readiness and the plans to confront it and its supporters in a stronger, more crushing way and without geographic limitations," he said.

On Tuesday night, US fighter jets targeted a missile launch site on Qeshm Island "with precision munitions" in response to Iranian "aggression targeting US personnel and assets", US military officials said.

Iranian media reported explosions on the island but cited a local official as saying they had caused no damage to infrastructure.

In a separate development, the Abu Dhabi National Oil Company (Adnoc) said a search-and-rescue operation was under way for a missing sailor following an attack on one of its vessels in the Strait of Hormuz on Tuesday. The rest of the crew were safely evacuated and 11 were receiving medical care, it added.

The UK Maritime Trade Operations (UKMTO) centre reported that a tanker was struck by an unknown projectile about 13 nautical miles (24km) north of Khasab, in Oman's Musandam peninsula. It said the attack caused a fire on board that was extinguished.

Iran said two oil tankers and a ship carrying liquefied natural gas (LNG) had caught fire after hitting naval mines while sailing along an "authorised route".

At least 25 seafarers have been killed across the Middle East since the start of the war, when the Strait of Hormuz was effectively closed due to Iranian missile and drone attacks on commercial vessels and a US naval blockade of Iranian ports.

The US and Iran reached a preliminary agreement to end the conflict and reopen the strait in June, but it collapsed within weeks after Iranian attacks on shipping resumed and the US reinstated its blockade.

Iran insisted it would not give up some measure of control over the waterway and began targeting vessels using a southern route through Omani waters that avoids its preferred route through Iranian waters.

In recent months, the US military has been providing protection for tankers shuttling through the Strait of Hormuz, allowing crude oil exports from Gulf states to reach around 80% of pre-war levels, according to analysts.

#106. White House replaces cyber security chief after tech outages

Sean Cairncross is leaving his role as national cyber director after a chaotic week of tech outages. Kirsten Davies, the Pentagon's chief information officer, will assume the position in an acting capacity.

Sean Cairncross is exiting his post as White House national cyber director after a series of technology-related outages in the United States over the week led to disruptions in air travel, banking and national security networks.

Shortly after Axios and other US news outlets first reported Cairncross' departure on Friday, Secretary of State Marco Rubio announced the Pentagon's Chief Information Officer, Kirsten Davies, as the new cyber director, congratulating her on X.

"Look forward to working together to strengthen America's cybersecurity," Rubio said.

Davies will assume the position in an acting capacity, media outlets reported citing White House officials.

"Our nation stands in the crossroad of analog, digital, and quantum technologies, our American way of living increasingly interconnected," Davies wrote on X late Thursday night, as she thanked US President Donald Trump and White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller for their "faith and steady confidence" in her.

"Our opportunity is to dominate the cyber landscape for generations to come …but only if we modernize and drive resilience into the foundations our great nation runs on, as well as build cyber talent for our future."

US Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth referred to Davies' tenure at the Pentagon as one in which "she has proven herself to be a dedicated patriot and professional."

"We are confident she will advance President Trump's America First agenda across the cybersecurity landscape," the Pentagon chief said.

The Trump administration came under scrutiny in recent days after the US witnessed a major tech meltdown as service outages affected not just travel, communications, and e-commerce, but also the National Security Agency.

Top US officials have since sought to assure people in the country that there was no evidence of a cyberattack.

In 2025, Trump nominated Cairncross to serve as his top adviser on cybersecurity and data protection policy. He was subsequently confirmed to the post by the Senate.

During Trump's first stint in the Oval Office, Cairncross took on various roles as a White House insider. He was ⁠also an executive at ​the Republican National Committee before becoming the cybersecurity chief.

#104. UK and EU plan first talks on sending refused asylum seekers to third countries

Two sides to commit to discussing third-country hubs as part of reset pact after five-country deal with Rwanda

The UK and the EU plan to establish formal talks for the first time about sending rejected asylum seekers to third-country hubs, according to a leaked document.

A draft of a joint statement expected to be signed by Andy Burnham and Ursula von der Leyen at a summit on Friday morning states that both sides will establish “a structured dialogue” to discuss “returns to third countries”.

Until now, the Home Office has been seeking bilateral agreements with third countries allowing the removal of migrants, but with no obvious success.

Last week, five EU countries – Austria, Denmark, Germany, Greece and the Netherlands – announced an agreement in principle with Rwanda to establish a migrant “return hub” there in 2027.

Burnham hinted at possible cooperation with EU countries last week when he told reporters that the UK had not had direct discussions with European governments involved in return hubs but “we will watch that [the five-country deal] with interest, learning from what went on in the past”. He said: “The public want us to do whatever we can to deal with illegal immigration.”

The UK has been fighting for deeper cooperation with the EU on migration, with the new deal on a “strategic dialogue” secured in the last two weeks by negotiators in Brussels.

It is a key part of the UK-EU reset pact, which also covers a youth mobility scheme, tariffs on Chinese imports, food and drink exports to the EU and a wider restatement of commitment on defence and security.

Informed sources confirmed this was the first time the UK and the EU had agreed to “open a dialogue on returns” after the launch of the bloc’s Channel action plan in June this year.

Whitehall sources said the UK was now open to the possibility of sending rejected claimants to Rwanda – a recent pivot from the position under Keir Starmer’s government, which rejected the move.

According to a draft of the joint agreement, negotiations over third-country hubs will be part of increased cooperation to tackle irregular migration.

The document states: “The EU and UK … will establish a structured dialogue on migration covering in particular the joint activities under the Channel action plan, action against irregular migration and migrant smuggling networks, strategic communication and border management, upstream cooperation and engagement with third countries, returns to third countries, pull factors for irregular migrants and legal pathway.”

In May, the UK and 45 other European countries signed an agreement that explicitly endorsed plans to send unwanted asylum seekers to third-country hubs.

A political declaration from the 46 members of the Council of Europe, the body that oversees the European convention on human rights (ECHR), said states had an “undeniable sovereign right” to control their borders.

It is understood that the UK has been seeking a deal with an unnamed third country, similar to the Italy-Albania agreement that allowed Rome to place detention centres in Albania. Albania has ruled out a bilateral agreement on a return hub with the UK.

Previous discussions have reportedly centred on 11 countries: Armenia, Egypt, Ethiopia, Ghana, Libya, Mauritania, Rwanda, Senegal, Tunisia, Uganda and Uzbekistan. Montenegro has denied reports that it is considering housing refused asylum seekers.

Starmer scrapped a Tory plan to forcibly send small-boat arrivals to Rwanda where their asylum claims would have been processed.

Before the election, the Conservative government had spent £700m on its Rwanda returns policy, which was meant to deter Channel crossings. The scheme hit legal and political obstacles from the start, with the UK supreme court eventually ruling it illegal.

Under Burnham, the Home Office has refused to close down the possibility of sending asylum seekers to Rwanda. This week, when asked if the UK might ever send asylum seekers to Rwanda, a Home Office spokesperson said: “The UK, like other European countries, is exploring innovative approaches including returns hubs to tackle illegal migration.”

The UK has been looking for years to strengthen cooperation with France and other EU member states to tackle small boats carrying asylum seekers across the Channel.

But sources say it is only in recent months that there has been a change of mood in the EU, with member states realising that the intensity of criminal trafficking across the Channel is “proving [to be] a pull factor” to enter the EU. Those that fail to make it to British shores remain in the EU’s migrant support system.

Responding to the planned cooperation on migrant hubs, Madeleine Sumption, the head of the Migration Observatory at Oxford University, said: “Its inclusion in the statement would confirm the now relatively widespread interest in ‘third country’ options.”

In response, Imran Hussain, the director of external affairs at the Refugee Council, said return hubs were likely to be difficult and costly to run.

“Removing people to places they have never stepped foot will be difficult and distressing,” he said.

“The last decade has shown that working with people to support their return to their countries is far more successful.”

A Home Office spokesperson said: “While we are not part of this EU member state initiative, there is widespread international agreement on the need to have practical and workable solutions to deal with the global challenge of illegal migration.

“We continue to explore innovative approaches, including returns hubs, to build on the 80,000 people with no right to be in the UK returned or deported since July 2024. We have consistently made clear that we support the principle of returns hubs.”

#102. William and Kate meet PM Modi as they wrap up India tour

The Prince and Princess of Wales have met India's prime minister as they ended their three-day visit to the country.

Narendra Modi thanked Prince William for bringing his Earthshot prize to the country during the couple's first international tour since 2022.

It comes a day after the royal couple gave out Â£1m prizes to winners of the environmental award, which was founded by the prince and remains one of his biggest personal projects.

Catherine also visited a maternity hospital, where she comforted a grieving mother who lost a baby after giving birth to premature twin girls.

Street cricket with a legend then metro ride as William and Kate begin India visit

On Wednesday, William and Catherine travelled from Mumbai to capital New Delhi for the final leg of their trip.

During a meeting at Modi's private residence, a Kensington Palace spokesperson said the royals and Modi discussed the "strength and importance of the UKâIndia bilateral relationship, reflecting on the deep and enduring ties between the two countries and the opportunities to strengthen their partnership in the years ahead".

The prince and princess also spoke "warmly" of their time in the country, thanking the prime minister and the people for their welcome, and saying how much they enjoyed returning to India, a decade after their last visit.

Modi called it a "pleasure" to meet them, and said he looks forward to "further strengthening" the UKâIndia partnership for the "shared prosperity of the people of our nations and the wider world".

When Prince William announced earlier this year that The Earthshot Prize ceremony was going to be held in India for the first time, he highlighted India's leadership in climate innovation, its biodiversity and its massive youth population as the key reasons for choosing the country.

The royal spokesperson said that the meeting with Modi touched on the success of the ceremony in Mumbai, and India's "growing leadership in environmental innovation".

The event, founded by Prince William in 2020, was hosted this year by Karan Johar, presenter of The Traitors India and one of the country's most well-known filmmakers.

The couple last met the Indian prime minister on their official visit to India in 2016.

Watch: Catherine visited a maternity hospital in Mumbai on the last day of royal visit

Ahead of their meeting with Modi on Wednesday, William met with film stars during a visit to a film production company, while Catherine visited a maternity hospital, where she met grieving mother Jyoti Karande.

Karande, whose other child remains in intensive care, is receiving treatment in the post-partum ward at Nowrosjee Wadia Maternity Hospital.

Catherine told the 29-year-old: "Stay strong. Well done. You've clearly been very, very brave."

William and Catherine's first official overseas trip in four years was short, but allowed them to combine personal projects - like Earthshot - with diplomacy.

As the future king and queen, they are viewed as a valuable diplomatic asset by the UK government.

And with Catherine recovering well and back on international duties following her cancer treatment, visits of a similar kind can be expected to fill their calendar.

#99. Parents jailed for torture and death of four-year-old girl found along French motorway in 1987

The mother of a four-year-old girl killed in central France and found along a motorway almost 40 years ago has been sentenced to 20 years in prison.

Halima El Bakhti, 72, was found guilty on Tuesday of "torture and acts of barbarity resulting in death without the intent to kill", French media reports.

The girl's father, 74-year-old Ahmed Touloub, was handed a 15-year sentence as an accomplice.

Four-year-old Inass Touloub was found along the A10 road in SuÃ¨vres in the Loir-et-Cher department on 11 August 1987. Her body bore multiple marks of abuse, including human bite marks, broken bones and burns with an iron.

She was buried in an unmarked grave near the location where her body was found. Unidentified for three decades, she was known only as the "Little Martyr of the A10".

Then, in 2018, she was identified after the DNA of her brother â tested in an unrelated case â matched with hers, leading to the arrest of their parents.

The trial took place in Loir-et-Cher Assize Court from 2 November, with the verdict delivered on Tuesday after several hours of deliberation.

During the trial, prosecutor AnaÃ¯s Allio emphasised that "not a single part of Inass's body was spared", and recalled the "sense of vertigo that overcame us all" upon viewing the "unbearable photographs" from the autopsy, France Info reports.

The jury concluded that the child's mother was suffering from impaired judgement at the time of the girl's death, which reduced the maximum potential sentence.

French media outlet France Info described the two defendants as sitting stoically in chairs before the court with their heads bowed as the reasoning behind the verdict was read out.

El Bakhti and Touloub have 10 days to appeal the verdict.

Inass's death sparked one of France's biggest ever investigations, with the girl's photograph posted in public places and an alert sent to more than 30 countries.

#98. UK-EU reset talks will go ‘down to the wire’ as crunch summit approaches

Britain makes concessions on youth mobility scheme as negotiations on food and farming go down to the wire

British and European negotiators were closing in on a reset deal on Thursday night as they provisionally agreed to allow 100,000 EU citizens to come to the UK every year under a new mobility scheme.

It is understood the EU dropped its demands at the last minute that EU students would pay home tuition fees if they attended British universities in a last minute concession to get the deal over the line.

The UK had strongly resisted EU text on the table committing it to home fees instead of “significantly reduced” international fees, but a compromise was hammered out before 10pm, with a “supplementary agreement” to work out the details within the next six months.

As talks went into the night, EU sources suggested both sides were on track for an agreement on Friday.

The EU-UK reset talks went down “down to the wire”, sources said, as both sides battled over the nature of the UK’s role in shaping future European food and farming rules.

But on the eve of a summit in Brussels, the EU was “going into a landing zone” on the question of a British voice in shaping the rules under a food and drink agreement the government hopes to unveil on Friday, an EU source said.

There also seemed to be a compromise by France after a dispute among member states over how far Britain could be involved in shaping policies related to the food and drink deal.

The UK had demanded a “voting” role on some law-making in relation to food standards but France heavily opposed, and London eventually conceded on a point many think was unrealistic from the start.

Instead, the UK will be consulted on new food laws, but the details on the level of involvement remain to be seen.

Earlier on Thursday the UK made concessions to increase the quota of EU citizens who could take part in a new youth mobility scheme, agreeing a cap of 100,000, instead of 50,000-80,0000 as previously sought.

It was also agreed to raise the age cap from 30 to 31.

The number of young British people allowed to go to the EU under the scheme will be matched but could be higher, with some countries, including Germany, being prepared to take unlimited numbers each year.

With less than 24 hours to go before Andy Burnham was due in Brussels for the summit with EU leaders, the two sides still needed to resolve some sticking points.

Speaking in general terms about the disagreements, another EU diplomat said: “It will go down to the wire. Our feeling is that the Brits have to move if they want a deal.”

A spokesperson for the British prime minister also described the talks as close, saying if an agreement was reached, the government would bring forward legislation, the cutting costs bill, to parliament next week.

At the long-awaited summit, the EU and UK hope to sign three agreements, which are expected to add 0.4% to British GDP, including the highly prized food and drink deal that was a Labour manifesto commitment.

The deal, expected to come into force on 1 March 2028, requires the UK to align with EU rules on sanitary and phytosanitary (SPS) standards, making Britain a rule taker.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Asked about Britain’s status as a rule taker, No 10 said the Labour manifesto had aimed to build closer ties with the EU, adding: “We were obviously elected on a manifesto that promised to reset the relationship and seek to deepen ties with our European friends.”

According to EU sources, the UK has been pushing to have a formal role in EU committees involved in implementing existing and setting new SPS rules.

An EU diplomat said: “They want to be able to shape decisions, not only implementation, but future decisions. That has always been a red line.”

Under the SPS proposal, the UK will agree to dynamically align with EU laws and will have a “voice” but “not be in the room” when laws are drafted.

A second diplomat from a country sympathetic to the British position said the European Commission had proposed an “extremely restrictive consultation mechanism” for the UK.

In contrast, the EU diplomat thought it was reasonable for a British representative to take part in the implementation of SPS legislation affecting the UK.

“Of course the UK cannot vote in the council as a non-member state, but we feel that we need to be pragmatic about coordination and implementation,” they said.

The issue proved difficult to resolve because it divided member states. France was perceived by other member states to be the main barrier to greater UK involvement in EU working groups on food and drink laws. “They are super principled on that point,” said a second EU diplomat.

The French government does not agree that it has been the main block, arguing that only a few member states wanted to accommodate the UK.

The issue raises the tensions at the heart of the UK’s status as a rule taker, and would be many times magnified were Britain to choose to enter the single market as a non-EU member state.

The precise details of how the UK would be consulted on future food and farming rules remain unclear.

Burnham’s spokesperson suggested there was a UK-EU consensus that there would be no further incremental reset summits beyond the one on Friday until a full negotiation commenced on a longer-term, more stable relationship, such as rejoining the single market.

“After the summit, we will begin those discussions with our European partners about the realistic options for our longer-term relationship,” he said.

The UK has prioritised seeking commitments on Britain’s position relating to the EU’s Made in Europe legislation. The trade defence policy will include tariffs on Chinese electric vehicles but will also affect British manufacturing. The sides are believed to be close to an agreement to language at the summit that would point towards UK inclusion, but nothing can be formalised until the EU passes its own legislation.

Burnham’s spokesperson said: “[The UK is] engaging constructively with the EU on that, and we’ve been clear about the challenges it could represent for British industries. We have deeply integrated supply chains with Europe, and we want to keep working together to boost growth and open up trade.

“The UK and Europe have shared economic challenges, and we believe shared challenges are best met with a collective approach that is in Britain’s best interests, and in Europe’s too.”

#96. Russian arrested for spying on US defence company Palantir over Ukraine support

The alleged foreign agent was discovered with two Russian passports and a forged American passport

A Russian man has been arrested in California's Silicon Valley for allegedly attempting to spy on employees of a technology and defence firm that supports the Ukrainian military.

Igor D'iu is accused of acting as an agent of a foreign government to conduct a covert operation in Palo Alto, near San Francisco. The BBC understands that the company targeted was Palantir Technologies.

D'iu is also charged with stalking employees and possessing falsified identity documents.

The company is not named in charging documents, but is described as a publicly traded technology and defence company that produces software used by Ukraine in its war with Russia.

"The allegations in this case describe a state-sponsored operation to target and harass employees of an American technology and defense company because of that company's assistance to Ukraine in its war with Russia," said US Attorney for the Northern District of California Craig Missakian.

"We will not tolerate any efforts - by foreign governments or otherwise - to turn Silicon Valley into a base for foreign intelligence operations."

Prosecutors say that D'iu entered the US unlawfully via the Mexico border, after travelling there from Istanbul in June.

He was allegedly recruited through the Telegram encrypted messaging app. His handlers referred to Russia as "'the Motherland' and directed D'iu to gather information about the company because it was supplying weapons used against Russia in Ukraine".

On their instruction, he purchased computers, phones, wireless networking equipment and remote access software - tools he needed for his spy mission.

He then went to a coffee shop located near the company's offices in Palo Alto, where he photographed laptop screens of employees who were working there.

His handlers also allegedly "instructed him to position his laptop at designated locations near the company so they could remotely access it and attempt to collect information from company employees in the vicinity".

The suspect allegedly took photos of employees screens at a cafe near the office

A source in the industry confirmed that Palantir was targeted, and added that the operation did not penetrate the company's private systems.

They added that the company has been "targeted extensively" by Russia over its involvement in Ukraine, and that the attacks are becoming "increasingly aggressive".

The source, who did not want to be named, said that this was the first time a Russian operative was found while on US soil attempting to spy on the company.

"I think the surprising thing is that they decided to take the risk of sending someone to the US," they said, in an attempt to get into the system "physically".

Palantir has not issued a statement in reaction to the charges.

D'iu also allegedly conducted surveillance of employees and their residences.

On 3 September, he followed a vehicle driven by one senior executive for nearly 30 miles. He fled after she drove to a police station. Police later obtained evidence that he had followed other employees' cars on previous days.

During the investigation, officials discovered identity cards that had been falsified - including a US passport, California ID card, and a Social Security card with the fake name "Hugo Landry Johns".

A raid on his home in October in East Palo Alto turned up a handwritten notebook that contained numerous home addresses of employees "family members' names, educational and employment histories, and assessments of their strengths and weaknesses", prosecutors say. Two Russian passports were also found.

He faces two charges related his alleged work as a foreign agent, one charge of interstate stalking, and one count of fraud related to his identity documents. He faces over 15 years in prison if found guilty.

The suspect was found with forged identification documents including a US passport

Ukraine's long-range drones using Western tech to hit Russia

He has been held in jail since 2 November. It is unclear whether he has hired a lawyer who can speak on his behalf.

Palantir, which was co-founded by billionaire Peter Thiel, has interests in US defence and security, and has contracts with the Israeli military.

The arrest comes as espionage allegations roil the US tech industry, amid the news that Nvidia ex-CEO Jensen Huang had a romantic affair with a woman suspected of spying for China.

Last month, a former OpenAI employee was charged with planting a listening device in a company office. The man was later accused of having "considerable support" from the Saudi government.

#93. Religion should be removed from terrorism definition under Australian law as it gives ‘false credibility’, watchdog says

Independent National Security Legislation Monitor says reference to religion has ‘contributed to discrimination and violence against religious minorities’

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Religion should be removed from Australia’s terrorism definition and making bomb threats should not result in life imprisonment, the country’s national security laws watchdog has said.

The highly anticipated report from the Independent National Security Legislation Monitor (INSLM), released Thursday, reviewed how terrorism has been defined in Australia over the past 25 years after a suite of laws were introduced following the September 11 attacks in the United States.

It comes as the Australian Federal Police (AFP) and the Australian Security and Intelligence Organisation (Asio) warn the rise of extremist, violent nationalism has changed the threat landscape and the motivations behind some terrorist attacks cannot be categorised into single ideologies.

Among the recommendations are suggested changes to the definition of terrorism to remove religion, which the INSLM head, Jake Blight, said gives accused terrorists “false credibility” and has a detrimental impact on religious minorities.

“Having a law that requires proof that terrorists were advancing a ‘religious’ cause gives these terrorists false credibility and reinforces their narrative that they are doing holy work,” the report said. “The same individuals will still be able to be prosecuted, but should be described as violent ideologues.

“Reference to religion in the definition of terrorism has also, in practice, contributed to discrimination and violence against religious minorities, especially Muslim Australians. This has a negative impact on social cohesion.”

The current definition of terrorism under the federal criminal code requires agencies to prove an act intends to cause serious harm to people or property in order to advance a political, religious or ideological cause.

A change in terrorism laws to remove religion as a primary motivation and place it as an example of a broader “ideology” category is in line with international best practice, Blight’s report said.

The INSLM noted the UN special rapporteur’s report earlier this year recommended defining terrorism acts by political and ideological causes and omitting religious motives, as references to religion have “discriminatory and stigmatizing effects that outweigh the benefits of criminally denouncing them”.

In his submission to the INSLM, Australia’s special envoy to combat Islamophobia, Aftab Malik, said keeping religion in as a terrorism motivation created a “climate of suspicion” around Muslims and “provided ammunition for far-right extremist narratives”.

Eleven Muslim advocacy and community groups, including the Australian Muslim Advocacy Network, welcomed the report’s recommendations and urged the Australian government to amend the laws.

“Every time someone in authority says that a motivation is ‘religious’, it uplifts ISIS, far-right and harmful media narratives all at once, by officially perpetuating the false premise that Islam encourages terrorism,” their joint statement said.

“This makes all Muslims feel under siege.”

The Executive Council of Australian Jewry, Australia’s peak Jewish body, described the report as “high-quality” but said removing religion as a motivating factor made “little sense when so many notorious terrorist acts have featured motifs, words and images that unmistakably refer to religious doctrines and beliefs”.

“The inclusion of ‘faith’ as a type of ideology does not solve the problem. Faith is not the same as religion and religion is more than an ideology,” the organisation’s co-chief executive, Peter Wertheim, said.

“It is clearly not appropriate for Australian law to decide which beliefs are an authentic expression of a religion and which are not. Even the adherents of a religion may have divergent views about such matters.”

The INSLM’s report made six other recommendations, including separating credible threats of terrorism from actual acts.

Under existing laws, a person found guilty of making a credible terrorist threat can be sentenced to life imprisonment with a mandatory minimum sentence of six years.

The report said the punishment was “disproportionate, unnecessarily complex and out of step with international standards” and recommended terrorism threats be treated as a separate charge with a maximum sentence of no more than five years.

The legislation monitor also recommended raising the bar on designating damage to property and electronic system as acts of terrorism while excluding advocacy, protest, dissent and industrial action.

Asio’s director-general, Mike Burgess, has warned of the rise in violent nationalist extremism in recent years. In an October submission to a parliamentary inquiry into radicalisation, Asio said around 65% of its counter-terrorism caseload related to religiously-motivated violent extremism while 35% related to ideologically-motivated extremism.

#91. Elon Musk says record $446m spending on US midterms was 'pretty foolish'

Elon Musk said on Wednesday that he felt "pretty foolish" about the record sum he spent on the US midterm elections, following sweeping Republican defeats.

The Tesla and SpaceX chief executive was the election's top individual donor, giving $446m (Â£339m) to support President Donald Trump's party, particularly in Texas where many of Musk's firms are based.

Musk, who had backed Trump's second White House bid, also said he was "not Maga" - the Make America Great Again movement associated with the president.

Democrats won control of both chambers in the US Congress, flipping both swing seats and making historic gains in places that have for decades been controlled by Republicans.

The election was seen as a bruising defeat for Trump, who has faced pressure over the impact his policies have had on the cost of living in the US.

Musk was speaking virtually, external at the New York Times DealBook Summit, an annual conference hosted in the city attended by major business and political leaders.

"I'm not Maga," Musk said, referring to the right-wing political movement.

He added: "I'm not sure what Maga means... if that means sort of blind adherence to a set of policies by the Republican Party, then I certainly don't have that."

This marks a major shift in tone by Musk, who has previously been seen wearing a Maga hat.

"I'm not just Maga, I'm dark, gothic Maga," he joked at a Trump rally in 2024.

Musk has worn a Maga hat in public on several occasions

For several months at the start of Trump's second term in the White House, Musk served as an unpaid "special government employee" and Senior Advisor to the President, leading the Department of Government Efficiency (Doge) to cut government spending.

In October 2026, the Pentagon named Musk as a co-lead of a future of warfare study called Project Meridian, while he continued to travel with and advise Trump.

Musk went on to say he did not see much for the money he had spent on the Republicans' campaign, though he added that it was likely that candidates would have fared worse without the spending.

The large number of Republican losses could have implications for Musk and his businesses.

Democrats coming into power in both the Senate and House of Representatives have pledged greater scrutiny of tech companies, including over the building of controversial data centres.

In Georgia, where SpaceX has data centre operations, the newly elected Democrat governor promised to limit the rollout of the massive buildings, as well as the tax breaks tech companies often get to build them.

Democratic lawmakers have also suggested that SpaceX, which has gained an estimated $10bn in new government contracts since Trump was re-elected, should be investigated.

According to BBC analysis, Musk had spent at least $45m in support of Paxton.

Musk said on Wednesday that he believes Texas' new governor, Democrat Gina Hinojosa, is "going to be quite moderate".

Hinojosa defeated Republican Governor Greg Abbott, ending more than three decades of the party's control of the state.

The Tesla boss added : "I don't think she's a far-left governor. And based on everything I've heard about her, I think things will be fine in Texas with Gina as governor."

#90. ‘Perfect weapon’: diabetic woman found guilty of murdering her 94-year-old parents with insulin

Jury convicts Raelene Polymiadis after four-month trial in South Australian supreme court

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A jury has convicted a diabetic woman of murdering both her elderly parents with insulin after a marathon criminal trial.

Raelene Polymiadis, 66, will face two mandatory life sentences after jurors on Friday delivered unanimous verdicts after a four-month South Australian supreme court trial.

She clutched the dock as the first verdict was read out, then dabbed at her eyes with a tissue after the second guilty verdict was announced, repeatedly shaking her head and blinking rapidly.

Polymiadis’s bail was revoked and she was remanded in custody ahead of sentence.

After a trial that began on June 15 and blew through its 12-week estimate due to illnesses and other delays, the jury needed less than five hours before returning verdicts on Friday morning.

They accepted Polymiaidis, who has type 1 diabetes, injected Brenda and Lynton Anderson with fatal doses of insulin in separate incidents in March 2022 and April 2023 respectively. Both were aged 94 at the time of their deaths.

Each had faced declining health and both were strongly opposed to being moved into aged care. One potential motive raised by prosecutors was a misplaced idea that killing her parents would spare them from entering a nursing home.

After Brenda had a fall in February 2022, she was admitted to Noarlunga hospital with a fractured vertebra, complicated by pneumonia and another fall.

She had been recovering and had a discharge plan in place, but after Polymiadis visited her mother on March 12, nursing staff found Brenda “largely unresponsive”.

The prosecution alleged Polymiadis administered insulin to her mother on two occasions – on 12 March and between 13 March and 15 March.

Lynton continued to live at home after his wife’s death, but became more frail and his four children began staying there at night on a shift basis.

Polymiadis left the house on 30 April, 2023, after staying with her father for the weekend.

Two hours later, a carer found Lynton unconscious. He died on 1 May as a result of insulin toxicity.

Polymiadis’s former boyfriend Ian Edward Willcourt told the court she had told him that “insulin was the perfect murder weapon because it’s natural in the body and it can’t be detected”.

In other key evidence, a police listening device captured Polymiadis alone in her car allegedly confessing to God that she had killed her parents.

Police also seized notes written by Polymiadis, including one which read: “What they will do is wait for a few weeks, months, year to see if I ‘think I got away with it’ and I get cocky and slip up.

“Hang on buddy, it could be a long ride, otherwise the suspect would be arrested now.”

Polymiadis’ husband, Michael, and her son, who had accompanied his mother to court throughout the trial, did not comment as they left the courthouse.

Justice Sandi McDonald thanked the jury for their service during the “long and difficult trial”.

Polymiadis will reappear in court on 16 December for a directions hearing.

#89. Israeli military completes ground operation in Gaza beyond Yellow Line

Residents fled the area near Nuseirat and Bureij refugee camps following the Israeli evacuation order

Israeli forces have completed a rare ground operation in part of central Gaza outside their control, the military says, a day after residents were ordered to evacuate.

Families fled their homes and tents on Wednesday as tanks advanced into an area east of Nuseirat refugee camp and onto the territory's main north-south road under the cover of artillery strikes.

The Israeli military said the "targeted" operation aimed to dismantle a "terror tunnel" west of the so-called Yellow Line, which marks the more than 60% of Gaza's territory occupied by Israel.

The Palestinian armed group Hamas, which controls the rest of Gaza, accused Israel of a "flagrant violation" of the October 2025 ceasefire deal.

Violence has continued in Gaza over the past 13 months, with near-daily Israeli strikes, and living conditions for the 2.1 million people there remain dire.

Wednesday's Israeli incursion deep beyond the Yellow Line was reportedly the first to be publicly announced by the Israel Defense Forces (IDF) since the ceasefire began.

In the early afternoon, Israeli military body Cogat's Arabic spokesperson announced the start of the operation and warned Palestinians living in an area north of Nuseirat and Bureij refugee camp to stay away for their own safety. It also said a section of Salah al-Din road that passed through the area would be closed to traffic.

In the evening, the IDF said the joint operation with the Shin Bet security service sought "to dismantle a terror tunnel west of the Yellow Line that posed an immediate and direct threat to IDF troops".

On Thursday morning, Cogat told Palestinians on social media that "the activity has ended, and the Salah al-Din axis has been reopened".

Hamas warned in a statement that the advance of Israeli military vehicles beyond the Yellow Line and the evacuation of residents constituted "a dangerous development and a flagrant violation of the ceasefire agreement".

Gaza's Hamas-run government media office meanwhile said that an estimated 6,000 people had fled their homes and tents because of the operation, and that this amounted to the war crime of forced displacement.

Footage published by Palestinian media outlets and on social media showed dozens of families fleeing areas near Nuseirat camp following the Israeli evacuation order.

In one video verified by BBC Verify, a man could be seen ducking after hearing artillery fire and then running along Salah al-Din road, about 1.5km (0.9 miles) west of the Yellow Line. "The Israeli occupation now is firing randomly near the houses and tents of people," he said.

An image, meanwhile, showed six armoured vehicles, including tanks and bulldozers, around Salah al-Din road.

A resident of Nuseirat who was forced to flee told BBC Arabic's Middle East Daily programme: "The situation is now extremely dire, with Israeli forces firing indiscriminately at areas where displaced people have sought shelter."

"We couldn't take any of our belongings with us - not even our blankets, pillows, or clothes. We had no choice but to leave everything behind."

A second resident also reported "indiscriminate gunfire and shelling" and said there had been "numerous casualties, including fatalities".

Asked about those reports, the IDF sent a general statement about the operation.

Multiple verified videos of the aftermath of the incursion on Thursday showed destruction, earth banks, and heavy vehicle tracks more than 1.2km beyond the Yellow Line.

That is believed to be the furthest Israeli troops have advanced into central Gaza since they occupied the so-called Netzarim Corridor before the ceasefire.

Some residents returned to check their properties following the Israeli withdrawal.

"The entire area has been destroyed, and we honestly do not know what to do," Abd Aliwa told Reuters news agency as he sat beside a pile of rubble.

Israel building new land barriers in Gaza and displacing families

Israel moves Yellow Line deeper into Gaza, satellite images show

Gaza's Hamas-run Civil Defence agency said three people were killed and others were wounded in three separate Israeli drone strikes on Wednesday.

One man was killed in Nuseirat, the second was killed to the north in Gaza City, and the third was killed in the central town of Deir al-Balah, it added.

The IDF told the BBC that it had "struck several military operatives".

Gaza health officials said another two people were killed on Thursday by Israeli fire in eastern Gaza City, according to Reuters. There was no immediate comment from the IDF.

At least 1,662 people have been killed in Israeli attacks in Gaza since the ceasefire began last year, according to the territory's Hamas-run health ministry, whose figures are considered reliable by the UN.

The Israeli military says six of its soldiers have been killed over the same period.

Meanwhile, more than 1.9 million people - about 90% of Gaza's population - remain displaced, with 1.4 million of them living in tents or makeshift shelters, according to the UN.

Reconstruction has yet to begin in Gaza, where an estimated 82% of all structures have been destroyed or damaged during the war, as US President Donald Trump's peace plan has largely stalled.

The disarmament of Hamas has been a key sticking point.

The group has agreed to give up its weapons in exchange for a phased withdrawal of Israeli troops from the 60% of Gaza they currently occupy. But Israel has insisted that Hamas must fully disarm before any pullout.

The war was triggered by the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, when about 1,200 people were killed and 251 others were taken hostage.

Since then, more than 74,710 people have been killed in Israeli attacks in Gaza, its health ministry says.

#86. NextDC vows to keep future datacentres away from homes after community backlash in Melbourne

Public expectations around datacentres have changed in the age of AI, chief executive Craig Scroggie says

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Community backlash to a datacentre expansion in inner Melbourne that resulted in NextDC’s share price dropping 40% will change the way the company engages with communities – and it says it will no longer pursue new developments near homes.

The company held its first annual general meeting since it was forced to delay the controversial expansion of its M3 site in West Footscray after power provider Jemena paused construction of new 22-metre power poles amid community protest.

The share price has since recovered to over $9 a share, but not before rival Goodman Group bought a nearly 10% stake in the business.

Shareholders revolted against the company at the AGM, issuing a second strike against executive pay, but the resulting motion on whether to spill the board was resoundingly defeated on Friday.

The chief executive, Craig Scroggie, said it was his “hardest AGM” in 15 years, and that he ultimately took responsibility for “delivery, power, planning, approvals, construction and community support”.

“At M3, we needed to work more closely with our power and construction partners, and with the community,” he said. “We are now doing that.”

Scroggie said the community deserved to be heard earlier than they had been during the Jemena construction works, and it was NextDC’s job to do that.

“M3 has certainly changed how we plan, build and operate alongside our neighbours,” he said.

“For more than 15 years we’ve built datacentres right across the country, and what earned trust then is not enough now. Community expectations have moved in the age of artificial intelligence and we must move with them.”

In addition to a new community consultative committee, including a standing forum for residents and local stakeholders, NextDC has said it will have a dedicated project website and contact channels with defined response times for complaints and inquiries.

NextDC’s chair, Doug Flynn, also revealed that the company would change where it builds in future.

“Given community sensitivities, we do not intend to pursue new development opportunities that are near residential properties,” Flynn said.

“The pushback against datacentres is very different right now than it was … six or so years ago.”

Flynn said that the M3 site in inner Melbourne had been an industrial site for 100 years, and had petrochemical works on it before NextDC built the datacentre.

After the blowback to taller power poles led to a delayed expansion, Flynn said the Jemena employee overseeing community engagement left the business.

“They recognise that they were just charging ahead regardless of community,” he said. “And they got a bloody nose.

A spokesperson for Jemena declined to comment, but pointed to a September statement announcing its general manager for electricity networks had decided to step down.

#85. ICJ ruling on Myanmar genocide: ‘Historic victory for Rohingya, landmark for international justice’

Erin Ogunkeye welcomes Reed Brody, Member of the International Commission of Jurists, Human Rights Lawyer and veteran war crimes prosecutor. The International Court of Justice's ruling on Myanmar marks, according to Brody, "a historic victory for the Rohingya, but also a landmark for international justice." He explains how the court assessed evidence of genocidal intent, rejecting Myanmar's justification of its military operations as counterterrorism. He argues that "the existence of an armed conflict or a security objective doesn't exclude genocidal intent," a finding he believes will have "far-reaching implications in future genocide cases," including South Africa's case against Israel over Gaza. Brody also discusses the Gambia's role in bringing the case and growing challenges to international accountability, warning that "we're in a very dangerous moment."

#84. Florida pastor found guilty over role in assassination of Haitian president

Christian Emmanuel Sanon was found guilty of all eight counts against him in a Florida court on Wednesday

A Florida pastor has been found guilty of a number of charges relating to the assassination of the Haitian President Jovenel MoÃ¯se.

Christian Emmanuel Sanon, a dual US-Haitian national, was convicted of all eight charges against him, including conspiracy to kill or kidnap a person. He faces a potential sentence of life in prison.

The 67-year-old was accused of being involved in a plot to replace MoÃ¯se as Haiti's president with himself in 2021, allegedly so he could award lucrative contracts to a Florida-based security outfit once in place.

The murder of MoÃ¯se has left Haiti without a leader for five years. That political vacuum has intensified gang violence in the impoverished Caribbean nation.

A presidential election is scheduled for next year and MoÃ¯se's wife, Martine, who was injured in the attack that killed her husband, announced this week that she intended to stand.

Sanon was found guilty on Wednesday following an eight-week trial in federal court in Miami, according to CBS, the BBC's US partner. His lawyer said he maintains his innocence and that he intends to appeal against the verdict, it reports.

Prosecutors said Sanon had "political aspirations" in Haiti and, in April 2021, met several other co-conspirators to devise a plan to oust MoÃ¯se in an armed raid on the presidential palace in the capital, Port-au-Prince.

Among them were the heads of the companies Counter Terrorist Unit Federal Academy and Counter Terrorist Unit Security - collectively referred to as CTU - who would finance the coup in exchange for government contracts for infrastructure projects, and to provide security forces and equipment.

A few months later, the other conspirators realised that Sanon had "neither the constitutional qualifications nor the popular support" to become Haiti's president and so shifted to another candidate, the US Department of Justice said.

At the same time, the plot had developed from one to forcibly remove MoÃ¯se from office to assassinating him, investigators said.

Dozens of people have been implicated in the conspiracy, which prosecutors said involved co-ordinating funding for and delivering weapons and mercenaries to Haiti to carry out the attack.

On 6 July that year, a group of Colombian mercenaries entered the presidential palace and killed MoÃ¯se. His wife, who was also injured in the attack, said at the time that the assailants had "riddled" her husband with bullets.

Jovenel MoÃ¯se was gunned down by mercenaries in Haiti's presidential palace in 2021

The successful assassination plot raised questions in Haiti about the relative ease with which the mercenaries were able to gain access to the president.

The country - the poorest in the Americas - has been ravaged by gang violence for years, but MoÃ¯se's assassination ushered in a period of worsening instability that emboldened gangs and prompted the deployment of a multinational force.

However, this has struggled to penetrate areas where gangs hold sway and more than 5,700 people have been killed or injured in the year to 11 September, the UN said last month.

Sanon and several other co-conspirators were arrested by Haitian authorities, while three mercenaries were killed in a gun battle with police. Eighteen people were transferred to Florida in September to stand trial over the plot.

Four men, including the heads of the CTU, were convicted of their role earlier this year. Sanon had been scheduled to be tried with them, but his case was delayed due to health reasons, according to CBS.

Sanon was born in Haiti but moved to the US as a teenager. He told the court he had worked in churches in Haitian immigrant communities in several parts of the US including Florida, US media report.

#83. Beau Lamarre-Condon’s explanations of body disposal ‘ring hollow’, court hears

Former police officer has pleaded not guilty to murdering Jesse Baird and Luke Davies in Paddington in February 2024

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Beau Lamarre-Condon knew he had murdered two men and his attempts to explain the disposal of their bodies “ring completely hollow”, a court has heard.

Lamarre-Condon is accused of fatally shooting Jesse Baird and his new partner, Luke Davies, with his police service weapon in Baird’s Paddington home in Sydney on 19 February 2024.

The former officer has pleaded not guilty to two counts of murder and one charge of aggravated break and enter.

On Friday, Crown prosecutor Brett Hatfield SC went through Lamarre-Condon’s attempts to clean up the alleged crime scene and dispose of the bodies as the trial wrapped up its ninth week.

The defence does not dispute Lamarre-Condon’s actions in the aftermath of the deaths, instead arguing he did so because he “panicked considerably”.

But Hatfield told the New South Wales supreme court his conduct was not that of a person who panicked after something unintended, but behaviour that was “deliberate, sustained and very purposeful”.

The crown prosecutor said Lamarre-Condon took extensive steps to distance himself from the deaths, manufactured evidence to make it look like Baird was alive by texting people from the dead man’s phone, and “disposed of the bodies in an utterly callous manner”.

“The lies, concealment and disposal of the bodies are not equally explained by manslaughter or accident,” Hatfield said.

“His conduct is immediate and the steps are so extreme you might think it’s only consistent with having intentionally killed both men and a planned murder.

“His explanations in his evidence … ring completely hollow.”

Lamarre-Condon claimed his initial steps came out of concern for the housemates, but by leaving the bodies in the courtyard, he risked them making a “completely terrifying discovery”, Hatfield told the court.

Hatfield said the accused had an understanding that “exactly what he’d done was murder”.

“His conduct was an exercise in avoiding accountability in what he knew he had done,” he said.

Earlier on Friday, Hatfield re-iterated the prosecution’s arguments about the day of the alleged murders, playing the audio of the two gunshots that killed Baird.

The lawyer invited the jury to consider what they could do in the four seconds between the shots.

Making a gun gesture with his own hand, Hatfield argued there was only time to walk a few steps and fire and “not opportunity for much more than that”.

Lamarre-Condon then came up with a “pathetic moral dilemma” and claimed he feared he could be killed in jail, when he knew he would be in protective custody, the prosecution alleged.

His decisions to go to Sushi Train for dinner with his friend and undergo a minor elective surgery in the aftermath of the alleged murders showed Lamarre-Condon was calm and at points “completely relaxed”, Hatfield alleged.

But within days, the accused must have been “feeling the world closing in”, the prosecutor said.

“He wants credit for handing himself in to police … but even in his last hours he’s there going to Bunnings, buying paint and painting the inside of the van … still trying to cover up what happened,” Hatfield told the court.

Lamarre-Condon eventually helped police locate the bodies, but only after being told about the state’s no body, no parole laws.

“The bodies were left … for a further four days after he’s arrested,” Hatfield said. “Evidence all the while being destroyed.

“Imagine the grief [of the families] on those four days.”

The defence alleges Baird and Davies were killed in two separate struggles when Lamarre-Condon was acting in “self-defence”.

Going through forensic evidence, Hatfield noted Davies’ DNA was found on the gun, but says this is “entirely unremarkable evidence”, alleging Lamarre-Condon had handled his body at the scene and then touched the weapon.

“It’s just something he’s grabbed on to in order to weave this invented narrative about Mr Davies shooting himself,” the lawyer said.

Earlier evidence provided by a weapons expert indicated it would take the force equivalent to almost 3kg to pull the trigger, which would not happen without a conscious movement on behalf of the person holding the gun, the prosecution alleged.

The trial before Justice Natalie Adams continues.

#79. Survivors of Uganda's deadly landslide seek justice at the UN

Bududa has suffered repeated deadly landslides over the years

Survivors of a 2019 landslide in eastern Uganda that killed more than 50 people have taken their fight for relocation and compensation to the United Nations.

The 48 complainants accuse the government of violating Uganda's constitution by failing to put effective disaster management measures in Bududa, a mountainous district prone to landslides.

Their case comes as heavy rains raise the risk of further landslides. They say the government relocation programme ignores their safety, land rights and livelihoods.

The UN Human Rights Committee is considering what is believed to be the first case seeking climate adaptation measures, one that could set a global precedent.

At least 33 of the 53 people who died in the 2019 landslide were children. Years later, survivors say every spell of heavy rain brings back fears of another tragedy.

William Tsama, one of the claimants, lost four members of his family.

"Victims in such situations, ignored by governments, can take them to court to compel them to help their citizens."

Uganda introduced a resettlement plan for landslide-prone areas in 2011, but progress has been limited, with relatively few people relocated.

Last month, it launched a new resettlement programme offering affected households 18m Ugandan shillings ($4,300; Â£3,300) to help them move to safer areas.

But some survivors have questioned whether the amount is enough to cover the cost of relocating and rebuilding their lives.

Under the programme, families can choose between receiving two acres of land, valued at 8m shillings, plus 10m shillings towards building a house, or taking the equivalent land allowance in cash, alongside the same housing grant.

The scheme is expected to benefit 1,410 households identified as living in high-risk areas.

The government has now given residents of designated high-risk areas two months, from November to December, to leave or face forced eviction.

The threat extends beyond landslides. The complainants say changing rainfall patterns and declining soil fertility have also affected farming, leaving many families struggling to secure their livelihoods.

"I want to get a new good place for my people, so even if I die, they will be okay. I am an old man, but I am doing it for my children," Tsama said.

The survivors first launched legal action in 2020 in Uganda, and received support from Ugandan environmental organisation Greenwatch three years later.

They are seeking compensation for lost family members and livelihoods, as well as emergency measures, including accessible early-warning systems in high-risk areas.

Greenwatch Executive Director Samantha Atukunda Kakuru Mwesigwa said the case is about changing how governments prepare and respond to climate-related disasters.

"Justice for claimants is not just about winning in court. It is about forcing government policy to keep pace with a changing climate, and ensuring loss and damage is no longer an afterthought in disaster management," she said.

The threat of climate-related disasters extends across East Africa.

In Kenya, floods and landslides killed at least 181 people between March and May 2024, according to the Kenya Red Cross. In Rwanda, floods and landslides killed 135 people in May 2023.

The dangers are also being felt further afield. In October 2026, a flash flood swept through Nepal's Gorkha district after a landslide blocked a river, washing away homes and infrastructure, and killing over 1,500 people.

"Disasters like the one in Bududa are only getting worse because of climate change â and they are happening all around the world, as we've seen recently and tragically in Nepal," ClientEarth lawyer Vesselina Newman said.

She said poorer countries, which have contributed relatively little to global emissions, are bearing some of the heaviest consequences of climate change and need greater financial support.

"High income countries must provide financial assistance and ensure that sufficient money goes directly to countries like Uganda, so they can roll out life-saving adaptation measures," she said.

The complainants say relocation must involve more than moving people out of danger.

They want the government to protect their livelihoods, respect their land rights, and take account of individual needs.

The case is now before the United Nations Human Rights Committee, which will ask the Ugandan government to respond within six months.

#75. US consortium wins huge Kenya mining deal, beating Chinese rivals

The US is playing catch-up with China's dominance in critical minerals mining and processing across Africa (file photo)

A US-led consortium, Frontier Minerals, has won the tender to develop Kenya's Mrima Hill mining project, beating seven bidders, including companies from China and Australia.

The site is estimated to hold deposits of critical minerals worth more than $60bn (Â£45bn), including niobium, used in electric vehicles, aerospace and defence technology.

Frontier Minerals says its proposal aims to keep more of the economic benefits in Kenya through "processing and refining of minerals in-country".

The deal comes as Washington seeks greater access to Africa's minerals, aiming to strengthen US supply chains and compete with China.

Frontier Minerals brings together three US companies - Bunker Hill Mining Corp, American Resources Corporation and ReElement Technologies Corporation. The consortium combines expertise in mineral resource development, critical materials supply chains and advanced refining technologies.

A central part of its proposal is to develop mineral processing and refining capacity in Kenya. Brad Barnett from Frontier Minerals said the company wanted the country to capture more value from its mineral resources.

Mrima Hill is located in Kwale County, near Kenya's Indian Ocean coastline. The site covers about 390 acres of forest.

The prospect of mining in the area has raised concerns among local residents, who fear the project could damage the forest and threaten their ancestral heritage.

There are also questions over whether communities living near the site will benefit from the investment.

Barnett was keen to reassure Kenyans that they would benefit: "We intend to create lasting opportunities for Kenyan workers, businesses and the wider economy through capacity-building and targeted investment, while prioritising environmental management and performance."

The consortium must still obtain a prospecting licence and meet the necessary regulatory requirements before the project can proceed.

The tender decision has drawn objections from competing bidders.

Reuters news agency reports that two rival companies intend to challenge the selection, with Australia's RareX alleging that Frontier Minerals missed an earlier submission deadline.

Kenya's government has maintained that the process was open and transparent.

The project comes as US strives to secure alternative sources of critical minerals and reduce dependence on China, which dominates the processing of several key minerals.

Washington has been pursuing partnerships with mineral-rich African countries, including the Democratic Republic of Congo and Zambia, as it seeks to strengthen supply chains for industries ranging from electric vehicles to defence technology.

Kenya's mining ministry says the sector is an important part of its plans to diversify the economy beyond traditional sources of revenue such as tourism, agriculture and financial services.
