#74. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#73. Kellie Sloane says she’s ‘pretty sure’ NSW Liberals exodus is over after Mark Speakman resigns

Former opposition leader warns party against being ‘One Nation lite’ as he becomes eighth out of 24 Liberal MPs not to recontest seat in March

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

The New South Wales opposition leader, Kellie Sloane, says she is “pretty sure” the exodus of Liberals before the March state election has finished, after Mark Speakman became the eighth MP to decide not to recontest their lower house seats.

In a late-night speech on Thursday, the final sitting day of NSW’s legislative assembly before the vote, Speakman said he would not run again in his south Sydney seat of Cronulla, which he has held since 2011.

A third of the 24 current Liberal MPs have announced they will not recontest their seat in March, including Jordan Lane, Mark Coure, Tanya Davies, Ray Williams, James Griffin, Alister Henskens and Mark Taylor.

In a press conference on Friday, Sloane insisted the departures were not an indictment of the party’s election prospects.

“No, that’s not the truth. What we are seeing is an impressive cross section of abilities and talent and tenure in our party,” she told reporters in the same room where, exactly a year ago, Speakman announced he would resign as Liberal leader in her favour.

“We have people who have been in government before. We have people who have been in for a medium amount of time, and we’re going to have people with fresh ideas.”

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

Sloane said she had tried to convince Speakman to stay and said many of the MPs leaving had been in parliament for more than 10 years, although Lane is a first-term MP.

Sloane said a significant number of Liberals had retired before the 2023 election, in which the Coalition lost government and the party dropped nine seats.

Asked if she could guarantee that further MPs would not step down ahead of the March vote, Sloane said: “Look, I certainly hope not. I certainly hope not. I’m pretty sure that’s done. I’m pretty sure that’s done.”

Since last year’s leadership spill, the Coalition has gone from commanding 28% of the primary vote in NSW to 23%, Sydney Morning Herald Resolve polling suggests.

Labor has gone from 37% to 30%, while One Nation, which Resolve did not start polling separately until March, is now at 26%.

Last week, Sloane said her party would not form government with One Nation, but she did not rule out a confidence and supply arrangement. The Australian Financial Review has reported many of her MPs privately expect the Liberals to go into a coalition with One Nation.

On Friday, Sloane rejected the claims from “anonymous names in a Financial Review article”, saying the Coalition only needed to flip six Labor seats to form minority government.

In his speech in the dying minutes of the parliamentary term, Speakman, a member of the party’s moderate faction, said the Liberals faced an “existential threat” following a wipeout at the 2025 federal election and an “orange wave that seems to be coming across the country”.

He warned the Liberals against becoming “Labor lite” or “One Nation lite”, saying they should stick to the values of “Menzian Liberalism”.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

Referencing Pauline Hanson’s notion of “monoculture”, Speakman said Australia was “the most successful multicultural society on the planet” and was “so much better for it”.

“You know, we hear certain people say we want a monoculture, and that people who don’t support that monoculture somehow are enemies of Australia, hate Australia,” he said.

“I love Australia for its multiculturalism. Diversity is our strength. It is our strength, and we should build on that.”

Speakman, a barrister, served as environment minister between 2015 and 2017, and was attorney general between 2017 and the Perrottet government’s defeat in the 2023 election.

He was chosen as the Liberal and opposition leader after Dominic Perrottet’s resignation, a position he held until November last year.

On Thursday, he said being opposition leader “is often said to be the toughest job in politics” and, while it had “ultimately unravelled” for him, he had mostly led an opposition “which was competitive with, or even ahead of, the government in the polls”.

He said his record as attorney general included legislating for NSW to be the first Australian jurisdiction to criminalise coercive control, introducing affirmative consent tests in criminal sexual assault trials and reforming criminal and civil child sexual abuse cases.

Earlier on Thursday, the NSW premier, Chris Minns, announced the former Liberal councillor and parliamentary staffer Tanya Raffoul would contest the Liberal-held seat of Epping as a Labor candidate.

On Friday, Sloane said Raffoul had been talking to her about Liberal party roles “only two months ago”.

“She’s decided to chase personal ambition. She’s a very ambitious person, but she’s changed sides, and that’s a decision Chris Minns has made.”

Earlier, the NSW treasurer, Daniel Mookhey, rejected the idea that One Nation was pushing Labor to become “Liberal lite”, saying Raffoul’s candidacy reflected “a journey that I think many Liberal people are going through as well”.

#70. Susan Smith denied parole again 32 years after drowning her two sons

Susan Smith's ex-husband makes emotional appeal against granting parole

A South Carolina woman who admitted to drowning her two sons 32 years ago has been denied parole for a second time.

Susan Smith, 55, was convicted of killing her boys by strapping them into their car seats and rolling her vehicle into a lake in 1994.

"I got to my lowest when I let that car go into that lake," Smith said during a hearing on Thursday with the state board that decides whether to release those in prison on parole.

The seven-member board later unanimously turned down her request to be freed. The father of the two boys has vowed to continue to oppose her release, and several people spoke against her in the hearing.

Smith, whose lawyers had argued she suffered a mental breakdown during the crime, can apply to the South Carolina Board of Probation, Parole and Pardon Services for parole every two years.

She told the board that she had changed and matured since killing three-year-old Michael and one-year-old Alex, and that "I am not the same person I was 32 years ago".

She also said she was "very, very sorry" and that she would go back and change the circumstances if she could.

"They were innocent children," Smith said. "They did not deserve to die."

Her children's father, David Smith, attended the hearing so he could advocate for their sons, he said.

The 32 years, he said, do not take away what Smith "deliberately" did.

"I know in my heart granting her parole would be a huge injustice to Michael and Alex," said David Smith, who had pinned a photo of both his sons to his lapel.

Smith was convicted of killing her two sons in 1994

Prosecutors argued during the trial that Smith killed the boys after a man with whom she was having an affair told her that her sons were the reason that the pair did not have a future together.

At Thursday's hearing, Smith said: "This did not happen because of a man. That's just not true."

When the boys first disappeared, Smith said she was carjacked late at night and a man drove away with her car. While she pleaded publicly for their safe return, her sons were at the bottom of a nearby lake.

But ultimately she confessed to the crime.

Defence lawyers said she had wanted to die with her children but left the car at the last minute.

Smith was convicted of murder. Under South Carolina law at the time, she was allowed to request parole every two years after serving 30 years in prison.

In the US, some people convicted of crimes are eligible for parole, which means they can be released from incarceration.

She would have needed two-thirds of the board members present to vote in her favour to be freed.

#68. Venezuela signs military deal with US less than a year after Maduro's removal

Pete Hegseth met Venezuela's interim president at the presidential palace

The United States and Venezuela have re-established military ties less than a year after US forces seized the then-leader of the South American country, NicolÃ¡s Maduro, in a raid on the capital, Caracas.

The interim government led by Delcy RodrÃ­guez, Maduro's former vice-president, signed a military co-operation agreement with US Secretary of Defence Pete Hegseth on Wednesday.

Few details about the deal have been released so far but Hegseth said that Venezuela had "gone from our most aggressive regional threat to a compliant security partner" in less than 12 months.

Interim President RodrÃ­guez said the deal would help confront "common threats".

Military co-operation between the two countries was terminated in 2005 by Hugo ChÃ¡vez, Venezuela's left-wing president at the time.

ChÃ¡vez alleged that US military personnel sent to Venezuela to train their counterparts had made disparaging remarks about his government.

He also accused the US military office located in Fuerte Tiuna, Venezuela's largest military complex, of being "an organ of the CIA... conspiring against the government".

Under NicolÃ¡s Maduro, who succeeded ChÃ¡vez after the latter's death from cancer, relations between the two countries deteriorated further.

They reached rock bottom late last year when the US doubled its reward for information leading to Maduro's capture to $50m (Â£38m) and eventually seized him on 3 January in a military raid.

Maduro and his wife, Cilia Flores, are now in custody in New York on charges of drug-trafficking and conspiracy to commit torture, which they deny.

Hegseth addressed the change in the two countries' relationship since Maduro was seized in his speech at the US embassy in Caracas on Wednesday.

"It's a great honour to be here as the first United States Secretary of War this century to set foot in Venezuela. The weight of this moment does not escape me," he said.

"Just one year ago, my presence here, our presence here would have been deemed impossible, almost unimaginable," he added before recalling a public appearance by then-President Maduro which had angered the Trump administration.

"Exactly one year ago today, the indicted narco-terrorist fugitive NicolÃ¡s Maduro, the man who spent more than a decade handing Venezuela's borders and territory to terrorists from all over the world, stood on a stage, not too far from here, and yelled mockingly: 'Come and get me!'. Well, we answered that call."

Hegseth added that the result of the US military raid on 3 January had been as Maduro predicted, quoting the deposed leader's words: "No war, yes peace."

He described the deal signed by him and Venezuela's defence minister on Wednesday as a "new chapter for the United States, for Venezuela, for the Venezuelan military and for the Western Hemisphere through the Americas Counter Cartel Coalition".

RodrÃ­guez described it as "extraordinarily relevant for our bilateral relations".

"In a matter of months, secretary, we have turned very difficult moments for our people into a space for co-operation and mutual benefit for both of our countries," she said addressing Hegseth during a meeting at the presidential palace.

While no details of the deal have been published, in her speech RodrÃ­guez mentioned the training of Venezuela's armed forces and improving its "operational equipment" and access to new technology "to confront shared threats".

In his speech at the US embassy, Hegseth was more direct about who he considers to be those threats. Addressing "the narco-terrorists who abuse this [Venezuela] as a safe haven", he warned that "we will hunt you down, we will find you, and we will kill you".

From Venezuela, Hegseth travelled on to neighbouring Colombia, whose president, Abelardo de la Espriella, is a close ally of the Trump administration.

Colombia is already part of the US-led Americas Counter Cartel Coalition and in August, Hegseth had floated the possibility of joint US-Colombian military operations against armed groups.

On Wednesday, President de la Espriella took to X to deny a report by the New York Times which had stated that he had rejected carrying out joint operations, external.

In his post, he stressed that any decision would be taken while respecting Colombia's constitution, and the country's laws and sovereignty.

Hegseth reposted the tweet commenting that the US-Colombian partnership "is stronger than ever before".

#66. Apec summit as it happened: Xi welcomes Trump on China visit as Putin claims Europe in ‘humiliating decline’

This blog is now closed, you can read more on this story here

The Associated Press is reporting that Donald Trump plans to hold a meeting with Vladimir Putin on Thursday, citing an anonymous US official. The meeting would be in addition to the blockbuster trilateral talks the two will hold with host Xi Jinping on Thursday.

Meanwhile, a meeting between the leaders of China, the United States and Russia planned for Thursday in Shenzhen is set to last less than 60 minutes, Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told Russian state media on Wednesday. “No more than an hour” has been set aside for the historic summit between Xi Jinping, Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin, Peskov was quoted as saying by Russian state news agencies.

Xi Jinping told Donald Trump he hoped their talks would help advance a “constructive, strategic and stable relationship” between China and the US, based on “respect, fairness and reciprocity”, according to China’s state news agency Xinhua. Xi also recalled Trump’s personal welcome at the airport during his visit to the US in September, saying China’s own welcome for the US president reflected its tradition of reciprocity.

Xi personally greeted Trump on the airport tarmac in Shenzhen, reciprocating the welcome the US president extended to the Chinese leader during his visit to the US in September.

Before landing in Shenzhen, Donald Trump enjoyed lunch in Manila with Philippine president Ferdinand Marcos Jr at the Malacañang presidential palace. Speaking during a meeting ahead of lunch, Marcos revealed that he had arranged for fried chicken from Filipino fast food chain Jollibee to be delivered to the US delegation as a late-night snack last night, praising Trump for “working very hard”.

Chinese state media have released a readout of Xi Jinping’s earlier meeting with Vladimir Putin. In it, Xi is quoted as saying China-Russia relations are “unshaken by storms and steady as a mountain”, and have set “an example of a new type of major-country relationship characterised by equality, mutual respect, trustworthiness, and win-win co-operation”.

Canada’s Mark Carney has hit back at Vladimir Putin’s earlier assertion that sanctions no longer work in this day and age. “Mr Putin can ask his financial institutions, he can ask his oligarch friends, he can ask his hockey players whether or not sanctions are a thing of the past. I think he’ll find out that they’re very real, they’re biting,” Carney told reporters.

Mexican president Claudia Sheinbaum won praise and went viral on Chinese social media on Wednesday for taking an economy flight to attend a regional summit in Shenzhen. World leaders this week descended on the southern Chinese city to attend the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation meetings – many arriving in specially chartered commercial airliners or dedicated government aircraft like US president Donald Trump’s Air Force One.

It is unclear whether North Korean leader Kim Jong-un will attend the talks, but Trump has floated the possibility. On Wednesday US secretary of state Marco Rubio said the US president was willing to engage, but he did not confirm whether a meeting had been set in Shenzhen.

Trump said on Wednesday that the US has just ⁠concluded a “very big ⁠trade ​deal” with the Philippines, but he did not provide any details, and Manila ⁠did not announce any agreement. “We’ve just consummated a very big trade deal, the ⁠biggest trade deal we’ve ever made so far, and it ​is a fair ‌deal for both countries,” ‌Trump said during a state luncheon at the Philippine ‌presidential palace. Trump is set to arrive in Shenzhen on Wednesday evening.

Trump landed in China on Wednesday seeking to reinforce America’s status as the world’s leading technology power, but a distressing series of technical outages back home has left his administration scrambling to calm the US public.

The issues put the administration on the defensive at a moment when the president wants to project strength and certainty before the meeting with Xi and Putin.

As he began his trip to Asia, Trump initially had been nonchalant about the outages that left people in the US worried about accessing their email, checking their bank accounts or safely flying on an airplane.

“It’s fine,” Trump said Tuesday from the Philippine capital of Manila, suggesting it was all just a consequence of being a nation that is “big and powerful.”

The meeting between the leaders of China, the United States and Russia planned for Thursday in Shenzhen is set to last less than 60 minutes, Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov told Russian state media.

“No more than an hour” has been set aside for the historic summit between Xi Jinping, Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin, Peskov was quoted as saying by Russian state news agencies.

The Associated Press is reporting that Donald Trump plans to hold a separate meeting with Vladimir Putin on Thursday, citing an anonymous US official.

The meeting would be in addition to the blockbuster trilateral talks the two will hold with host Xi Jinping on Thursday.

If confirmed, it would be their first bilateral since they met in Alaska in 2025 for talks that failed to reach a deal on the war in Ukraine.

Trump had already suggested he might meet with Putin, as well as with North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, but neither option had been added to his official schedule.

Before landing in Shenzhen, Donald Trump enjoyed lunch in Manila with Philippine president Ferdinand Marcos Jr at the Malacañang presidential palace.

Speaking during a meeting ahead of lunch, Marcos revealed that he had arranged for fried chicken from Filipino fast food chain Jollibee to be delivered to the US delegation as a late-night snack last night, praising Trump for “working very hard”.

The gift may have gone down particularly well with Trump, who has something of a reputation for his love of fast food. The US president has long been associated with McDonald’s and KFC, with his former campaign aides revealing in 2017 that his usual McDonald’s order included two Big Macs, two Filet-O-Fish sandwiches and a chocolate milkshake. The Guardian reported on their revelations at the time in this article:

Mexican president Claudia Sheinbaum won praise and went viral on Chinese social media on Wednesday for taking an economy flight to attend a regional summit in Shenzhen.

World leaders this week descended on the southern Chinese city to attend the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation meetings – many arriving in specially chartered commercial airliners or dedicated government aircraft like US president Donald Trump’s Air Force One.

double quotation markThis evening, the Prime Minister, Mark Carney, met with the President of the United States, Donald J. Trump, on the margins of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) Economic Leaders’ Meeting, in Shenzhen, China.

The leaders discussed the work underway to reach a mutually beneficial trade agreement that would create good jobs, boost affordability, and fully respect Canada’s languages, culture, and sovereignty. They agreed to continue the work and to stay in close contact.

double quotation markAt tomorrow’s Leaders’ Meeting, we will discuss measures to expand mutual openness, explore pathways for innovation-driven growth, broaden opportunities for pragmatic cooperation in various fields, and promote the implementation of more outcomes. I look forward to discussing the grand plan for Asia-Pacific cooperation and jointly advancing the cause of Asia-Pacific development with you all.

Now, I propose that we raise a glass together.

For the common prosperity of the Asia-Pacific region,

For the success of this informal leaders’ meeting,

For the health of all guests and their families,

China’s state news agency Xinhua has published the text of Xi Jinping’s welcome speech at tonight’s Apec banquet in Shenzhen.

In his remarks, Xi praised the city’s transformation into an international economic and innovation hub, describing it as a symbol of China’s development.

Here are a few passages from his address:

double quotation markThis evening, the shores of the South China Sea are aglow with lights, and autumn is in full swing here in Shenzhen, the City of the Phoenix. As the 33rd Apec Economic Leaders’ Meeting is about to commence, I would like, on behalf of the Chinese Government and the Chinese people, to extend a warm welcome to all our distinguished guests.

I have often said that to see a thousand years of China, one should visit Xi’an; to see five hundred years of China, one should visit Beijing; and to see a hundred years of China, one should visit Shanghai. Today, I would like to say to you all: to see fifty years of China, come to Shenzhen.

Shenzhen is a place with a rich history. In 1978, when I first came to Shenzhen, it was still a small fishing village. More than 40 years have passed in the blink of an eye, and now it is filled with high-rise buildings and bustling traffic. The changes are truly remarkable.”

#61. Van Gogh painting sells for record $190.4m after row over its Nazi history

Watch: Moment Van Gogh painting sells for record $190.4m (Â£144.8m)

Auction house Sotheby's has sold a painting by Vincent van Gogh for a record $190.4m (Â£144.8m).

The sale of ChÃ¢taigniers en fleurs ("Chestnut Trees in Blossom") shatters the renowned artist's previous auction record of $117.2 million set in 2022.

The historic sale in New York on Wednesday comes after an eleventh-hour agreement was reached to resolve a dispute regarding claims the art piece was previously sold under duress during the rise of the Nazis.

ChÃ¢taigniers en fleurs was painted in 1890 in the final two months of Van Gogh's life. It is considered one of the most important works of art from that period to remain in private hands, according to Sotheby's.

Sotheby's auction of $180m Van Gogh challenged over claims it was 'sold under duress'

It was not immediately clear who purchased the painting. The BBC has contacted Sotheby's for comment.

The oil painting, which was estimated to carry a value of $180m (Â£136m) prior to the sale, was put up for auction as part of a set called the Blaquier-Arrieta Collection.

Sotheby's announced in the hours before the auction that it had resolved a dispute between the Blaquier-Arrieta family and the heirs of the Fischer family - the prior German owners of the work - regarding the sale.

Representatives acting for heirs of the Fischer family had claimed the painting may have been sold under duress during the rise of the Nazis.

A lawyer for the family's heirs reportedly wrote to Sotheby's on their behalf, telling the auction house that the painting was sold in Amsterdam before World War Two because of financial losses the Fischer family's publishing company suffered under the Nazis. The BBC has contacted the lawyer for comment.

"In recognition of the painting's complex Nazi-era history and the suffering endured by the Fischer family during the Holocaust, the two families have reached a mutually satisfactory resolution and the work will be sold pursuant to their agreement," Sotheby's said on Wednesday.

Sotheby's website details the provenance of the oil painting and how it has researched the artwork changing hands since 1890.

It says Samuel Fischer - a famous German publisher - acquired it in 1909 before it was passed down in his family after his death in 1934.

The painting was deposited with Paul Cassirer in Amsterdam in 1932 or 1933 before it went to Henriette and Marcel Kapferer in Paris in 1936, Sotheby's said.

The Blaquier family acquired it in 1975 and owned it for more than half a century before the latest sale, the auction house said.

#58. Datacentres to create 70% more carbon emissions by 2030, IEA report warns

Global energy watchdog forecasts huge rise as US and China turn to fossil fuels to power AI growth agendas

The world’s datacentres will create 70% more carbon emissions within the next four years as the US and China turn to fossil fuels to power their AI growth agendas, according to the world’s energy watchdog.

The International Energy Agency (IEA) warned that datacentres will consume twice as much electricity by 2030 compared with today, with the global expansion of artificial intelligence accounting for three-quarters of this growth.

The Paris-based agency found that the breakneck pace of the industry’s growth means that within four years AI datacentres will consume an extra 350 terrawatt-hours of electricity every year, or more than the annual electricity consumption of Great Britain.

Its influential annual report warned that the world’s energy future was less certain after the impact of the Middle East war, which it has described as the greatest energy market disruption in history.

“I don’t see any credible pathway for the energy world to return to the way it was before,” said the IEA’s executive director Fatih Birol.

Instead, it will be reshaped by the policy decisions governments make after the crisis to meet the increasing demand for energy to power a growing global population, the rising need for air-conditioning and a booming AI economy, the IEA said.

Datacentre developers have emerged as the world’s biggest corporate buyers of renewable energy to help fuel their servers which have a connection to local power grids. Despite this, the report showed that the appetite for fossil fuels is expected to grow even faster, particularly in the US and China.

By the end of the decade, around half of the electricity needed to power China’s AI datacentres will come from coal power plants, according to the IEA. The rest will come from renewable energy, with smaller amounts from nuclear reactors and very little from gas plants.

In contrast, the US is expected to rely on gas to provide about 60% of the electricity for its datacentres by 2030. There were plans for about 180GW of new gas-fired power plants linked to US datacentres in August this year, the agency said. That is almost as much new gas capacity as the total now installed across the European Union.

As a result of the growth in fossil fuel generation for datacentres, emissions associated with this sector were on track to rise by 70% by 2030, the IEA said.

The appetite for gas power among US datacentre developers is more than 16 times higher than in Canada, where the IEA identified plans for 11GW of gas generation to power their servers. In the rest of the world, AI developers have put forward plans for new gas plants with a combined capacity of just 4GW, the agency said.

This is because renewable energy is expected to play a greater role in supplying datacentres outside the US and China. In the EU, the IEA expects datacentres to rely on renewables for about 70% of their electricity by 2030 – followed by nuclear power, gas generation, and a very small amount of coal power.

The agency based its forecasts for the notoriously opaque industry on data showing the annual global shipment of AI servers, which have climbed eighteen-fold since 2020. It also used space-based imagery to track the buildout of 93 large “AI factories” around the world.

Its analysis suggests that the world’s datacentre capacity has climbed tenfold in the last 24 months to 17GW. By the time the AI factories were completed, their combined capacity was likely to be about 50GW, the IEA said.

The world’s existing stock of datacentres, including those with AI-optimised servers, now stands at 120GW, with 80% of this capacity in China, the US and the EU. A further 120GW will be built by the end of the decade under the IEA scenario often considered its central base case, of which three-quarters will be AI datacentres.

→ Baca artikel asli

#57. Putin vows 'far more painful' response to Ukraine's strikes on Crimean Bridge

Vladimir Putin spoke at a press conference following a historic summit with Xi Jinping and Donald Trump

Moscow's retaliation for attacks on the Crimean Bridge will be "far more painful" than Kyiv's strikes, Vladimir Putin has vowed.

In the early hours of Thursday, the double-arched, 12-mile bridge connecting Russia to Ukraine's occupied peninsula of Crimea was hit for a third time this week.

A combined missile-and-drone strike on Tuesday destroyed the central road section of the bridge, collapsing part of it into the water and rendering it unusable for road traffic.

But at a press conference after historic talks with his Chinese and US counterparts in Shenzhen, Russian president Putin said Moscow's response would be even more significant.

Putin claimed the bridge was used exclusively for civilian purposes and not to transport military equipment, as Kyiv has alleged, or petroleum products.

Since illegally annexing the Crimean Peninsula in 2014, Moscow has used it as a military base, with airbases, troop concentrations, training grounds and artillery stockpiles.

From there it has also carried out strikes on the rest of the country and resupplied its troops in southern Ukraine.

Commenting on the strikes on the bridge, Kyiv's defence ministry said that for Ukraine, they amounted to to "time gained and lives saved. The less that reaches the occupiers, the less hits our warriors."

The collapse of the Crimean Bridge's arch was the worst damage to be inflicted onto the structure in several years.

The bridge had long been in Ukraine's sights because it represents Moscow's pretensions over sovereign Ukrainian land, and because of its logistical importance in supplying Russian troops in the occupied Ukrainian southern regions.

It was built and unveiled to great fanfare in 2018, four years after Russia illegally annexed Ukraine's Crimea.

Thursday's strike was carried out with Neptune cruise missiles, Ukraine said. Russia's transport ministry said nobody had been hurt in the attack, and that specialists were assessing damage to the bridge. Train services on the bridge were suspended but resumed in the afternoon.

Car traffic, however, will be prohibited for the foreseeable future as a section about 200m (660ft) long of the tarmac was dragged below into the sea when missiles hit the arch on Tuesday.

President Volodymyr Zelensky called the initial strikes on the Crimean Bridge an "unprecedented operation" weeks in the making.

The seven-stage operation first required the destruction of air defence systems, radars, electronic warfare assets which protected the bridge. Missiles and drones then dealt the structure the final blow.

Final moments before stretch of Crimean Bridge collapsed after Ukrainian attack earlier this week

In response to Tuesday's hit, Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said the bridge "will be rebuilt, and it will be even better than before".

But the operation to repair it could take a long time, and it will likely have to be carried out under frequent Ukrainian fire.

Russia has regularly targeted Ukrainian civilian infrastructure since launching its full-scale invasion in 2022.

This week alone, as the Crimean Bridge was being hit, Moscow targeted key bridge crossings over the Dnipro River in Kyiv.

→ Baca artikel asli

#55. New page in history? Xi, Trump and Putin summit was more of a footnote

Far from the grand ambitions declared in Yalta in 1945, Shenzhen felt like a meeting in search of an agenda

It is arguably true, given it was the first such meeting of the leaders of China, Russia and the US, that Thursday’s gathering in Shenzhen deserved the description of “historic”.

But in terms of tangible outcomes, it is unlikely to warrant the “new page in the history of diplomacy” that Vladimir Putin claimed had been written, and will instead be consigned to a smallish footnote in any future volume of the 21st century.

It’s true that the meeting could yet be an icebreaker and the start of a permanent new dialogue. It’s also true that each leader can return home with something from the 90 minutes they spent together.

Despite falling asleep during the main Apec summit, Donald Trump had a chance to show his proximity to two authoritarian leaders he admires. With Ukraine having destroyed Russia’s bridge into Crimea, the US president greeted Putin as not just a friend but almost a wartime ally.

The Russian president, although occasionally pushed to one side by protocol during the early photo opportunities, will be able to show back home that it is Moscow, and not his adversary Europe, let alone Ukraine, that commands the respect and attention of Trump and Xi Jinping. As such, the summit was a free gift to Putin, and underlined that Trump was no longer the leader of the free world, as the former British foreign secretary Jeremy Hunt commented.

From Putin’s perspective, a meeting in Berlin of the coalition of the willing was a sideshow. Europe’s attempts to isolate Russia had failed and the Asia-Pacific region had become the centre of the global economy, Putin boasted. His relationship with Trump, he said, despite the difficulties, was founded on “similar civilisational values”.

For Xi, it was a prestigious chance to play host and implicitly demonstrate that the era of American hegemony is over. No longer the sole arbiter in world affairs, the US, the consumer economy, must share the stage with China, the manufacturing giant, and Russia, the energy bank.

The trio’s brief joint statement covered four pretty disparate topics: boosting energy supply, reforming the United Nations, limiting the powers of the international criminal court (ICC) and a tentative commitment to discuss nuclear disarmament and non-proliferation.

For grandeur at a time of various global crises, these ambitions hardly matched Yalta in 1945 or even the declaration after the Tehran summit in 1943, when the leaders of the UK, US and Russia sat on the veranda of the British embassy and pledged that their countries would work together after the war to build a “world family of democratic nations” free of tyranny and oppression. Winston Churchill declaimed in Tehran: “In our hands we have the future of mankind.” Joseph Stalin went further. “History has spoiled us,” he said. “She has given us very great power and very great opportunities.”

Shenzhen, by contrast, felt like a meeting in search of an agenda. The vacuum was filled with platitudinous commitments to “maintain strategic stability and international security”, a task that – looking around the world – none of those in attendance are currently pursuing with great effect.

Perhaps technology, multipolarity and their own divisions mean even they do not have “very great power”. Certainly there were no accompanying plans for how to reform the UN, let alone boost energy production or show respect for international law – a subject Trump’s current favoured successor, Marco Rubio, had just disparaged in a speech as a plaything of the global elite.

It was the summit’s chief instigator, Putin, who was the most eager of the three to set the meeting in a historic context, describing China, Russia and the US as the victors of the second world war, in a deliberate slight to Britain. A pledge to “preserve and strengthen the system of international law” seemingly referred to ensuring certain world leaders were freed from the prospect of the ICC serving them with arrest warrants.

By coincidence, the other world court, the international court of justice (ICJ), found a state – Myanmar – guilty of genocide for the first time on the same day. The Shenzen statement did not name the ICJ as a target, beyond an oblique reference to the ICC and “other similar institutions”. Doubtless that is only because ICJ rulings can be dismissed with near impunity.

Xi, nervous that China’s status as protector of the global south might be damaged by being involved in a global superpower carve-up, stuck to a familiar script: “History tells us that when major powers are at peace, the world is secure. When they clash, there is chaos.”

It may be that behind the scenes, or at other events, serious progress is being made on how China might act as a guarantor of a peace deal with Iran, or on who should lead the UN through its coming phase of reform, or even on how Putin might bring himself to seek peace in Ukraine in a way that protects Ukrainian sovereignty.

For the moment, though, it looks as if what little history was made in Shenzhen was made for exclusively the wrong reasons.

#52. Trump accused of falling asleep during Xi Jinping speech at Apec summit in China

US president appeared to doze off while Chinese leader made speech, but White House insisted he was awake

Donald Trump was, again, accused of falling asleep during a televised event, this time during the speech of the Chinese president, Xi Jinping, at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) summit in China.

The US president appeared to close his eyes for roughly 10 seconds on Thursday. As his eyes closed, Trump’s head tilted downward, and his hands remained clasped in front of him.

Several prominent Trump critics on social media alleged that the US president was asleep, including Republicans Against Trump, California governor Gavin Newsom’s press office and conservative lawyer George Conway.

White House officials denied the allegations, claiming the 80-year-old US president was looking down at papers in front of him.

“Fake News! President Trump was looking at papers in front of him while the speaker made remarks. You can clearly see his eyes move back and forth,” Steven Cheung, the White House director of communications, wrote on X in response to a Newsweek article.

“This is why the people despise media so much. They constantly lie,” Cheung added.

Trump often faces accusations of falling asleep during televised events such as Oval Office press conferences, cabinet meetings and sporting events.

Occasionally, while listening to other people speak, the US president will close his eyes for several seconds, appearing as though he is falling asleep. Despite footage of the instances going viral, the White House and Trump allies have insisted the US president does not fall asleep during meetings.

Administration officials have excused Trump’s apparent drowsiness as blinking or an example of the president listening intently. They consistently claim Trump “doesn’t sleep”.

Marco Rubio, the US secretary of state, testified to lawmakers earlier this year that he had “never seen” Trump fall asleep.

“On the contrary, the guy doesn’t sleep, which is a big problem because he calls me at two in the morning,” Rubio said in June.

Last year, JD Vance said that Trump hardly ever sleeps, even while traveling overnight.

“If we go on a 20-hour trip somewhere, he doesn’t sleep the entire time. And of course, if he’s not sleeping, if he’s working, he expects everybody else to be working too,” Vance told Fox News.

Trump became the oldest US president ever inaugurated when he was sworn in nearly two years ago. Despite his age, Trump often claims he is in near-perfect health, often bragging about his “perfect” cognitive test results and insisting he has a lot of energy.

#51. Dozens missing after boat carrying migrants sinks off Senegal's coast

The damaged boat washed ashore on a beach

Senegalese authorities are continuing search operations after a boat said to be carrying between 50 and 100 migrants capsized in the Atlantic Ocean.

Four bodies have reportedly been recovered so far. Dozens of people are still missing.

Personal belongings were found on the badly damaged boat, which washed ashore near the village of Yenne, south of Dakar. Search operations continued throughout Wednesday and were due to resume on Thursday.

This comes less than two weeks after a shipwreck off the coast of the north-western town of Thieppe killed at least 39 people, in one of Senegal's deadliest migration disasters.

Oumar Sow, a representative of the village chief, said the latest tragedy happened around 02:00 local time (02:00 GMT).

"Cries could be heard coming from the pirogue that were audible even from the beach," the AFP news agency quoted him as saying.

A survivor questioned by the police said there were about 100 people on the boat, including five women, AFP reported.

However, a local official told Reuters news agency it was carrying 50 people, adding that the boat hit a rock as migrants were transferring from small fishing boats to a larger vessel.

Police recovered identity documents, several mobile phones and personal belongings from the wreckage, including a school enrolment document.

At a cabinet meeting on Wednesday, President Diomaye Faye expressed concern about a renewed increase in irregular departure by migrants hoping to reach Europe.

He instructed the interior ministry to prepare a report examining the factors driving the dangerous journeys and proposing measures to tackle the problem.

Following last month's tragedy, Faye had urged Senegalese people not to risk their lives attempting the dangerous sea crossings.

Many thousands of people from West Africa have died or gone missing trying to flee conflict, authoritarian rule or poverty in recent years.

The UN's migration agency IOM said last month that it had recorded at least 454 deaths and disappearances off the West African coast this year alone.

It warned the figures were likely to be an underestimate, as many boats disappear without leaving survivors or a trace.

The Senegalese navy said it had rescued more than 1,400 people on Monday and Tuesday this week.

As it has become more difficult to reach Europe through North Africa, due to tighter controls and increased co-operation with European countries, migrants are increasingly targeting Spain's Canary Islands.

They often travel in wooden boats known as pirogues that are frequently overloaded and not suited to such long voyages across the Atlantic Ocean.

Most migrants arriving in the Canary Islands this year are from Senegal, according to the UN.

They are driven to leave home by limited employment opportunities, family pressures and established migration networks, according to the IOM.

On 7 November, El Hierro, one of the Canary Islands, declared a state of emergency following a surge in migrant arrivals.

It said more than 3,300 people had reached the island in about 30 boats since the beginning of the month. This amounts to almost a third of the island's population.

#48. Trump posthumously pardons US baseball legend Pete Rose

Rose was a 17-time Major League Baseball All-Star, but his legacy was tarnished by a lifetime ban

US President Donald Trump has posthumously pardoned baseball legend Pete Rose.

"I have just given a Complete and Total Pardon to the Late, Great Pete Rose, a true Baseball SUPERSTAR & LEGEND," Trump wrote on Truth Social on Thursday.

Rose, who died in 2024 at the age of 83, pleaded guilty in 1990 to two counts of filing false tax returns. He was also given a lifetime ban from Major League Baseball (MLB) after an investigation revealed he bet on games.

The baseball star was posthumously reinstated to MLB, in which he played for 24 seasons, last year and made eligible for the Baseball Hall of Fame.

Trump also said on Thursday that Rose's "record of 4,256 Hits in a Season will never be broken".

In fact, Rose accumulated a record 4,256 hits - which MLB defines as a batter striking the baseball into fair territory and reaching base without doing so via an error or a fielder's choice - during his career, which is more than any other player. He played a total of 3,562 games.

According to Trump, Rose "was treated terribly by Major League Baseball in not allowing him to go into the Baseball Hall of Fame. Politics, I guess".

The US Constitution gives a sitting president the broad power to grant a pardon, which represents legal forgiveness and ends any further punishment.

Because Rose's pardon was issued after his death, it is largely symbolic and does not change Rose's Hall of Fame status. The Department of Justice (DoJ) typically does not process posthumous pardon applications, though presidents have still issued them.

The BBC has contacted the Hall of Fame and the DoJ for comment.

The US president had previously floated the idea of pardoning Rose in March last year before the MLB reinstated him.

"Major League Baseball didn't have the courage or decency to put the late, great, Pete Rose, also known as 'Charlie Hustle,' into the Baseball Hall of fame," he wrote on Truth Social at the time.

"Now he is dead, will never experience the thrill of being selected, even though he was a FAR BETTER PLAYER than most of those who made it, and can only be named posthumously. WHAT A SHAME," Trump added.

MLB Commissioner Rob Manfred issued a decision in May 2025 that created a pathway for Rose to be eligible to be inducted into the baseball Hall of Fame - a ruling that also applied to 16 other people - as early as 2028.

In August 1989, Rose agreed to the permanent ban from the sport after MLB investigators found that he bet on his team, the Cincinnati Reds, multiple times while he was playing on and managing the team from 1985 to 1987.

In July 1990, after Rose admitted to failing to report $354,968 (Â£271,107) in income over a four-year period, he was sentenced to five months in prison. He also was fined $50,000 and ordered to perform community service at the time.

Major League Baseball legend Rose dies aged 83

#47. China, Russia and US agree peace declaration with no mention of Ukraine and Iran wars

Xi, Putin and Trump outline priorities that include UN reform, boosting oil supplies and ‘protection of diplomatic immunity’

China, Russia and the US say they have agreed a peace statement after an unprecedented trilateral summit, but the declaration made no mention of the wars in Ukraine and Iran caused by two of the participants.

The declaration on Thursday also outlined a set of priorities that included changing the UN, boosting oil supplies and what it said was the protection of diplomatic immunity.

The statement affirmed that “a nuclear war cannot be won and must not be fought”, but stopped short of suggesting the beginnings of a framework for denuclearisation.

The meeting between Xi Jinping, Vladimir Putin and Donald Trump has dominated the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) summit this week, with China, the host, promising it would be “groundbreaking”.

“We are really making history,” Trump told Xi and Putin at the start of the meeting. “No peace was ever forged by declining opportunities for engagement.”

Putin told a press conference later that the three men had had a “free discussion” and now had a “mutual understanding”.

Some had hopes that the talks, which lasted about one hour and 20 minutes, could be used to discuss reductions in arms between the world’s three largest nuclear powers. Beijing has previously rebuffed Trump’s advances to join such talks, pointing to the larger US and Russian stockpiles. On Thursday, though, Xi called for nuclear weapons to “be completely prohibited and thoroughly destroyed over time to make the world free of [them]”.

The language of the declaration around nuclear war is the same as that previously issued by the five permanent members of the UN security council, which includes France and the UK.

“The real test will be whether the three countries follow through on their stated commitment to discuss nuclear risk reduction and preventing nuclear proliferation,” said Patricia Kim, an expert on Chinese foreign policy at the Brookings Institution, a US thinktank. Even the suggestion that China would engage was significant, said Yun Sun, a senior fellow at the Stimson Center.

Analysts had said that the meeting’s primary significance was in its optics. “Overall, very little of substance seems to have been accomplished … This summit appears to have been all about the symbolism and prestige value,” said Prof William Hurst, from the University of Cambridge’s Centre for Geopolitics. Putin pushed back at the suggestion the summit had just been a photo-op. “It is a serious collaborative work,” he said.

In the declaration, the men “committed themselves to maintaining strategic stability and international security”.

First among their concerns was “promoting UN reform so that it may respond more effectively to global challenges”. Sun said this was a favourite topic of China’s and showed that Beijing was “flexing its diplomatic muscles”. She said: “It is able to set the agenda and the deliverables according to its priorities … and make other great powers accept them.”

The declaration also agreed that “the international criminal court [ICC] and other similar institutions must not abuse their jurisdiction”. Putin is subject to an ICC arrest warrant and so the clause added to the perception that he has done well from the summit. Since the 2022 invasion of Ukraine, the Russian president has been treated as an international pariah by Kyiv’s allies and he has not been to an Apec summit in years.

Hurst said: “This was no Yalta moment, but it was certainly a solid public demonstration of Russia’s (and Putin’s) continuing relevance and power. It’s yet another example of Putin playing a poor hand to maximum effect.”

Xi, his status as a global power broker now assured, began the morning by addressing Apec leaders, reiterating the themes of trade openness and innovation he has emphasised throughout the summit. However, Apec affairs appeared to take a back seat for most of the day, with business dominating the discussions. For all the camaraderie on display, relations are complex. Russia is under US sanctions over the Ukraine war, while China and the US spent much of the last year engaged in an escalating tit-for-tat trade war before Trump and Xi called a truce.

Trump and Putin earlier held a separate meeting – their first since August 2025 – where the US president called his Russian counterpart his “friend”.

European allies meeting in Berlin to show their support for Ukraine expressed indignation at the cosy optics. “Mr Putin is not our friend and ⁠will never be,” the Polish prime minister, Donald Tusk, said in a rejoinder to Trump. The Danish prime minister, Mette Frederiksen, said she found it “very incomprehensible” that a US ​president would want to sit down with Putin.

The peace declaration in Shenzhen said the three countries planned “to increase the production and supply of refined petroleum products for the benefit of all nations”. That was important, said Shan Guo from Hutong Research, a Shanghai-based advisory firm, but “now let’s see if the US will lift sanctions”.

Trump and Xi then held their third bilateral of this year. Xi said the US-China relationship was “generally stable and positive”, according to state media. But he cautioned Washington over Taiwan, saying the US “must keep doing the right thing to avoid major setbacks in US-China relations”.

Another watched-for topic lacking detailed findings was that of AI. Xi said the two countries should “enhance communication and coordination to guard against security risks … and ensure that AI remains under human control at all times”.

Ultimately, the lack of breakthroughs might suit China, which has no appetite to move beyond the role of convener, said Kim. “Beijing has little interest in taking on the diplomatic risks of brokering an actual peace agreement in Ukraine or resolving the conflict with Iran. That would entangle China in conflicts it would rather avoid,” she said.

#43. Judge finds Thomas Markle mentally fit to stand trial on child abuse charges

A judge in Florida has declared Thomas Markle Sr, the father of Meghan, Duchess of Sussex, to be mentally competent to stand trial for possessing indecent images of children.

The hearing came after an evaluation by a court-appointed psychiatrist conducted on Prince Harry's 82-year-old father-in-law.

Markle, who has denied possessing images of child sex abuse, was ordered to stay in jail pending trial earlier this month after allegedly removing a location monitoring device.

The FBI arrested Markle on 6 November at an elderly care home that specialises in dementia. His arrest came after a search of a storage unit he had allegedly rented in Los Angeles uncovered the abuse material.

Markle appeared in court in Tampa in a wheelchair, and his lawyers did not dispute the court-appointed psychiatrist's finding that he is mentally competent to understand the nature and consequences of the charge against him.

His next court hearing is scheduled for early December, but any trial is expected to take place in California, where the federal charge was filed.

Inside the storage units in Los Angeles the FBI says agents found about 78 items, including film negatives, slides and books that they allege contained child sex abuse material.

Witnesses "positively identified" Markle in two of the images believed to contain child sex abuse material.

In those images, the man pictured "appears to be in the age range of 30s to 40s", which suggests the alleged abuse dates back decades.

Markle has called the allegations "sick lies".

He moved to the Philippines last year after he had a series of strokes, and lived in Cebu until May. He was reportedly learning to walk again in Florida after having his leg amputated.

On Monday, police in the Philippines asked prosecutors to charge Markle with possession of and intent to distribute child sexual abuse material.

Last month, police there obtained a warrant to examine devices allegedly removed from Markle's former home in the country.

Four boxes of material, including computers, phones, hard drives and old video tapes, were handed to officers by Meghan's half brother Thomas Markle Jr.

The duchess has been estranged from her father since 2018, and he did not attend her wedding in London that same year.

The BBC understands she broke contact with him last year, telling him she and the authorities knew he had abused young girls.

In a previously shared statement, Meghan confirmed the existence of the letter and wrote: "I am deeply distressed... my thoughts are with any children who may have been harmed."

She said she hoped it could be understood why she had largely cut off contact from her father and did "not allow him to have contact with our children".
