#41. Isolated Brazilian tribe emerges amid alarm over threats to group’s way of life

Indigenous Awá people of Amazon make contact with villagers and accept food to take back to territory

About 20 Indigenous people emerged from isolation in Brazil’s Amazon rainforest, several organizations reported on Tuesday, raising the alarm about increased invasions into the tribe’s territory.

According to the NGO Survival International, members of the Awá people, who live in voluntary isolation, arrived on Monday in a village and made contact with a government team in a “historic encounter”.

The village they entered was within the Arariboia Indigenous territory in the north-eastern state of Maranhão, home to the Guajajara ethnic group.

Local community leaders said the villagers offered food to the group.

“They asked for yams and took them back into their forest,” said Survival International.

The NGO explained that the Awá typically subsist on hunting and gathering. However, they have faced increased incursions from illegal loggers, hunters and cattle ranchers.

A video of the encounter, filmed by Guajajara villagers and released by Survival International, shows a group of men, women and children at the edge of the forest.

Partially concealed by vegetation, they appear naked and are wearing necklaces. The men carry bows and long arrows.

“The physical condition of some members, particularly the children and the elderly, caused concern among the villagers, who noted clear signs of severe emaciation,” warned Coapima, an organization representing Indigenous groups in Maranhão.

Coapima highlighted the risks that unplanned contact poses to such groups, noting that they are particularly “exposed to diseases” against which they have little to no immunity.

Funai, the public agency responsible for Indigenous affairs, said in a statement sent to AFP that its technical teams were closely monitoring the situation and analyzing available information regarding the group’s health.

Survival International’s director, Caroline Pearce, said the group’s choice to emerge from isolation was a “troubling indication of how the continued invasion of uncontacted peoples’ forests puts otherwise self-sufficient Indigenous people in real danger”.

She said the Awá were being driven into “an ever-smaller island of the Amazon rainforest, surrounded by destruction”.

According to the NGO, about 196 “uncontacted” peoples and groups, who choose to live in isolation, have been identified across 10 countries.

More than 90% of such groups live in the Amazon rainforest, primarily in Brazil, with others in Indonesia and India.

The Forest Guardians – a group of Guajajara people who have battled illegal invasions for years – warned in comments in the Survival International statement that “climate change, fires, deforestation and land encroachment” were affecting the Awá’s way of life.

According to Survival International, many of the guardians have lost their lives trying to protect the territory they share with the Awá.

#38. China issues Mount Fuji warning as family of missing climber offers reward

The $75,000 reward offered by Chen Jie's family has sparked fears of more reckless climbing attempts

The Chinese embassy in Japan has warned its citizens against off-season ascents of Mount Fuji amid concerns that the hefty reward offered by a missing hiker's family could encourage more reckless climbing.

Chen Jie, 25, vanished on 8 November on the mountain. His family is offering 500,000 yuan ($75,000; Â£57,300) to anyone who finds him alive, and 100,000 yuan to anyone who recovers his body, say Chinese and Japanese media reports.

Strong winds and blizzards pose serious risks during the closed season, especially as shelter and first-aid centres are not operational, the embassy said on Tuesday.

Mount Fuji's official climbing season runs from around early July to early September.

Earlier this year, authorities of Yamanashi province, where the northern part of Mount Fuji sits, had said that people who attempt to climb Mount Fuji during off-peak seasons and then require rescue by helicopter will have to pay for the service from next year on.

Authorities will also require those looking to scale Fuji during the off-season to submit detailed climbing plans.

"We hope that these measures will cause people to rethink what climbing Mount Fuji during the winter is like," Kotaro Nagasaki, the prefecture governor, said at the time.

On Wednesday, Nagasaki told reporters that attempts to find the missing climber could lead to "secondary disasters"

According to Japanese news outlet the Mainichi Shimbun, Nagasaki appeared to appeal to Chen's family, saying: "I understand that your family is important, but please refrain from doing anything that could endanger others."

Some social media users in Japan have similarly expressed fears would entice people to venture into the mountains ill-prepared and lead to more accidents,

Yamanashi police said Chen had set out on his hike on 8 November with two friends, who turned back after they realised the mountain was closed and that they weren't suitably equipped for the conditions of the trails.

Chen pressed on alone from the fifth station of the Yoshida Trail, which sits at around 2,300m (7545ft) above sea level.

A friend last heard from Chen at 10:30 that day, when he sent a video message saying: "I'm going up the mountain... There's no one in sight."

The highest point of Mount Fuji, which is Japan's tallest peak, stands at 3,776m.

Local authorities launched a search for Chen on 10 November, but it had to be halted several times because of poor weather.

Chen's father reportedly arrived in Japan on 13 November. It is not clear when he posted the reward offer.

#37. Caribbean nations fear economic impact of shuttering citizenship program after EU threats

St Kitts and Nevis to wind down citizenship-by-investment program after EU warns of end to visa-free access

The decision by St Kitts and Nevis to end a lucrative programme offering citizenship to wealthy foreigners in exchange for investment threatens its economy and ability to support people in need, the premier of the island of Nevis has warned.

On Friday, Terrance Drew, the prime minister of the twin-island Caribbean nation, announced he would wind down the citizenship-by-investment (CBI) programme, after the European Union threatened to end visa-free access for St Kitts and Nevis.

Mark Brantley, the premier of Nevis, which has autonomous self-governance in some aspects, said he understood that the “country was given very little choice”, but did not “support the winding down of the program”.

The 42-year-old CBI programme, which St Kitts and Nevis pioneered, allows wealthy foreigners to obtain citizenship and ultimately a passport by investing in infrastructure and other development projects. It has brought billions of dollars into the country and in some years accounted for the largest share of government revenue.

In June, the European Commission wrote to the five Caribbean nations that currently operate CBI programmes: St Kitts and Nevis, Dominica, Antigua and Barbuda, Grenada and St Lucia. It warned that countries may lose visa-free access to its 29-country Schengen area if they did not phase out CBI.

In Friday’s statement, Drew blamed concerns over the programme on mismanagement by previous administrations, including years of underselling to undercut other countries, a controversial prison project, weak escrow protections and investment sums not being properly paid into the citizenship unit.

He said his government had led major reforms to the Caribbean’s programmes with international partners, including the US treasury, but argued that continuing the programme risked visa-free travel to parts of Europe and access to international banking. That could disrupt services allowing visitors to use overseas cards and families abroad to send money home.

Brantley, however, argued it was unfair for the EU to force St Kitts and Nevis to make a move without offering assistance to navigate the transition to other industries.

“I have not yet seen from the EU any corresponding suggestions of assistance. And we have to realise that these citizenship-by-investment programs, not only in St Kitts and Nevis, but … smaller countries of the Caribbean, have contributed significantly to our gross domestic product and also to ensuring that our people … can get adequate access to water and to education, to healthcare and the like,” he said.

Brantley described the 1 June 2028 deadline the EU gave the country to phase out the lucrative programme as “frankly ludicrous”.

In his statement, Drew said the CBI programme “did a lot of good, but it also created a danger”.

“When one source pays for half or more of everything, you are at its mercy. If it stops, everything stops. That is putting all of your eggs in one basket,” he added.

The prime minister said the government aimed to diversify its economy by strengthening tourism, yachting, and digital and creative industries, among others. He added that the sale of clean, geothermal energy was “at the heart of it” with drilling and exploration already planned.

“I will not pretend that the road is easy. Less CBI money means we must spend wisely and plan carefully. We will. Our plan is to make this change by growing our economy. Raising taxes is not part of that plan,” Drew said.

Antigua and Barbuda has also expressed willingness to end the programme, though it is trying to negotiate a longer transition period. The five Caribbean governments have taken differing positions on the EU demand. According to local media, Grenada has indicated it wants its programme to continue until 2032, while Dominica and St Lucia have responded to Brussels without publicly committing to ending their programmes.

Jermine Abel in Basseterre contributed reporting

#34. Hegseth uses Venezuela visit to boast of Maduro’s capture by US military

Defense secretary asserts US dominance on trip that would have seemed ‘impossible, almost unbelievable’ a year ago

Pete Hegseth has used his first visit to Venezuela since US special forces abducted its former president, Nicolás Maduro, to boast of the dictator’s capture during what he called “the single most sophisticated military operation in the history of Latin America”.

Maduro was controversially seized by Delta Force operatives in the early hours of 3 January, setting in motion an improbable rapprochement between Washington and Venezuela’s anti-imperialist “Chavista” regime, which has ruled since 1999.

Speaking outside the recently reopened US embassy in Venezuela’s capital, Caracas, on Wednesday, Hegseth said: “It’s a great honor to be here as the first United States secretary of war this century to set foot in Venezuela.​ Just one year ago my presence here … would have been deemed impossible, almost unimaginable.

“In fact, exactly one year ago today … [Maduro] stood on a stage not too far from here and yelled mockingly, ‘Come and get me!’” Hegseth recalled, before adding sarcastically: “Well, we answered that call.

“The reality is that … January 3 is the day that will go down in history as a day of liberation for Venezuela,” Hegseth claimed, although Maduro’s allies continue to hold power with US support.

Earlier in the day, Hegseth was welcomed to the Miraflores Palace by Maduro’s former vice-president, Delcy Rodríguez, who became acting president with Donald Trump’s blessing after his abduction.

In the aftermath of the deadly US attack, Hegseth was one of the bluntest Trump administration voices when it came to celebrating the invasion. “Maduro had his chance … he effed around and he found out,” the defense secretary bragged.

Speaking at the palace, Hegseth struck a more conciliatory tone as he vowed to deepen cooperation with Venezuela’s new leaders in brief comments that were broadcast by the state broadcaster VTV.

“I stand here on behalf of President Trump, who sends his greeting and looks forward to the opportunities, not just militarily … but also economically: the incredible resource capabilities that your country has, that we look forward to unlocking together with you,” Hegseth told Rodríguez and senior cabinet members, including the defense and foreign ministers, Gustavo González López and Oliver Blanco.

Hegseth said his visit to Rodríguez’s “beautiful country” was designed to re-establish and increase “defense cooperation” between “two great militaries” that less than a year ago were briefly at war. Earlier this month, the New York Times reported that Trump officials were trying to negotiate a long-term US troop presence in Venezuela as part of their push for regional hegemony.

“I appreciate your leadership and commitment to your country,” Hegseth told Maduro’s successor, a once outspoken anti-imperialist leftist who has overseen an unlikely realignment with Washington since her ally was grabbed.

During her five-minute address, Rodríguez, whose oil-rich country is widely seen to have become a US protectorate after January’s invasion, made no mention of Maduro, who is behind bars in New York City.

But referring indirectly to his capture and the subsequent reconciliation with Washington, she told Hegseth: “Thanks to the political and geopolitical maturity of our governments, we have – in a very short period of time, just a few months – turned what were very difficult and very tough times for our people, into an extraordinary space for cooperation and mutual benefit.”

Rodríguez hailed the “historic” energy deal her government recently struck with Trump, who has called it “the biggest oil deal in world history” as well as “the spoils” of his “conquest” of Venezuela.

Hegseth claimed the agreement, which reportedly involves 65bn barrels of reserves, “makes the strait of Hormuz a non-factor for the western hemisphere”.

Critics of Rodríguez’s political movement painted Hegseth’s visit as a humiliating act of genuflection for a president with no choice but to roll out the red carpet for the man whose troops captured her boss.

Writing on X, Luz Mely Reyes, a prominent Venezuelan journalist, said Hegseth’s visit was designed to underscore Washington’s “direct administration” of her country.

“Ten months after Nicolás Maduro’s capture, Washington no longer needs to fire shots to remind everyone who is in charge: the mere presence on Venezuelan soil of the architect of the January incursion reaffirms that the country’s operational sovereignty is subject to the White House’s directives,” Reyes said.

Reyes suspected the visit also carried a message for members of Venezuela’s armed forces. “Domestically, the trip forces the military high command to legitimize the man who directed the extraction of their former commander in chief,” she said. “In this regard, I wonder whether it might be a message of discipline intended to prevent any pockets of resistance in the barracks.”

Trump’s attempts to expand his Shield of the Americas anti-cartel coalition of Latin American governments suffered a major blow last month with the defeat of his far-right ally, Flávio Bolsonaro, in Brazil’s presidential election. The leftwing winner, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, has indicated his country will not join the initiative.

But Venezuela’s interim government is in little position to resist. During his surprise visit to Venezuela, Hegseth said Venezuela had gone from being “our most aggressive regional threat to [being] a compliant security partner”. He praised Rodríguez for working “closely, pragmatically, and diligently with the United States to stabilize Venezuela”. Hegseth claimed US and Venezuelan armed forces would work together to attack “narco-terrorists” operating on Venezuelan soil, vowing: “We will hunt you down. We will find you. And we will kill you.”

Concluding her speech at the palace, Rodríguez told her foe-turned-friend: “Thank you, secretary, and I wish you a lifetime of happiness.”

#33. Fiona the hippo dies after swallowing a hat at Cincinnati Zoo

From premature baby to social media star - Famed Cincinnati Zoo hippo Fiona dies

The Cincinnati Zoo in Ohio says its beloved hippopotamus Fiona has died just days after swallowing a knitted hat that slipped into her enclosure.

"It is with extreme sadness that we share with you the devastating news that Fiona did not make it after multiple attempts from our amazing vets and keeper team to help her through what ended up being a medical emergency," the zoo said on Thursday afternoon.

Fiona, who became a social media star after her premature birth in 2017, swallowed the hat about 10 days ago and required emergency care.

The vet staff "tried everything they could to help her" but "it was ultimately too much to overcome", the zoo said.

Fiona initially seemed fine after swallowing the hat more than a week ago

"We are in shock at the moment," Cincinnati Zoo director Dave Jenike said in a statement. "We did not expect this outcome. She fought until she couldn't anymore."

Michelle Curley, a spokesperson for the zoo, had said that the hat got into Fiona's enclosure by accident, after a visitor tossed it in the air and it caught on a ceiling fan that flung it through a small opening.

"We probably couldn't make [it] happen again if we tried," Curley said.

While she seemed fine at first, her appetite started to decrease a few days later, and the zoo staff tried to encourage Fiona to eat to help push the hat through her digestive system.

An ultrasound before her death showed the hat passing through Fiona's small intestine. A zookeeper said surgery was not an option for full-grown hippos and "our best chance for success with Fiona would be if she could pass this on her own".

The zoo also tried a different approach to attempt to get the hat to pass by encouraging Fiona to stand on a vibration plate platform borrowed from a local horse farm.

"Our animal care team was heroic in their efforts to save her. They did everything possible, but her body couldn't fight anymore," Jenike said.

Fiona was born premature at the Cincinnati Zoo in 2017 and at first struggled to survive. At birth, she weighed 13 kg (29 lbs), which the zoo said was about half the previous lowest recorded birth weight for her species.

Cincinnati's mayor said Fiona brought a smile to many faces

Her death is also very premature, with hippos normally living up to 50 years in human care, according to the zoo.

"Although this was not an intentional incident, of which, regrettably, there are many, Fiona's painful death is a tragic reminder that everyone should behave carefully and respectfully around animals," said Debbie Metzler, the managing director of captive wildlife for the animal rights group People for the Ethical Treatment of Animals (Peta).

"Peta expects that the zoo will review its protocols to help prevent anything like this from happening again, and we urge every visitor to secure their belongings and never toss objects near an animal's enclosure."

As well as being famous online, Fiona's story inspired an ice cream flavour and a character in Cincinnati's ballet.

Cincinnati Mayor Aftab Pureval said Fiona "was a symbol of perseverance and joy" in the city.

"She brought smiles to so many faces, from kids to parents to visitors from around the world," he wrote on X. "Our city is heartbroken, but we couldn't be more grateful that she was part of our lives and our community as a whole."

Fiona the hippo undergoing care after swallowing a hat at Cincinnati Zoo

#30. Florida pastor found guilty over 2021 assassination of Haitian president

Christian Emmanuel Sanon faces life in prison after conviction on charges arising from killing of Jovenel Moïse

A Florida pastor with dual Haitian-American citizenship was convicted on Wednesday of US charges arising from the 2021 assassination of the Haitian president Jovenel Moïse.

Christian Emmanuel Sanon was found guilty on charges including conspiring to kill Moïse after a nearly eight-week trial in federal court in Miami. He had faced a total of eight charges including conspiracy to kidnap or kill a person outside the United States.

He faces up to life in prison at sentencing.

Federal prosecutors said south Florida was a hub for planning and financing the plot to oust Moïse and install a president of the conspirators’ choosing. The government said Sanon was the conspirators’ initial choice for Moïse’s replacement. Prosecutors also said Sanon helped raise money for the conspiracy and arranged travel to Haiti and lodging for himself and others in that country.

Sanon acknowledged in trial testimony that he was in communication at one point with people trying to replace Moïse, but pointed out that the group had moved on to another candidate long before the assassination. He denied any knowledge of plans to kill Moïse.

Sanon was born in Haiti and moved to the US as a teenager. He testified that he worked with churches in Haitian immigrant communities in Florida and the midwest.

Haiti has not had a president since authorities said that a squad of mostly retired Colombian soldiers killed Moïse and wounded his wife in a night-time raid at their home on 7 July 2021. The poorest country in the Americas, Haiti has a history of dictatorship, political upheaval and foreign intervention that has long hindered democratic rule.

The election of a new president is scheduled for next year.

Prosecutors said the scheme to kill Moïse included paying about two dozen foreign mercenaries with money obtained from US government programs that were intended to help businesses during the Covid pandemic, according to court records. US prosecutors have charged more than 40 people with crimes related to the assassination and a dozen have been convicted, including five who are serving life sentences.

The plot to oust Moïse began taking final shape in April 2021, when Sanon met in south Florida with a former Haitian senator and several US businesspeople, according to an indictment. The group agreed that Sanon, once in power, would award contracts to two Florida-based companies – Counter Terrorist Unit Federal Academy and Counter Terrorist Unit Security, collectively known as CTU – for infrastructure projects, security forces and military equipment, according to investigators.

CTU initially hired about 18 Colombian nationals with military training to provide security for Sanon, according to prosecutors. Conspirators also spent months obtaining weapons and body armor and attempting to build ties with Haitian gangs, officials said.

By June 2021, the conspirators realized Sanon had neither the constitutional qualifications nor sufficient popular support to become president. They shifted their support to Wendelle Coq Thélot, a former Haitian supreme court judge who died in January 2025 while a fugitive.

Sanon was previously scheduled to be tried with four other men who were convicted earlier this year, but his case was separated from theirs and delayed for health reasons.

#28. California targets Nvidia in China AI chip probe

California has served an investigative subpoena on the AI chip designer Nvidia in the wake of a scandal that ended in the departure of longtime CEO Jensen Huang.

The state's top law enforcement officer, attorney general Rob Bonta, said he is probing whether Nvidia or Huang's actions resulted in China obtaining advanced chips or confidential information, adding that such actions could violate California law if the company is found to have mislead investors and the public.

The BBC has contacted Nvidia for comment.

"Nvidia is in a messy, transnational situation but the underlying principle of our corporate securities law is clear: Companies cannot mislead their investors," Bonta said on Thursday.

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

Nvidia has its global headquarters in Santa Clara, California.

Earlier this month, it emerged that Huang had an extramarital relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang, who was an executive at the Singapore-based data centre company Megaspeed.

Some members of the US Congress have urged the Department of Commerce to investigate whether Megaspeed, a major Nvidia customer, diverted Nvidia chips to China.

Megaspeed has previously said it is aware of reports "containing assertions about our business, supply chain and global operations that are not supported by verifiable evidence".

Nvidia shares have plummeted 22% since the New York Times reported on the affair in early November.

Earlier this week, the company's board chair Harvey Jones said Nvidia had no intention of reinstating Jensen Huang after the longtime Nvidia boss and co-founder issued a statement saying he would be "honoured" to return, as some tech leaders including Elon Musk campaigned on his behalf.

Huang has acknowledged engaging in extramarital affairs â and left the company last week after reports that he had relationships with subordinates.

Nvidia says it won't reinstate Huang as it reports soaring sales

#26. Race to be next UN secretary general exposes divisions among world powers

Debate about dominance of permanent five members could reignite if major powers block Costa Rican frontrunner

The race to become the next UN secretary general could reach a decisive moment on Friday when a UN security council vote is likely to reveal whether the Costa Rican frontrunner for the post would be blocked by the big powers. If so, the setback will reignite a debate about the dominance of the permanent five (P5) members at the United Nations.

Rebeca Grynspan, an economist and UN insider who is from a family of Polish-Jewish wartime refugees to Costa Rica, has been in the lead in all but one of the eight straw polls of the 15 UN security council members since the first secret ballot was held on 30 July. Each member goes down the list of candidates, casting an “encourage”, “discourage” or “no opinion” vote for each candidate.

Despite countries not being required to reveal how they voted, it is thought Grynspan’s candidature has been resisted by Russia and the US, two of the five permanent security council members that can ultimately wield a veto against a candidate. Knowledge among the security council delegations of how countries are voting is down to private intelligence, leaks and a process of elimination.

The intensive lobbying over the job has spread across the globe, but Friday is especially important since the poll is slated to differentiate the voting of the P5 from the 10 elected members (E10) of the security council. To be recommended as secretary general to the UN general assembly, a candidate needs nine votes and no vetoes. In Friday’s colour-coded secret ballot, the P5 will cast red ballots and the E10 white.

One diplomat said: “The electoral system means it is important how many votes you win, but equally how many of certain votes you don’t lose.”

The protracted 140-day deadlock confirms the polarisation that has hampered the incumbent secretary general, António Guterres, but also belies claims the role is now an irrelevance.

Friday’s secret ballot may also reveal whether the United Arab Emirates’ campaign for its diplomat, Lana Nusseibeh, is about to blow up in its face due to the hostility to the UAE’s brand of active, commercially driven diplomacy, not just in Sudan, but also Somalia, Western Sahara, Libya and Yemen.

A late entrant, in the two straw polls in which she has contested so far, Nusseibeh secured only seven “encourage” votes, six “discourage” and two “no opinions”. This is despite prestigious big-power backing and her acknowledged personal diplomatic skills.

Nusseibeh has US and Russian backing. The French foreign ministry may have misgivings about the UAE, but Emmanuel Macron, the French president, travelled to the UAE in the midst of French riots to see the UAE president, Sheikh Mohamed bin Zayed Al Nahyan, and secure 100,000 metric tons of diesel a month.

In the UK, the foreign secretary, Ed Miliband, has kept his counsel, but he shares a Polish-Jewish refugee heritage with Grynspan and cannot be an admirer of the UAE’s human rights record or its evasion of EU sanctions on Russia. The prime minister, Andy Burnham, has UAE connections from his time as mayor of Manchester, and Nusseibeh is a fellow alumnus of the University of Cambridge. At the UN she worked closely with the UK in an informal group called “Friends of the Future of the UN”.

Yet 68 British MPs, including the Labour chair of the international development select committee, Sarah Champion, last week signed a letter calling on Burnham to veto Nusseibeh, describing her as “a tainted and compromised figure”.

Some of the opposition to her from elected security council members is not wholly political. Latin American countries believe an informal rotation system means it is their turn to lead the UN. As a result Panama and Colombia, currently on the security council, will oppose her. China has also been supportive of this rotation principle.

But others deeply object to what they consider to be the UAE’s dollar diplomacy and preparedness to use its extraordinary wealth to exacerbate and exploit pre-existing divisions in a country to gain a commercial foothold. Somalia, for instance, has a seat on the Security Council, and its president, Hassan Sheikh Mohamud, this week went on TV to accuse the UAE of buying ports, establishing military bases and supporting armed forces outside Somalia’s national security framework without federal approval.

It is also possible that Finland and Latvia, the two elected members with the greatest stake in Ukraine, are unimpressed that the UAE gave Vladimir Putin red carpet treatment when he visited Abu Dhabi in December 2023, nine months after the international criminal court had issued an arrest warrant for child trafficking. Greece, less engaged in Ukraine, may be more flexible.

Since the last poll, Nusseibeh has been to Bahrain, Britain and Pakistan, a clue as to where extra votes may be harvested. She also had a “thoughtful exchange” with Zeng Jixin, the Chinese ambassador to the UAE.

The two hurdles are whether someone so deep inside the UAE inner circle would have the courage in office to show true independence, and whether her active denials of UAE involvement in arming the RSF in Sudan leave her irretrievably tainted. From a Palestinian intellectual family, she insists she would make peace in Sudan her number one priority.

In the last ballot on 5 November, Grynspan won 12 votes in favour and three negative. But on 16 October – the only colour-coded ballot so far – which distinguishes between permanent and elected members – she received two discourage votes from permanent members, the equivalent of a veto. Nusseibeh had not joined the race at that point.

In an administration as personalised as the US, it has been hard for Grynspan to gain influence, with many sensing that Mike Waltz, the US ambassador to the UN, is the key influencer.

Grynspan has been reluctant to make the fact she is Jewish a feature of her candidacy, but the election of a Jewish person to run the UN might be seen as something the US could embrace as transformative. Her age – she is 70 – could be considered a drawback among those calling for a younger generation to renew the institution.

Although there are other Latin American candidates, Grynspan is seen as a more viable candidate than her rival, the Argentine Rafael Grossi, the head of the UN weapons inspectorate. Russia has opposed Grossi, objecting to his interventions over the Zaporizhzhia nuclear plant in Ukraine.

One way or the other, the logjam has to be broken soon.

#24. Trump, Putin and Xi release 'peace declaration' after historic meeting

Watch: What did Trump, Xi and Putin get out of their historic meeting?

US President Donald Trump and his Russian and Chinese counterparts Vladimir Putin and Xi Jinping have issued a "joint declaration of peace" following historic trilateral talks.

The three leaders met for 90 minutes in China's south-eastern city of Shenzhen, on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) summit.

While the presidents have previously had one-on-one meetings, these are their first trilateral talks.

In the declaration, they said "nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought" and that the countries planned to increase the production and supply of petroleum. They also said they were "committed to reform the UN" to respond more effectively to global challenges.

Thursday's statement also flagged the issue of immunity enjoyed by state officials and said "they agreed that the International Criminal Court and other similar institutions must not abuse their jurisdiction".

Trump praised the trilateral meeting calling it "a great and highly consequential trip", in a social media post while flying back to Washington on Air Force One.

In the same post, the president announced he would host a news conference to talk about the trip on Friday at the White House.

While in China, Trump met Putin ahead of the trilateral, in their first meeting since August 2025, where the US president greeted his Russian counterpart as his "friend".

He also spoke with Xi in a separate conversation, describing the pair's relationship as "very special".

Beyond the warmth and shared niceties, however, the three men had different goals and motivations for the sit-down.

Analysis: Trilateral summit high on spectacle, short on substance

This is a remarkable diplomatic moment for China's Xi - but it comes with pitfalls

The opening remarks of the trilateral gave some indication about what was to be discussed during the closed-door meeting.

Before giving the floor to his fellow leaders, Xi said that "positive interaction among major powers is not a matter of choice, but a mandatory requirement".

Putin followed this by highlighting a shared interest in ending the Ukraine war and said the conversation would also touch on food and energy security.

China has officially remained neutral since Putin launched a full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, rejecting accusations from Kyiv and its allies that it has been tacitly supporting Moscow, including by exporting dual-use technology used by the Russian military.

Beijing has also repeatedly called for negotiations and a political settlement while avoiding condemnation of Russia's aggression.

Trump greets 'my friend' Putin ahead of meeting with Xi

Meanwhile, Trump closed by saying the presidents of three of the world's largest countries have a "special responsibility" to maintain productive dialogue.

"Too often past leaders have avoided talking to nations with whom they had disagreements," he said, adding: "We feel the opposite."

Trump referenced the three countries' nuclear capabilities and said they are "uniquely positioned to discuss how to reduce the number of nuclear weapons on Earth".

It is "vital" that they co-operate to "combat the threat of nuclear proliferation in other countries around the world", he said, adding: "That's what we're doing in Iran."

"We're not going to let Iran have a nuclear weapon".

Trump then turned to Ukraine and said he and Putin have had a "long discussion" on the conflict there already.

He added that Putin "and I hope the other side, would like to see an end to that war".

Putin addressed the media following the trilateral meeting and described it as an "important chapter in the history of diplomacy".

He said Ukraine and Europe were not yet ready to make compromises to resolve the conflict in Ukraine - but he said Russia had invited EU representatives to Moscow to better understand Europe's position.

As the talks were under way in China, and even as Putin was holding his press conference claiming to be ready for peace, Ukraine's capital Kyiv was under attack from Russian drones.

Putin has repeatedly rejected all ceasefire offers from Ukraine, insisting on his maximalist demands which Kyiv and its European allies say are tantamount to de facto capitulation of Ukraine.

In Shenzhen, the Kremlin leader also vowed a "far more painful" retaliation in response to Ukrainian attacks on the bridge connecting Russia to Ukraine's occupied peninsula of Crimea. A missile and drone attack on Tuesday destroyed its central road section.

There was no mention from Putin of almost five years of Russian attacks on power plants, bridges and residential buildings in Ukraine.

The Russian president also directly addressed comparisons between his meeting with Trump and Xi and the 1945 Yalta Conference, where the leaders of the Soviet Union, Britain and the US met towards the end of World War Two.

"Nobody divided up the world at Yalta. It simply codified the realities that had emerged after WW2.

"And when it comes to today's conflicts, the realities on the ground will have to be accepted."

The three presidents spoke behind closed doors at the Kylin Villa, the exclusive resort in Shenzhen

The Kremlin released a video showing Putin leaving the summit venue and saying goodbye to Xi.

"Thank you for organising this," Putin said. "I don't think anyone even expected everything to go so well."

Meanwhile, China's president described his meeting with Putin and Trump as "positive, constructive, and highly productive", according to the country's state broadcaster CCTV.

It added that Xi recalled "the history of fighting side-by-side during WW2", as the three leaders emphasised the importance of "cherishing the hard-won peace".

Thursday's trilateral meeting was reportedly the idea of Putin.

Some Russian newspapers called it a "diplomatic victory" as after Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine many Western leaders were trying to isolate him.

The summit also marked the first time since Russia's war in Ukraine began that Putin took part in an international gathering attended by leaders from countries including Canada, Japan and Australia.

Russian 'nuclear briefcase' spotted at APEC summit

US lawmakers responded with frustration to Trump's warmth towards America's geopolitical adversaries.

"I was encouraged by some of the commitments that came out of the President's meetings in Asia, particularly the recognition that nuclear war must never be fought," Republican Senator John Curtis said in an emailed statement to the BBC.

But Curtis also said he was concerned about Trump's approach to the foreign leaders, adding that "Vladimir Putin and Xi Jinping are not our friends".

"We should not treat them as though they share our values or interests," Curtis, who sits on the Senate foreign relations committee, added.

"Doing so risks undermining our national security, weakening trust with our allies, and sending the wrong message about where America stands."

Jeanne Shaheen, a ranking Democratic member on the same committee, chose harsher words in her comment.

"President Trump's 'friend' is a murderous war criminal," she wrote on X.

With additional reporting from Brandon Drenon

#21. Europe ready for talks with Russia on ending war in Ukraine, leaders say

European leaders have said they are ready for negotiations with Russia on ending the war in Ukraine, in a strong show of support for Kyiv.

"Europe has a clear and consistent and united negotiating position," UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham said, while German Chancellor Friedrich Merz stressed that Moscow's "maximalist demands are not realistic".

They were speaking at Berlin's summit of Ukraine's allies known as the Coalition of the Willing.

Donald Trump's son-in-law Jared Kushner, one of the two US envoys who were in Berlin, said the US president wanted to make a deal with Moscow "with Europe by his side". Russia's Vladimir Putin has so far rejected any European role in the US-led peace talks.

Trump had earlier held historic trilateral talks in Shenzhen, south-eastern China, with Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping and later dialled into the Berlin summit from Air Force One.

In a lengthy press conference following his talks with Trump and Xi, Putin addressed the question of multilateral peace talks, saying the Americans were "insistently" asking Russia to allow the Europeans to take part.

Putin claimed Russia was not against the idea but said Moscow wanted to clarify "what Europe will bring to these talks".

"That is why, through diplomatic channels, we have invited the Europeans to come to Moscow in order to determine their positions. And after that, we will decide in what format we will continue the dialogue with Ukraine."

But Putin also said that "every day" Russia heard that European leaders were talking about war coming in 2030. "But we are not planning to attack anyone... Is that normal? I do not think so," he said. Still, Putin added, Russia understood that "discussing issues linked to security in Europe is pointless without the Europeans".

Jared Kushner told reporters that the US president had come away from Shenzhen believing that "peace is achievable".

Striking an upbeat note, Kushner said that the Russians were willing to trust the instinct of the US envoys "on our behalf" that the Europeans should be at the table and that Moscow was now "comfortable" with Ukraine having security guarantees based on the idea enshrined in Nato's Article Five that requires allies to come to a country's defence if it comes under attack.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky, who also was at the summit in Germany's capital, has long sought a guarantee equivalent to Nato's collective defence clause, and there has been little indication that Putin would accept that kind of condition.

Russia rejects outright the idea of Ukrainian membership of Nato, but reports say US negotiators have mulled the idea of giving Kyiv Article Five-style guarantees without being part of the Western defensive alliance if it agrees to painful territorial concessions.

Zelensky and the three European leaders â Merz, Burnham and French President Emmanuel Macron â were not as upbeat as Kushner, and the Ukrainian president made clear that the Russian president had spurned every proposal so far: "Putin has always said no."

The German chancellor was equally sceptical, although he said with US involvement the chance of peace was increasing. Europe was willing to enter "serious discussions on European security" with Russia, he explained, "as long as it's matched with a serious discussion and only first a peace agreement with Ukraine".

"These talks have to begin, and we will accept if conditions are acceptable for Europe and Ukraine," said Merz.

He also said any attempts to divide Europe into spheres of influence run by major powers was unacceptable, amid concerns on the continent that this issue may have been on the agenda at the trilateral talks in Shenzhen.

Analysis: Trilateral summit high on spectacle, short on substance

"A 'Yalta format' will not bring peace," Merz said, referring to the post-World War Two agreement between the US, the USSR and Britain in 1945.

He also sought to downplay the Shenzhen summit as above all "a photo opportunity."

"The US understands that there can be no peace without us. We, in turn, know that with the United States by our side, the prospects for peace increase," Merz added.

Polish Prime Minister Donald Tusk also took exception to Trump's description of Putin as a "friend" at the Shenzhen summit, saying: "Mr Putin is not our friend and will never be."

Meanwhile, Macron urged Ukraine's allies to do the "maximum" to deliver the much-needed air defence systems Kyiv needed to repel deadly Russian attacks.

The French leader also urged the allies to "reopen the question" of Ukraine's membership in Nato.

There have been mixed messages from Moscow on European participation to the talks. Last week, following a visit to Moscow, the US envoys, Steve Witkoff and Kushner, appeared certain that Europe would soon have a seat at the negotiating table.

But the Russians were more circumspect and this week Putin's aide Yury Ushakov said a "great deal" would have to be done before the format could be expanded, suggesting that several more rounds of discussions would have to be held before any four-sided talks could take place.

Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022.

Several buildings were damaged in the latest Russian attack on Ukraine's capital Kyiv

While Russia has engaged with several rounds of talks over the last year, little in Moscow's rhetoric or actions suggests that the Kremlin is considering scaling down its war on Ukraine.

It continues to pound Kyiv's energy infrastructure to cripple it as winter takes hold, increasing numbers of civilian casualties are being recorded across the country, and Kremlin officials have not budged from Russia's demands for Ukrainian sovereign territory.

As delegations were meeting in China and Germany on Thursday, several buildings in Kyiv's historic centre came under attack from drones. One person was killed in a strike on a residential building in the Podilskyi neighbourhood, while the Dutch and Austrian embassies as well as Saint Volodymyr's Cathedral reported damage.

And Putin also vowed that Moscow's response to a Ukrainian strike on the Crimean Bridge linking Russia with occupied Crimea would be "considerably more painful" than what Kyiv had achieved.

He also said he had discussed a "possible ceasefire in a number of areas" but had decided against it after the attack on the bridge.

Still, Putin claimed to be "interested" in ending the war and was ready to make compromises, while Ukraine and Europe were not.

"I assure you, we have no desire at all to continue this armed conflict," Putin said, despite the fact that Russia has in recent months intensified its attacks on Ukraine's critical infrastructure as cold winter approaches.

Kyiv and its European allies say that â in the absence of any major success on the vast front line â the Kremlin leader is seeking to break Ukrainian resistance by leaving millions of civilians without electricity and water supply.

#20. Voyager 1 spacecraft reaches one light-day milestone

NASA's Voyager 1 probe, which has been traveling through space for nearly 50 years, hit a milestone distance of one light-day away from Earth on Wednesday. Already the most distant man-made object in the universe, the spacecraft is now about 26 billion kilometers (16 billion miles) away, according to the US space agency. That is the distance a particle of light can travel in 24 hours. FRANCE 24's Emerald Maxwell reports.

#19. Five EU states announce plans for immigration ‘return hubs’ in Rwanda

Germany, Austria, Netherlands, Denmark and Greece likely to face challenges to policy criticised as ‘grave moral failing’

Five EU member states have announced plans to set up an offshore centre in Rwanda for people with no legal right to stay in Europe, in a pilot scheme that is expected to face legal challenges and has been denounced as “a grave moral failing”.

The five countries – Germany, Austria, the Netherlands, Denmark and Greece – are the first to seek to use the EU’s newly agreed returns law that allows people who have exhausted all avenues of appeal against their removal from Europe to be sent to a third country.

In a statement released on Monday the five EU countries said “concrete negotiations with partner country Rwanda will begin for implementation”, saying the scheme would make “an important contribution to further curbing illegal migration, dismantling the business model of smugglers and relieving the burden on our population”.

Officials hope to send 100 people to Rwanda from early next year to test the model. The scheme will apply to adults whose claims to asylum have been rejected or others with no legal right to live in the EU.

In theory, according to diplomatic sources, migrants would stay in a reception centre in Rwanda for a couple of months, before choosing to integrate into life in the Great Lakes country or return to their home nation.

Unlike the failed scheme pursued by the British Conservative government in Rwanda, or Italy’s agreement with Albania, it will not apply to people pursuing asylum claims.

The reception centre, which would be an open facility, was not intended to house people long term but to accommodate regular flows of people, the countries said. Diplomatic sources said the EU countries were not using British-funded facilities built in Kigali.

The governments involved argue it will protect the European asylum system while removing the incentive for irregular migrants to come to Europe.

Denmark’s immigration minister, Morten Bødskov, whose government has pioneered a tough line on migration widely emulated across the EU, said it was “a huge breakthrough”.

The German interior minister, Alexander Dobrindt, said: “Return hubs offer a positive approach for people without the right to stay and with no prospects in Europe, whose countries of origin do not cooperate in the repatriation process.”

Andy Burnham told reporters last week that the UK had not had direct discussions with European governments involved in return hubs but “we will watch that with interest, learning from what went on in the past. The public want us to do whatever we can to deal with illegal immigration.”

Critics have highlighted the ethical difficulties of striking an agreement with Rwanda’s repressive government, which was found in 2024 by the US state department to allow state security forces and civilian officials to commit human rights abuses with impunity, while also raising concerns about freedom of expression and forced labour.

Spain’s socialist prime minister, Pedro Sanchez, said on Monday the move had no “future or humanity”. “I don’t see any future or humanity in that. It’s not the solution,” Sanchez, an outspoken defender of immigration in a Europe that has tilted to the right in recent years, told Telecinco television.

The French Green MEP Mélissa Camara denounced the agreement between the five countries and Rwanda as “an agreement of shame” and “a grave moral failing”. She said: “Under this agreement, people will be deported thousands of kilometres away from the European Union, with no guarantees regarding the compliance with their rights.”

Alain Berset, the secretary general of the Council of Europe, the continent’s human rights body, said it was not possible “to be so precise a priori” about what any return hubs proposal meant. Speaking to the Guardian before the announcement, he said all members of the Council of Europe were committed to the European convention on human rights “and this must be the lighthouse” for all discussions on return hubs.

“On concrete cases, then we will have to see if this is compliant with the convention,” he said. He stressed that “all countries must be able to define their migration policy”.

Officials involved in setting up the migration centres expect legal challenges, but say the scheme is grounded in human rights law.

Rights organisations say the scheme is bound to fail. “Member states may call them ‘return hubs’ or even ‘welcome centres’, but no euphemism can hide the violence of uprooting people from the life they’ve been building in Europe, and sending them to a country they don’t even know, without family, networks or meaningful prospects,” said Chiara Catelli of Picum, a pan-European organisation that campaigns for the rights of undocumented migrants.

#16. European Commission accused of sugarcoating figures to release funds to Egypt

Leaked documents raise questions about claims Egypt met welfare targets to qualify for €2.5bn in loans

The European Commission has been accused of sugarcoating figures to enable it to release funds to Egypt under a landmark pact with the authoritarian government of Abdel Fatah al-Sisi.

The charge relates to a €7.4bn deal promised by Ursula von der Leyen, the European Commission president, to al-Sisi on a high-profile visit to Cairo in 2024. The three-year “strategic and comprehensive partnership” was intended to bolster Egypt’s shaky economy, deter migrants from coming to Europe and shore up a region reeling from the 7 October attacks and Israel’s war on Gaza.

The disclosure raises broader questions about the EU-Egypt relationship, as Egyptian authorities face scrutiny over shrinking press freedom following the jailing and alleged torture of six journalists, the entire newsroom of Matsadaash, a factchecking and investigative platform, and the closure last month of one of the country’s last independent news outlets and conviction of its high-profile editor, Lina Attalah.

Attalah is appealing against that conviction and will be in court this week as she seeks to overturn other charges including “publishing false news”, for which she was sentenced to a year in prison in absentia without her knowledge.

The spotlight is on €5bn in EU “soft” loans to Egypt – lending with easy repayment terms, known as macro-financial assistance, that is meant to be available only to countries that respect human rights and democracy. While the EU conditions on democracy and rights are vague, the loans impose much more specific requirements on Egypt to carry out economic reforms.

Separate data sources – from leaked EU documents and deleted from an Egyptian government website – raise questions about the European Commission’s claims that Egypt met targets linked to social welfare programmes, which enabled the EU to release €2.5bn in assistance.

In confidential documents, the commission told EU lawmakers that Egypt had “broadly fulfilled” and then “fulfilled” two separate targets to increase the numbers of poor people on social welfare programmes between 2024 and 2026. But data points to a net reduction of 85,734 households as the Egyptian government removed people from its welfare programmes rather than expanded coverage.

If Egypt had expanded the programmes in line with the EU’s apparent conditions – and not removed any households – about 245,000 households, equivalent to roughly 909,000 people, would be getting these benefits. (The Guardian used World Bank data to make this rough calculation).

According to the leaked documents, the commission wanted Egypt to “further expand cash transfer programmes”, but despite a net reduction in beneficiaries it gave the green light anyway to two separate “soft” loans totalling €2.5bn.

In response to the Guardian’s findings, one of the European parliament’s leading spokespeople on EU-Egypt relations, the Dutch MEP Tineke Strik, said she was introducing a question to the European Commission demanding clarification.

“The finding by this investigation that the commission is seemingly sugarcoating Egypt’s performance is worrying,” Strik said. “From the beginning, the commission has used macro-financial assistance primarily as a tool to lure Egypt into migration cooperation, while considering formal conditions and its reporting obligations towards parliament as an inconvenient formality.”

Under the plan, Egypt has so far received €3.5bn in soft loans in three instalments, with a final payment of €1.5bn expected before the end of 2027.

The charge of sugarcoating figures relates to two social welfare programmes: the Takaful and Karama cash transfer programmes for poor and vulnerable households.

The Takaful (Solidarity) programme gives poor families cash payments as long as recipients have regular health visits and good attendance at school. The Karama (Dignity) programme gives unconditional cash payments to people living with a disability, older people, orphans and widows.

One of the EU conditions for Egypt to gain the first €1bn loan was to increase the number of Takaful and Karama beneficiary households by 100,000 to 4.7 million.

In a leaked report to lawmakers in the EU council of ministers and the European parliament, the commission described that requirement as “broadly fulfilled”.

The data in that report, however, reveals a net fall of 62,281 households during this period, owing to what the EU officials describe as “a multi-annual recertification process”. Some beneficiaries were added to the list but a larger number were removed because they were deemed ineligible.

In order to achieve a later instalment of €1.5bn, Egypt was required to “further expand” the programmes, adding 60,000 households to the Takaful and Karama beneficiaries list. A second leaked report to EU lawmakers shows that the commission concluded the target was “fulfilled” because 442,118 families were added to the list.

On this occasion, the commission did not report on numbers removed. Figures from Egypt’s ministry of social solidarity, however, show a net reduction of 23,453 beneficiaries to the programmes in the 11 months until June 2026, the period leading up to the release of that payment. The EU announced the release of the €1.5bn payment in July 2026.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

The data has since been deleted from the Egyptian ministry website but the Guardian has seen screenshots and archived webpages.

During a period from 2024 to 26 when the EU required Egypt to add 160,000 households to the rolls of the Karama and Takaful programmes, there was a net decline of 85,734 households.

Timothy Kaldas, the deputy director of the Tahrir Institute for Middle East Policy, said the commission had generally “done a poor job of leveraging the funds of European taxpayers to support genuine stabilisation” on the other side of the Mediterranean. “The strategic interest of Europe is seeing a more economically stable Egypt with a population that is less precarious,” he said.

Sandra Gómez López, a Spanish socialist MEP who recently visited Cairo to assess human rights in Egypt, said the data presented by the Guardian raised important questions. “The commission must explain transparently why it considers these commitments to have been fulfilled and demonstrate that European funding is genuinely contributing to improving social protection for the Egyptian population,” she said.

She stressed that the EU should not withdraw support, “but neither can we allow the country’s strategic importance to lead us to lower our standards when it comes to human rights”.

For critics, the questions about the EU’s willingness to hold Egypt accountable on very precise socioeconomic targets do not bode well for the broader but less defined goals for democracy and human rights.

Strik said: “The fact that Egypt and the commission even seem to ignore the crystal-clear socioeconomic conditions doesn’t increase my expectation that they adhere to the vague precondition on democracy and the rule of law.”

Claudio Francavilla, an associate EU director at Human Rights Watch, said: “Without benchmarks, the EU has turned a human rights conditionality into a blank cheque for Egypt’s authoritarianism.”

In a statement to the Guardian, the European Commission said the policy target on Takaful and Karama referred to new beneficiary families “and not to its net increase”. The spokesperson said there was “no contradiction” between this statement and that in a confidential document that said Egypt was required to “further expand cash transfer programmes”.

Asked about the release of the remaining €1.5bn in promised loans, the commission said it “will be made once all conditions have been met”, referring to 24 economic policy conditions, keeping an IMF programme on track and “taking concrete and credible steps towards respecting effective democratic mechanisms, including a multiparty parliamentary system, and the rule of law, and guaranteeing respect for human rights”.

It added: “Compliance with the conditionality is examined thoroughly” by EU officials and diplomats based on “information provided by the Egyptian authorities and other relevant sources”.

Egypt’s mission to the EU in Brussels did not respond to a request for comment.

#15. Myanmar committed genocide against Rohingya minority, top UN court rules

Rohingya genocide: What was said in court and what happens now?

Myanmar has been found guilty of committing genocide against its Rohingya minority by the International Court of Justice (ICJ).

The verdict marks the first time a state has ever been found to have committed genocide by an international court.

Yuji Iwasawa, president of the UN's top court, said Myanmar had the "intention to destroy" a substantial part of the Rohingya population and engaged in "extensive destruction of evidence" to hide its crimes.

A military crackdown in 2017 killed thousands of Rohingya and forced more than 700,000 to flee Buddhist-majority Myanmar, formerly known as Burma. Myanmar denied the claims against it, saying its forces had responded to attacks by armed groups.

Bowen: Myanmar genocide verdict is historic and could have far-reaching implications

Rohingyas at the Cox's Bazar camp in Bangladesh, where around a million refugees now reside, watched the verdict live

The court found on Thursday that "genocidal intent is the only reasonable inference that can be drawn from the evidence" it had seen.

The verdict was reached by a majority of nine votes to six among a panel of judges from around the world. The ICJ also found in favour by majority that Myanmar had violated several other parts of the 1948 Genocide Convention.

The case brought by The Gambia centred around so-called "clearance operations" initiated in 2017 in Rakhine State, where many of the Rohingya Muslim minority resided.

After fighters from the Arakan Rohingya Salvation Army (Arsa) launched deadly attacks on more than 30 police posts there, Myanmar troops, backed by local Buddhist mobs, launched a wave of violence, prompting a mass exodus of Rohingya.

Refugees recounted entire villages being burned down and the mass killing of civilians, while numerous survivors and eyewitnesses provided testimony that the Myanmar military had also raped and abused Rohingya women and girls.

The ICJ determined that around 10,000 Rohingya were "violently killed" during the clearance operation, while more than 700,000 are thought to have fled to Bangladesh by foot or by sea. The court found that approximately 815,000 had been targeted in some way.

Watch: Genocide judgement will 'mean a great deal' for Rohingya community

Iwasawa said Myanmar's military engaged in "indiscriminate" killings, the execution of men, "widespread" sexual violence, "extreme violence" against children and the deliberate burning of Rohingya homes.

He said the military had also attacked Rohingya as they were fleeing to Bangladesh.

Myanmar, he added, had engaged in "extensive destruction of evidence" by burning or disposing of bodies, burned and bulldozed sites that were cleared and failed to prosecute those who had perpetrated the acts of violence.

While the ICJ recognised the role Arsa played, it found there was "overlap" between an "anti-Rohingya hate speech campaign" - which portrayed the Rohingya as "sub-human" and "a violent threat" - and the clearance operations that made violence against the population more likely.

Myanmar consistently denied genocide against the Rohingya, saying its operations had targeted militant or insurgent threats in response to the Arsa attacks.

In January, government representative Ko Ko Hlaing told the ICJ that the country "was not obliged to remain idle and allow terrorists to have free reign". He said he had "no comment" following Thursday's verdict.

The ICJ also looked at the wider treatment of Rohingya leading up to the clearance operation and found that policies like limiting married couples to two children were discriminatory and aimed to erode their rights.

It said Myanmar had an obligation to pay proportionate reparations, but said an exact figure could be determined at a later date.

The court's decision was welcomed by Rohingya communities and human rights groups.

"This is like formal recognition of our suffering," says Mohammed Nowkim, a Rohingya who was driven out of Rakhine in 2017 and now lives in Canada. "The Rohingya have been waiting for justice for decades."

Human Rights Watch said the ruling was "long-awaited recognition of the suffering the Rohingya have endured".

Meanwhile, UN human rights chief Volker TÃ¼rk said the "suffering, misery and humiliation" inflicted on the Rohingya people was a "stain on the conscience of humanity". He added that the court's decision "must now translate into real action".

To implicate the Myanmar government in genocide, the court had to determine not just that terrible acts had been committed, but that there was an intent on the part of officials or arms of the state to partially or completely destroy the group.

Thursday's ruling is without precedent. Myanmar now carries the stain of being the first state to have been found to have violated the 1948 Genocide Convention.

The ICJ has only twice before ruled on claims of state-backed genocide in the years since the Genocide Convention was signed in the aftermath of the Holocaust, in both cases finding there was insufficient evidence to attribute the actions to the state.

Dozens of individuals have been found guilty of genocide, but finding their actions were at the direct behest of the state requires a higher evidential bar to prove genocide, the gravest of crimes under international law.

Rohingya refugees were seen reacting as the ICJ verdict was broadcast on a big screen in Cox's Bazar, Bangladesh

Philippe Sands KC, a top human rights lawyer who helped bring the case, described it as an "historic judgment".

He told BBC Radio 4's World at One programme that it was "right up there with the great moments of international justice: Nuremberg, Pinochet, and others", referring to the trials of Nazi leaders following World War Two and that of the Chilean dictator, respectively.

However, the ICJ is not a criminal court and its powers of enforcement are limited. It cannot send military officials to prison, while it relies on the UN Security Council and political pressure to impose its rulings.

The ruling will, though, have implications for other genocide cases pending with the ICJ, including one brought by South Africa against Israel over its war in Gaza.

Sands said that pending case had been the "elephant in the room" during the Myanmar hearings.

He added that, while ruling against Myanmar did not mean the court would find similarly with Gaza, if it had not found Myanmar responsible, the case against Israel "would, in effect, be over". Israel says the case against it is "wholly unfounded".

ICJ president Yuji Iwasawa said Myanmar had the "intention to destroy" a substantial part of the Rohingya population

Antonia Mulvey, executive director of Legal Action Worldwide, told the BBC that the court had not accepted Myanmar's counter-terrorism defence and had found for the first time that rape and sexual violence was an act of genocide, positions it would bear in mind for future cases.

A spokesperson for the US State Department said "the proper interpretation" of the Genocide Convention is of "paramount importance", and so is "preventing politicisation" of the International Criminal Court. "We are studying the decision to fully assess its implications," they said.

They said they share the "grave concerns over the continued abuses of human rights against Rohingya Muslims and members of other minority groups", and added the US is the "leading donor for the Rohingya refugee humanitarian response".

Following Thursday's ruling, UK Foreign Secretary Ed Miliband said the government was ordering Myanmar's defence attachÃ© be expelled and that he was summoning its charge d'affaires.

He pledged to set out "further action in the coming days to ensure those responsible are held to account" after a cross-party group of MPs called for the government to resume sanctions against Myanmar's military.

#11. 'I tell my son it's just fireworks': People inside besieged Yemeni city speak to BBC

Houthi attacks on Taiz have killed more than 140 civilians since August, local government officials say

In the Yemeni city of Taiz, ringed by jagged mountains and Houthi rebel fighters, Ashwaq has only one way to try to soothe her frightened child - lie.

"When my 11-year-old son hears a loud sound, I tell him, 'Don't be afraid, it's just fireworks'," she says. "I don't want him to panic, but deep down I know he is old enough to understand what is happening around him. He has lived through war for most of his life."

Taiz has long been a prize - and a front line - in Yemen's civil war.

Houthi forces tightened their grip last month, fully encircling the government-controlled city, and the population - estimated to be between 600,000 and 1 million.

Since then, Yemen's third-largest city has been cut off from the world, and its residents have been largely unheard. We reached seven people inside the city who mapped out the anatomy of the siege.

They gave detailed accounts of the Houthis' death grip on Taiz. Only a minimal amount of food can get in. Prices for staples like rice, sugar and cooking oil have doubled or even trebled. Essential supplies, like medicine, water and fuel, are dwindling.

Burning piles of rubbish are producing toxic smoke that hangs over the city

On streets that used to hum with traffic and background chatter, a silence has fallen. Schools are closed and restaurants are shuttered. Suffering is being heaped on suffering. Piles of rubbish are trapped inside the city, alongside the people. When residents try to get rid of it by burning it, there is a toxic smoke. Even the air is now a Houthi weapon.

From positions on the hills, rebel fighters - and snipers - can wage war on the city below.

Estimates for the death toll vary widely. At the Office for Siege Affairs, a local government department, they say 148 civilians have been killed by Houthi attacks since August.

Some of those we contacted in Taiz do not want to be identified because they fear for their safety. Many in the city are reluctant to even speak on the phone because the Houthis control key mobile carriers.

"Sometimes it feels like the city has become a huge prison," says Ashwaq, a teacher and mother of three, who only wanted her first name to be used. She yearns for the old Taiz.

"It used to be a lively city," she says. "People loved life, worked hard, visited one another, and had hopes for the future, despite all the difficulties. Now, we hardly go out anymore. The [Houthi] shelling can happen any time, and sniper fire near the front lines makes even a short trip dangerous."

She keeps her son and two elder daughters indoors.

"I worry about them every single day, every hour, and honestly every minute," she says.

Taiz has been a major battleground in Yemen's civil war for more than a decade

War is not new to Taiz. The strategic city is the gateway to Aden - the temporary seat of the internationally recognised government - and to southern Yemen.

Houthi rebels - backed by Iran - reached the outskirts of Taiz in 2015, cutting off roads, and laying siege from three sides. A year earlier, they had seized Yemen's capital, Sanaa.

More than a decade on, the government, which is weak and fractured, has been unable to reclaim the capital and much of north-western Yemen. That's despite the backing of Saudi Arabia, and its air strikes.

In recent months, the conflict has flared up and expanded after a four-year lull. The Houthis made a lightning advance, taking territory from the government, and striking Saudi Arabia with missiles and drones.

A long-running civil war has become a threat to regional stability.

An emergency room doctor, whom we are calling Yaser, bears witness to that.

Among the war wounded recently brought to his hospital were four children, shot by Houthi snipers. They usually aim for the head and chest, he tells us. Two of the four children died.

The hardest thing he faces, as a father himself, is treating wounded children. "I have to rise to the responsibility, compose myself and support the families," he says. "It's a difficult situation that I cannot even put into words, when I see a father looking at his badly wounded child right in front of him."

Most of the injured are victims of shelling. Reaching the hospital can be an ordeal. Some families have to walk for hours from outlying areas.

They arrive exhausted, like the doctors who will treat them, and who are struggling with "everything", says Yaser. Even sterilising fluid for surgical instruments is in short supply.

He tells us the hospital is running on empty, and can carry on for two more weeks, "maybe".

Taiz's hospitals have lost 70% of their capacity and 120 health centres have closed, its governor says

If the emergency rooms are busy, so are the graveyards.

"Funerals heading to the cemeteries have become the defining scene in Taiz, around the clock," says Taiz's Governor, Nabil Shamsan.

It's not just bombs and bullets that kill, he tells us.

"The people of Taiz are losing their lives due to supply shortages, lack of food, and loss of income," he says. "Every single day, we bury dozens of Taiz residents."

He remains inside the city, and inside the siege. "I live among my people," he says.

On a video call, he looks weary but delivers a lengthy chronicle of a city being starved of oxygen - literally.

He tells us that hospitals have lost 70% of their capacity, and 120 health centres have closed. Diseases are spreading, neonatal deaths have increased, and malnutrition is surging. Hundreds of kidney patients are not getting dialysis because they can't get to hospitals or because fuel is short when they do.

The Houthi leader, Abdul Malik al-Houthi, has denied Taiz is besieged. Late last month, he claimed that routes remained open for food, medicine and humanitarian aid.

That is denied by the government, residents of Taiz, aid agencies and the United Nations.

What's at stake in the battle for the city is more than territory, says Shamsan.

He says the Houthis - who mostly follow a branch of Shia Islam - want to change the character of this predominantly Sunni city, long known as a seat of learning and a bastion of resistance.

"Everyone knows that Taiz is a hub of culture, a city characterised by freedom, political pluralism and all forms of civic life," he says. In sharp contrast, he says, the Houthis aim to impose their strict religious doctrine and drag the city "back to the dark ages".

Whatever the pressure on the city and its people, the governor insists the Houthis will never win.

"Until the population perishes," he replies.

Essential supplies, like medicine, water and fuel, are dwindling

They are defending their city, alongside relatives, friends, neighbours, and five brigades of pro-government troops, who can't do the job on their own.

They don't have heavy weapons or enough numbers for the expanding front line.

Training camps have sprung up for volunteers, but the lessons can be as brief as a day or two before they are sent to join the fight.

Akram (not his real name) is already a veteran. He's 31, with a child on the way.

When Taiz was fully encircled before, in 2015, he broke away from his studies and took up arms. In that round of fighting, the Houthis gained control of his district on the outskirts, and he was uprooted. He hasn't seen his old home since then.

Last month, when Taiz was completely cut off once again, he bought a rifle, paying about $1,200 (Â£910) for a Chinese version of a Kalashnikov. It used to cost less than half that sum.

He tells us this fight is different to 2015.

"This time the Houthis have new technology and new missiles," he says. "They can wage a war in the air with drones. We fear the death of our families and our civilians, and ourselves."

He has already lost four friends in the past month, who left behind wives and children.

But Akram, and many others, see defending Taiz as their duty. He says that "all the sons of Taiz" will fight the Houthis "and their extremism".

The armed group is currently designated as a foreign terrorist organisation by the US (which has gone back and forth on the issue) but not by the UK or the European Union.

"They say God gave them the right to rule everyone in Yemen. We don't accept that," says Akram. "We live in freedom. We cannot surrender; we cannot leave. We cannot breathe without our city."

Almost 350,000 people have been displaced by the fighting in recent months

But it can be suffocating inside Taiz, where nightfall brings no rest.

"Every night we have bombing and shelling," says Ahmad Al-Basha, a photographer. "I think the Houthis prefer to fight at night. Maybe they don't want us and our children to sleep inside our homes."

The people of Taiz feel let down, he tells us, by their government and by the international community. "We feel frustrated and sad," he says, "because we don't see any move to help us."

Many believe the Houthis will keep a chokehold on Taiz from the outside, and exert significant control over the city without trying to enter it.

Ahmad believes it won't come to that but fears the worst if it does.

"Maybe they will clean Taiz of any resistance, kill people and settle scores with anyone who is against them," he says.

His greatest concern is for his children, who cannot escape the sounds of war.

Like Ashwaq, the teacher, he tries to explain it away.

"I tell them it's a party," he says, "and if it's really loud, I say it's a big party."

As Taiz endures bombing, shelling, and a sense of abandonment laced with growing hunger, Ashwaq has a parent's plea for the international community.

"Please don't forget Taiz," she says. "We are ordinary families. We want our children to study, play and dream like other children around the world. We are not just numbers in news reports."

Additional reporting by Wietske Burema and Albaraa Mansoor
